\title{Algebraic Topology}
\maketitle

\phantomsection
\label{section-phantom}

\tableofcontents

\section{Paths and homotopies}
\label{section-paths-homotopies}

We denote by $I$ the unit interval $[0,1]$
and by
$X$ an arbitrary topological space.

\begin{definition}
A \emph{path} is a function $f:I\to X$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
A \emph{homotopy of paths} in $X$ is a family
$f_t:I\to X$, with $0\leq t\leq1$, such that
the
associated function
$$
\begin{aligned}
F:I\times I&\to X\\
(s,t)&\mapsto f_t(s)
\end{aligned}
$$
is continuous.

We say that the homotopy $F:I\times I\to X$
is
\emph{relative to the endpoints} if
$$
F(0,t)=x_0\qquad\forall t\in I
\qquad
F(1,t)=x_1\qquad\forall t\in I
$$
for $x_0,x_1\in X$.

Finally, two paths $f_0$ and $f_1$ in $X$
such that
$f_0(0)=f_1(0)$ and $f_0(1)=f_1(1)$ are
\emph{homotopic rel} $\mathbf{0,1}$ if there
is a
homotopy relative to the endpoints between
them. This is denoted
$f_0\simeq f_1\operatorname{rel}\{0,1\}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
The relation
$f_1\sim{}f_2\iff f_1\simeq
f_2\operatorname{rel}\{0,1\}$ is an
equivalence relation on the set of paths in
$X$, which we
will denote by $\Omega(X):=\{f:I\to X\}$.
\end{theorem}
\section{The fundamental group}
\label{section-fundamental-group}

On our way to defining the fundamental group,
we first
define an operation between paths.

We denote by $[f]$ the homotopy class rel
$0,1$ of
$f\in\Omega(X)$.

\begin{definition}
Given $f,g:I\to X$ such that $f(1)=g(0)$, we
can define
the \emph{concatenation of paths}
$$
\begin{aligned}
f&\cdot g:I\to X\\
f\cdot g(s)&=\begin{cases}
f(2s)\qquad s\in[0,1/2]\\
g(2s-1)\qquad s\in[1/2,1].
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
If $f\simeq f_1\operatorname{rel}\{0,1\}$ and
$g\simeq g_1\operatorname{rel}\{0,1\}$, then
$f\cdot g\simeq f_1\cdot
g_1\operatorname{rel}\{0,1\}$.

In other words, if $[f]=[f_1]$ and
$[g]=[g_1]$, then
$[f\cdot g]=[f_1\cdot g_1]$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
We say that $f:I\to X$ is a \emph{loop based
at}
$x_0\in X$ if $f(0)=f(1)=x_0$. We denote by
$$
\Omega(X,x_0)=\{f:I\to X:f(0)=f(1)=x_0\}
$$
the set of such loops.
\end{definition}

The equivalence relation
$\operatorname{rel}\{0,1\}$ restricts
correctly to
$\Omega(X,x_0)\subseteq\Omega(X)$, so we can
finally define:

\begin{definition}
The \emph{fundamental group} of $X$ based at
$x_0$ is the
set
$$
\pi_1(X,x_0)
=\Omega(X,x_0)/
\operatorname{rel}\{0,1\}.
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
The fundamental group is a group with the
concatenation
operation $[f][g]=[f\cdot g]$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We have to check that:
\begin{itemize}
\item the product is well defined;
\item there is an identity, the constant path
$c_{x_0}:I\to X$ given by $c_{x_0}(t)=x_0$
for every
$t\in I$;
\item there are inverses;
\item there is associativity.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}
\section{Homotopies of maps}
\label{section-basic-notions}

A homotopy is a continuously varying
map. This simple idea is the equivalence
relation behind the constructions that
follow.

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-homotopic-maps}
\label{def:func-homot}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be spaces. A
\emph{homotopy} from $f:X\to Y$ to
$g:X\to Y$ is a continuous map
$$
H:X\times I\longrightarrow Y
$$
such that
$$
H(x,0)=f(x),
\qquad
H(x,1)=g(x).
$$
We write $f\simeq g$. A homotopy is
\emph{relative to} $A\subset X$ if
$$
H(a,t)=f(a)=g(a)
$$
for every $a\in A$ and $t\in I$.
\end{definition}

If we have $A\subseteq X$ and $B\subseteq Y$,
and $f$ is
such that $f(A)\subseteq B$, we write
$$
f:(X,A)\to(Y,B).
$$
A homotopy between functions of this kind is
a function
$H:X\times I\to Y$ such that all the
functions obtained by
changing the time parameter still send $A$ to
$B$. That is,
we have functions
$$
H(\_,t):(X,A)\to(Y,B)\qquad\forall t\in I
$$
such that
$$
H(x,0)=f(x)\qquad\forall x\in X,
\qquad
H(x,1)=g(x)\qquad\forall x\in X.
$$

In particular, if $A=\{x_0\}$ and
$B=\{y_0\}$, just as
before, we write
$$
f:(X,x_0)\to(Y,y_0).
$$
In this case, we say that a
\emph{base-point-preserving
homotopy} is a function $H:X\times I\to Y$
such that
$$
H(x_0,t)=y_0\qquad\forall t\in I,
$$
$$
H(x,0)=f(x)\qquad\forall x\in X,
\qquad
H(x,1)=g(x)\qquad\forall x\in X.
$$
We will simply say that $f$ and $g$ are
homotopic,
specifying when necessary that the homotopy
preserves the
base point.

\begin{proposition}
If $f,g:(X,x_0)\to(Y,y_0)$ are homotopic,
preserving the
base point, then they induce the same
homomorphism on
fundamental groups, that is,
$$
f_*=g_*:\pi_1(X,x_0)\to\pi_1(Y,y_0).
$$
\end{proposition}

Homotopy classes form the morphisms in the
homotopy category. A map $f:X\to Y$ is a
\emph{homotopy equivalence} if there is a
map $g:Y\to X$ such that
$$
g\circ f\simeq\operatorname{id}_X,
\qquad
f\circ g\simeq\operatorname{id}_Y.
$$
The map $g$ is a \emph{homotopy inverse}.
A space is \emph{contractible} if it is
homotopy equivalent to a point. A map is
\emph{null homotopic} if it is homotopic
to a constant map.

\begin{corollary}
\label{lemma-based-homotopy-equivalence}
If $f:(X,x_0)\to(Y,y_0)$ has a based
homotopy inverse, then
$$
f_*:\pi_1(X,x_0)\longrightarrow
\pi_1(Y,y_0)
$$
is an isomorphism.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Suppose that $g:Y\to X$ is as in the
preceding definition.
It is enough to show that $f_*$ and $g_*$ are
inverses of one
another. And they are, because
$\operatorname{id}_{\pi_1(X,x_0)}
=(g f)_*=g_*f_*$ and similarly for $Y$.
\end{proof}

For an unbased homotopy equivalence between
path-connected spaces, the same conclusion
holds after applying the appropriate
change-of-base-point isomorphism.

\begin{exercise}[Hatcher 0.11]
\label{exercise-hatcher-homotopy-equivalence}
\label{hatcher:0.11}
\emph{One can use different functions to go
and come back.} A map $f:X\to Y$ is a
homotopy equivalence if there are maps
$g,h:Y\to X$ such that
$fg\simeq\operatorname{id}_Y$ and
$hf\simeq\operatorname{id}_X$. More
generally,
$f$ is a homotopy equivalence if $fg$ and
$hf$ are homotopy equivalences.
\begin{proof}[Solution]
The first assertion is the special case of
the
second in which both composites are already
homotopic to identities. In the homotopy
category, invertibility of $fg$ gives $f$ a
right inverse, while invertibility of $hf$
gives $f$ a left inverse. A morphism with
both
a left and a right inverse is invertible,
since
the two inverses agree. Thus $f$ is a
homotopy
equivalence.
\end{proof}
\end{exercise}

We add one last property, for which we define
two notions
that are used throughout the text.

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-retraction}
\label{def:retracto}
A \emph{retraction} of a space $X$ onto a
subspace $A$ is a
function $f:X\to A$ such that
$f|_A=\operatorname{id}_A$. A
\emph{deformation retract} of $X$ onto $A$ is
a homotopy
$f_t:X\to X$ such that
$f_0=\operatorname{id}_X$ and
$f_1(X)=A$, and also
$f_t|_A=\operatorname{id}_A$ for every $t$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
If a space $X$ retracts onto a space $A$,
then the
homomorphism
$i_*:\pi_1(A,x_0)\to\pi_1(X,x_0)$ induced by
the inclusion $A\hookrightarrow X$ is
injective. If $A$ is a
deformation retract of $X$, then $i_*$ is an
isomorphism.
\end{proposition}
\section{Change of base point}
\label{section-change-base-point}

What happens when we change the base point?
Points in distinct path components can have
different fundamental groups. Within one
component, paths organize all changes of
base point.

\begin{definition}
The \emph{fundamental groupoid} $\Pi_1(X)$
has the points of $X$ as objects and
endpoint-preserving homotopy classes of
paths as morphisms. Composition is path
concatenation, after reparametrizing paths
to fit the two halves of the interval.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
\label{theorem-change-basepoint}
\label{teo:cambioptobase}
Let $\gamma$ be a path from $x_0$ to $x_1$.
Then
$$
\begin{aligned}
\beta_\gamma:
\pi_1(X,x_0)&\longrightarrow
\pi_1(X,x_1),\\
[f]&\longmapsto
[\overline\gamma\cdot f\cdot\gamma]
\end{aligned}
$$
is an isomorphism. Its inverse is
$\beta_{\overline\gamma}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Endpoint-preserving homotopies can be pasted
on either side with $\gamma$ and
$\overline\gamma$, so the formula is well
defined. Associativity up to path homotopy
and the contractions of
$\gamma\cdot\overline\gamma$ and
$\overline\gamma\cdot\gamma$ show that the
two displayed change-of-base-point maps are
inverse homomorphisms.
\end{proof}

These maps are functorial in the fundamental
groupoid. The same construction acts on the
higher and relative homotopy groups of a
pointed pair.

\begin{exercise}
For a path-connected space $X$, show that
$\pi_1(X)$ is
abelian if and only if the
change-of-base-point
homomorphisms $\beta_h$ depend only on the
endpoint of $h$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}[Solution]
Let $h$ and $g$ have the same initial and
terminal points. The two change-of-base-point
maps differ by conjugation with the loop
formed
by $h$ and the reverse of $g$. If the
fundamental group is abelian, that
conjugation
is trivial, so $\beta_h=\beta_g$.

Conversely, compare an arbitrary loop
$\delta$
at $x_0$ with the constant path at $x_0$.
Endpoint independence says conjugation by
$[\delta]$ is the identity on
$\pi_1(X,x_0)$. Thus every $[\delta]$ is
central, so the group is abelian.
\end{proof}
\section{The fundamental group of the circle}
\label{section-fundamental-group-circle}

Let us think of
$S^1=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=1\}$.

\begin{theorem}
$\pi_1(S^1,x_0)\cong\mathbb{Z}$ for every
$x_0\in S^1$.
\end{theorem}

Two familiar consequences illustrate the
force
of the circle computation.
\section{Brouwer's fixed point theorem in
dimension 2}
\label{section-brouwer-fixed-point}

\begin{theorem}
Every function $f:D^2\to D^2$ has a fixed
point.
\end{theorem}
\section{The Borsuk--Ulam theorem in
dimension 2}
\label{section-borsuk-ulam}

\begin{theorem}
For every continuous function
$f:S^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$ there exists
$x\in S^2$ such that $f(x)=f(-x)$.
\end{theorem}
\section{The fundamental group of a product}
\label{section-fundamental-group-product}

\begin{theorem}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be topological spaces and let
$x_0\in X$,
$y_0\in Y$. Then
$$
\pi_1(X\times Y,(x_0,y_0))
\cong
\pi_1(X,x_0)\times\pi_1(Y,y_0).
$$
\end{theorem}
\section{Induced homomorphisms
(functoriality)}
\label{section-induced-fundamental-map}

First let us establish some notation. If
$f:X\to Y$,
$x_0\in X$, $y_0\in Y$, and $f(x_0)=y_0$, we
write
$f:(X,x_0)\to(Y,y_0)$.

\begin{theorem}[Functoriality]
The function $f:(X,x_0)\to(Y,y_0)$ induces a
homomorphism
$$
f_*:\pi_1(X,x_0)\to\pi_1(Y,y_0)
$$
such that
\begin{enumerate}
\item if $g:(Y,y_0)\to(Z,z_0)$, then
$(g\circ f)_*=g_*\circ f_*$;
\item $(\operatorname{id}_X)_*
=\operatorname{id}_{\pi_1(X,x_0)}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that $f_t:X\to X$ is a homotopy such
that $f_0$ and
$f_1$ are the identity. Show that for every
$x_0\in X$, the
loop $f_t(x_0)$ represents an element in the
center of
$\pi_1(X,x_0)$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}[Solution]
Let $[f]$ be the equivalence class of the
loop $f_t(x_0)$,
and let $[\gamma]$ be any element of
$\pi_1(X,x_0)$. We want
to prove that $f\cdot\gamma\simeq\gamma\cdot
f$.

One can think of loops like $f_t(x_0)$ but
starting at any
point of the space $X$. In particular we have
the family of
loops $f_t(\gamma(s))$.  Its domain is
represented by the following square.
\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.34\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/homotopy-square
}
\end{center}
Of course
$f_0(\gamma(s))=\gamma(s)=f_1(\gamma(s))$,
and
$f_t(\gamma(0))=f_t(x_0)=f_t(\gamma(1))$.
Thus the left and
top edges represent $f\cdot\gamma$, and the
bottom and right
edges represent $\gamma\cdot f$. Any path
inside the square
joining the lower-left corner to the
upper-right corner
represents a path homotopic to either of the
above.
\end{proof}
\section{Higher-dimensional spheres}
\label{section-higher-dimensional-spheres}

\begin{proposition}
$\pi_1(S^n)=0$ if $n\geq2$.
\end{proposition}

This is proved using the following lemma.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-loop-factorization-open-cover}
\label{lema:homotopia}
Let $X=\bigcup_{\alpha\in I} A_\alpha$ be
such that
\begin{itemize}
\item the $A_\alpha$ are open sets, i.e. they
are an open
cover;
\item there exists $x_0\in\bigcap A_\alpha$;
\item $A_\alpha$ is path connected;
\item $A_\alpha\cap A_\beta$ is also path
connected for all
$\alpha,\beta$.
\end{itemize}
Then every loop $\gamma$ in $X$ is homotopic
rel $0,1$ to a
product, i.e. concatenation, of loops of the
form
$\gamma\cong\gamma_1\cdots\gamma_n$ such that
each
$\gamma_i$ is completely contained in some
$A_\alpha$.
\end{lemma}
\section{Invariance of Euclidean dimension}
\label{section-invariance-dimension}

\begin{theorem}
$\mathbb{R}^2$ is not homeomorphic to
$\mathbb{R}^n$ if $n\neq2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
If $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$, then
$\mathbb{R}^n-\{x\}$ is homeomorphic to
$S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}$.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
$\mathbb{R}^n\cong\mathbb{R}^m\iff n=m$.
\end{theorem}
\section{Free products}
\label{section-free-products}

In group theory, we normally define the
direct product by
thinking of something like
% D01
$$
\xymatrix{
  & G\times H \\
G \ar@{^{(}->}[ur]^{g\mapsto(g,1)}
  && H \ar@{_{(}->}[ul]_{h\mapsto(1,h)}
}
$$
where the product is commutative, that is,
$$
(g,1)(1,h)=(1,h)(g,1).
$$
Now we define a product called the \emph{free
product}, in
which the elements will not commute.

\begin{definition}
Let $\{G_\alpha\}_{\alpha\in I}$ be a
collection of groups.
As a set, the free product $\ast_{\alpha\in
I}G_\alpha$
consists of the words $g_1g_2\ldots g_m$, for
$m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}$, where each $g_i$
lies in some
$G_\alpha$. Also, $g_i\neq1$, and
$g_i,g_{i+1}$ always
belong to different $G_\alpha$, that is,
$g_i\in G_\alpha$
and $g_{i+1}\in G_\beta$ with
$\alpha\neq\beta$. In this
case, we say that $g_1\ldots g_m$ is a
\emph{reduced word}.

The binary operation on $\ast_{\alpha\in I}$
is given by
concatenation.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Each of the $G_\alpha$ is contained in
$\ast_{\alpha\in I}G_\alpha$, since its
elements appear as
one-letter words. In symbols:
$$
\begin{aligned}
G&\hookrightarrow\ast_{\alpha\in I}G_\alpha\\
g&\mapsto g.
\end{aligned}
$$
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Universal property of the
free product]
Given homomorphisms
$\varphi_\alpha:G_\alpha\to H$, there
exists a unique homomorphism
$\varphi:\ast_{\alpha\in I}G_\alpha\to H$
extending the
$\varphi_\alpha$.  Equivalently, the
following
triangle commutes.
% D02
$$
\xymatrix{
G_\alpha \ar[rr]^{\varphi_\alpha}
  \ar[dr]
  && H \\
  & \displaystyle
    \ast_{\alpha\in I}G_\alpha
    \ar[ur]_{\varphi}
}
$$
\end{theorem}

\begin{example}
If $F_n$ and $F_m$ are free groups generated
by $n$ and $m$
elements, then $F_n*F_m\cong F_{n+m}$.
\end{example}
\section{The Van Kampen theorem}
\label{section-van-kampen}

Imagine that we want to compute the
fundamental group of a
space but do not know how. This happens very
often. But it
turns out that we can view this space as the
union of many
subspaces whose fundamental groups we know.
This is the
setting of the Van Kampen theorem.

Take a topological space $X$, a base point
$x_0\in X$, and
suppose that $X=\bigcup_{\alpha\in
I}A_\alpha$ for certain
spaces $A_\alpha$ such that $x_0\in A_\alpha$
for every
$\alpha\in I$. Also suppose that the
$A_\alpha$ are path
connected.

Since the $A_\alpha$ are subspaces, we can
consider the
inclusion $A_\alpha\hookrightarrow X$ and the
induced
homomorphisms on fundamental groups, denoted
$j_\alpha:\pi_1(A_\alpha,x_0)
\longrightarrow\pi_1(X,x_0)$.
Then, by the
universal property of the free product, we
have a
homomorphism from the free product of these
groups to the
fundamental group of $X$:
$$
\ast_{\alpha\in I}\pi_1(A_\alpha,x_0)
\to
\pi_1(X,x_0).
$$
The theorem will tell us that this function
is surjective.
Intuitively, it says that every loop in $X$
is homotopic to
another loop which is the concatenation of
certain loops,
each completely contained in one of the
$A_\alpha$.

Now think of a loop that lies in the
intersection of two of
the $A_\alpha$. We can think of the loop as
lying in, say,
$A_\alpha$, and its inverse as lying in
$A_\beta$. For distinct indices, the two
classes form a word in
$\ast_{\alpha\in I}\pi_1(A_\alpha,x_0)$.
This word can be nonidentity, while its
image in $\pi_1(X,x_0)$ is the identity.
Thus the map we have built need not be
injective.

Now suppose that for $\alpha,\beta\in I$ we
have the
inclusion $A_\alpha\cap
A_\beta\hookrightarrow A_\alpha$,
which induces a map
$$
i_{\alpha\beta}:
\pi_1(A_\alpha\cap A_\beta,x_0)
\longrightarrow
\pi_1(A_\alpha,x_0).
$$
The order of the subscripts is important,
since the
codomain is given by the first symbol in the
subscript.

Putting the preceding paragraphs together,
the word
$$
i_{\alpha\beta}[f]i_{\beta\alpha}[\bar f]
\in \ast_{\alpha\in I}
\pi_1(A_\alpha,x_0)
$$
can be pushed to $\pi_1(X,x_0)$. The Van
Kampen theorem will tell us that the kernel
of the homomorphism is generated precisely
by elements of this kind.

Now we can state it.

\begin{theorem}[Van Kampen]
If $X=\bigcup_{\alpha\in I}A_\alpha$ is an
open cover, with each $A_\alpha$ path
connected, $x_0\in A_\alpha$, and also
$A_\alpha\cap A_\beta$ path connected for all
$\alpha,\beta\in I$, then the homomorphism
$$
\Phi:\ast_{\alpha\in I}\pi_1(A_\alpha,x_0)
\to\pi_1(X,x_0)
$$
is surjective.

If moreover $A_\alpha\cap A_\beta\cap
A_\gamma$ is path
connected for all $\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in I$,
then
$$
\ker\Phi=
\left\langle\left\langle
 i_{\alpha\beta}(\omega)
 i_{\beta\alpha}(\omega)^{-1}
 :\omega\in
 \pi_1(A_\alpha\cap A_\beta,x_0)
\right\rangle\right\rangle.
$$
In particular,
$$
\pi_1(X,x_0)\cong
\ast_{\alpha\in
I}\pi_1(A_\alpha,x_0)/\ker\Phi.
$$
\end{theorem}
\section{The fundamental group of a wedge of
spaces}
\label{section-fundamental-group-wedge}

Consider a collection of topological spaces
$X_\alpha$ with
$\alpha\in I$ and choose a point in each
space, say
$x_\alpha\in X_\alpha$. We define the
\emph{wedge} of these
spaces by
$$
\bigvee_{\alpha\in I}X_\alpha
:=
\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in I}X_\alpha
\Big/\{x_\alpha\sim{}x_\beta,
\forall\alpha,\beta\in
I\}.
$$
Assume also that each $x_\alpha$ is a
deformation retract
of some neighbourhood $U_\alpha\subseteq
X_\alpha$.

Then we have:

\begin{theorem}
Under these hypotheses, the homomorphism
appearing in the
Van Kampen theorem,
$$
\Phi:\ast_{\alpha\in I}\pi_1(X_\alpha,x_0)
\to
\pi_1\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in
I}X_\alpha\right),
$$
is an isomorphism.
\end{theorem}
\section{Group presentations}
\label{section-group-presentations}

Consider a group $G$ and a generating set
$S\subseteq G$,
that is, such that $\langle S\rangle=G$. This
is equivalent
to saying that every element of $G$ can be
seen as a product
of elements of $S$.

Also consider the free group generated by
$S$, say $F_S$,
and use the universal property of free groups
to see that
there exists a unique surjective homomorphism
from $F_S$ to
$G$ extending the inclusion of $S$ in $G$.
% D03
$$
\xymatrix{
S \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
  \ar@{_{(}->}[d]
  & G \\
F_S \ar@{-->>}[ur]_{\varphi}
}
$$

We have proved:

\begin{theorem}
Given a group $G$, there exists a free group
$F_S$ which
surjects onto $G$. In particular, by the
first isomorphism
theorem, every group is a quotient of a free
group.
\end{theorem}

If in addition we have a set
$R\subseteq\ker\varphi$ which
normally generates the kernel, that is,
$\langle\langle R\rangle\rangle=\ker\varphi$,
then
$$
G\cong F_S/\ker\varphi
\cong F_S/\langle\langle R\rangle\rangle,
$$
and we say that
$$
G=\langle S,R\rangle
$$
is a \emph{presentation} of $G$.

\begin{proposition}
If $G=\langle S,R\rangle$ is a group
presentation, $K$ is
another group, and $f:S\to K$ is a function
such that the word obtained by applying $f$
to every letter of each relator in $R$ is
the identity in $K$, then there exists a
unique homomorphism $\bar f:G\to K$
such that the following diagram commutes.
% D04
$$
\xymatrix{
S \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
  \ar[d]_{f}
  & G \ar@{-->}[dl]^{\bar f} \\
K
}
$$
\end{proposition}

That is, to define a homomorphism out of a
group given by a
presentation, we only have to make sure that
the relations
are respected.
\section{Realizing groups as fundamental
groups}
\label{section-realizing-fundamental-groups}

\begin{lemma}
Let $X$ be a topological space, let $x_0\in
X$ be a base
point, and let $f:S^1\to X$ be such that
$f(1)=x_0$. For the
adjunction space $Y=X\cup_f D^2$,
$$
\pi_1(Y,x_0)\cong
\pi_1(X,x_0)\big/\langle\langle
[f]\rangle\rangle,
$$
where we view $f$ as a loop in the
fundamental group.
\end{lemma}

This is called ``killing'' a loop.

\begin{lemma}
Let $X$ be a topological space, let $x_0\in
X$ be a base
point, and let $f_\alpha:S^1\to X$, with
$\alpha\in I$, be
such that $f_\alpha(1)=x_0$ for every
$\alpha$. For the adjunction space
$Y=X\cup_{\{f_\alpha\}}\{D^2\}$,
$$
\pi_1(Y,x_0)\cong
\pi_1(X,x_0)
\big/
\langle\langle [f_\alpha]:\alpha\in
I\rangle\rangle,
$$
where we view each $f_\alpha$ as a loop in
the fundamental group.
\end{lemma}

And now:

\begin{theorem}
For every group $G$ there exists a
topological space $X$
such that $\pi_1(X,x_0)\cong G$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First take a presentation
$G=\langle S,R\rangle=F_S/\langle\langle
R\rangle\rangle$,
and then take the wedge of as many circles as
there are
elements in $S$, say
$X=\bigvee_{\alpha\in S}S^1_\alpha$.
We know that
$\pi_1(X,x_0)\cong *_{\alpha\in
S}\mathbb{Z}\cong F_S$.

Now we have to kill as many loops as there
are relations. A
relation is a word in the generators, say
$r=s_1^{\varepsilon_1}
s_2^{\varepsilon_2}\ldots
s_m^{\varepsilon_m}$ with $s_i\in S$ and
$\varepsilon_i=\pm1$. This word essentially
determines a
path in the wedge of circles, so we can glue
in one disk for
each relation, and by the preceding lemma we
are done.
\end{proof}
\section{Attaching higher-dimensional cells}
\label{section-attaching-higher-cells}

To formalize the title of this section, we
need:
\begin{itemize}
\item a connected topological space $X$;
\item a cell $e^n$ of dimension $n>2$, that
is, a space
homeomorphic to the closed $n$-disk;
\item an attaching map $f:S^{n-1}\to X$;
\item the adjunction space $Y=X\cup_f e^n$;
\item a point $x_0\in\operatorname{im}(f)$.
\end{itemize}
Then we have:

\begin{theorem}
The inclusion $i:X\hookrightarrow Y$ induces
an isomorphism
$i_*:\pi_1(X,x_0)\to\pi_1(Y,x_0)$.
\end{theorem}

That is, when we glue a cell of dimension
greater than 2 to
a space, the fundamental group stays the
same.




\begin{exercise}
Use Lemma
\ref{lemma-loop-factorization-open-cover} to
show that if $Y$ is obtained from a
path-connected space $A$ by adjoining a cell
$e^n$ with $n\geq2$, then the inclusion
$A\hookrightarrow Y$
induces a surjective function on $\pi_1$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}[Solution]
Choose collar neighbourhoods in the attached
cell so that $U=Y\setminus\{c\}$ retracts
onto
$A$, while $V$ is a contractible open
neighbourhood of the cell center $c$. They
may
be chosen with path-connected intersection,
homotopy equivalent to $S^{n-1}$. Choose the
base point in $U\cap V$.

The loop-factorization lemma writes every
loop
in $Y$ as a product of loops in $U$ and $V$.
Loops in $V$ are nullhomotopic, and loops in
$U$ come, after the deformation retraction
and
a base-point change, from loops in $A$. Hence
$\pi_1(A)\to\pi_1(Y)$ is surjective.
\end{proof}

\section{Homological algebra for topology}
\label{at-section-homological-algebra}

\noindent
Let $R$ be an associative ring with unit.
The ring need not be commutative. Usually,
we have one of
$\mathbb{Z}$, $\mathbb{Q}$, or
$\mathbb{R}$. An $R$-module plays the role
of a vector space whose scalars lie in $R$.
We begin with the category of left
$R$-modules and $R$-linear maps.  We will
also use chain complexes of $R$-modules,
which have the form
$$
\xymatrix{
\cdots \ar[r]^-{\partial_{n+1}}
  & C_n \ar[r]^-{\partial_n}
  & C_{n-1} \ar[r]^-{\partial_{n-1}}
  & \cdots
}
$$
and satisfy
$$
\partial_{n-1}\partial_n=0.
$$
Thus
$$
\operatorname{im}(\partial_n)
\subset
\ker(\partial_{n-1}).
$$

\noindent
A morphism of chain complexes is written
$$
\xymatrix{
C_\bullet \ar[r]^-f & D_\bullet.
}
$$
It consists of maps $f_n:C_n\to D_n$
making every square in the following
diagram commute.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.7em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & C_{n+1}
    \ar[r]^-{\partial_{n+1}}
    \ar[d]_{f_{n+1}}
  & C_n
    \ar[r]^-{\partial_n}
    \ar[d]_{f_n}
  & C_{n-1}
    \ar[r]
    \ar[d]_{f_{n-1}}
  & \cdots \\
\cdots \ar[r]
  & D_{n+1}
    \ar[r]_-{\delta_{n+1}}
  & D_n
    \ar[r]_-{\delta_n}
  & D_{n-1}
    \ar[r]
  & \cdots
}
$$
The homology of $C_\bullet$ is
$$
H_n(C_\bullet)
=
\frac{\ker(\partial_n)}
{\operatorname{im}(\partial_{n+1})}.
$$

\begin{definition}
We say that $(D_\bullet,\delta)$ is a
\emph{sub-chain-complex} of
$(C_\bullet,\partial)$ if
$D_p\leq C_p$ for every $p\in\mathbb{Z}$ and
$\partial|_{D_p}=\delta_p$. The quotient
$(C_\bullet/D_\bullet,\partial)$ is the chain
complex whose
terms are $C_p/D_p$, with boundary maps
$\partial_p/\delta_p([c])=[\partial_p(c)]$.
% D17
$$
\xymatrix@C=2em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & C_{p+1}/D_{p+1}
    \ar[r]^{\overline\partial_{p+1}}
  & C_p/D_p
    \ar[r]^{\overline\partial_p}
  & C_{p-1}/D_{p-1} \ar[r]
  & \cdots
}
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\begin{itemize}
\item Elements of $C_p$ are called
\emph{$p$-dimensional
chains}.
\item Elements of $\ker\partial_p:=Z_p$ are
called
\emph{$p$-dimensional cycles}.
\item Elements of
$\operatorname{im}\partial_{p+1}:=B_p$ are
called \emph{$p$-dimensional boundaries}.
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

Two cycles $z$ and $z'$ are \emph{homologous}
if they determine the same homology class.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.34\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/homologous-cycles
}
\end{center}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-chain-exactness}
A chain complex $C_\bullet$ is called
{\it acyclic} if
$H_n(C_\bullet)=0$ for every $n$.
The sequence
$$
\xymatrix{
C_1 \ar[r]^-\varphi
  & C_2 \ar[r]^-\psi
  & C_3
}
$$
is {\it exact} if
$$
\operatorname{im}(\varphi)=\ker(\psi).
$$
The sequence
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & C_1 \ar[r]
  & C_2 \ar[r]
  & C_3 \ar[r]
  & 0
}
$$
is a {\it short exact sequence} if it is
exact at every term.  An indefinitely
extended exact sequence is called a
{\it long exact sequence}.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
A chain complex is exact if and only if all
of its homology groups vanish. Thus homology
measures the failure of exactness. A chain
complex may be finite: outside its displayed
range, its modules are zero.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-short-exact-quotient}
In a short exact sequence
$$
0\longrightarrow C_1
\xrightarrow{\varphi}C_2
\xrightarrow{\psi}C_3
\longrightarrow0,
$$
the map $\varphi$ is injective, the map
$\psi$ is surjective, and
$$
C_3\cong C_2/\ker(\psi).
$$
After identifying $C_1$ with its image,
one may write $C_3\cong C_2/C_1$.
One must remember that this notation
depends on the chosen embedding of $C_1$.
\end{proposition}


An exact sequence may extend indefinitely:
$$
\xymatrix@C=2.2em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & C_{p+1} \ar[r]^{f_{p+1}}
  & C_p \ar[r]^{f_p}
  & C_{p-1} \ar[r]^{f_{p-1}}
  & \cdots
}
$$
Exactness means exactness at every displayed
term.

\begin{proposition}
\label{proposition-exact-sequence-criteria}
\begin{enumerate}
\item $0\to A\xrightarrow{\alpha}B$ is exact
if and only if
$\ker\alpha=0$, that is, $\alpha$ is
injective.
\item $A\xrightarrow{\alpha}B\to0$ is exact
if and only if
$\operatorname{im}\alpha=B$, that is,
$\alpha$ is
surjective.
\item $0\to A\xrightarrow{\alpha}B\to0$ is
exact if and only
if $\alpha$ is an isomorphism, by the two
preceding items.
\item The sequence
$$
0\to A\xrightarrow{\alpha}B
\xrightarrow{\beta}C\to0
$$
is exact if and only if $\alpha$ is
injective, $\beta$ is
surjective, and
$\ker\beta=\operatorname{im}\alpha$, so that
$\beta$ induces an isomorphism $C\cong
B/\operatorname{im}\alpha$.

If we think of $\alpha$ as the inclusion of
$A$ as a subgroup
of $B$, we can write $C\cong B/A$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}[First isomorphism theorem]
If $M'\subseteq M$, then
% D19
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & M' \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
  & M \ar@{->>}[r]
  & M/M' \ar[r]
  & 0
}
$$
is an exact sequence.
\end{remark}


\noindent
For each fixed $n$, homology is a functor.
The forgetful inclusion into abelian groups
can be pictured as follows.
$$
\xymatrix{
  & \operatorname{Ab} \\
\operatorname{Ch}(R)
  \ar[r]^-{H_n}
  \ar[ur]
  & \operatorname{Mod}_R \ar[u] \\
C_\bullet \ar@{|->}[r]
  & H_n(C_\bullet)
}
$$
A chain map sends cycles to cycles and
boundaries to boundaries.  It therefore
induces a map on homology.  These maps obey
$$
H_n(fg)=H_n(f)H_n(g)
$$
and preserve identities.  Since the order
of composition does not reverse, homology
is covariant.

\begin{exercise}[Induced function]
If $(C_\bullet,\partial)\xrightarrow
f(C'_\bullet,\partial')$
is a chain map, then $f(Z_p)\subseteq
Z'_p$ and
$f(B_p)\subseteq B'_p$, so the induced
function
$$
\begin{aligned}
\bar f_p:H_p(C_\bullet)&\to H_p(C'_\bullet)\\
a+B_p&\mapsto f_p(a)+B'_p
\end{aligned}
$$
is well defined. If we also have a second
chain map
$(C'_\bullet,\partial')\xrightarrow
g(C''_\bullet,\partial'')$,
then $\overline{g\circ f}=\bar g\circ\bar f$.
Finally,
$\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{C_p}
=\operatorname{id}_{H_p(C)}$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-chain-homotopy}
Let
$$
f,g:(C_\bullet,\partial)
\longrightarrow
(C'_\bullet,\partial')
$$
be chain maps.  They are {\it chain
homotopic} if there are homomorphisms
$$
h_n:C_n\longrightarrow C'_{n+1}
$$
for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}$ such that
$$
f_n-g_n
=
\partial'_{n+1}h_n
+h_{n-1}\partial_n.
$$
The two summands can be seen as the two
diagonal routes in the following picture.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.65em@R=2.8em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & C_{n+1}
    \ar[r]^-{\partial_{n+1}}
    \ar[d]_{f_{n+1}-g_{n+1}}
  & C_n
    \ar[r]^-{\partial_n}
    \ar[d]^{f_n-g_n}
    \ar[dl]_{h_n}
  & C_{n-1}
    \ar[r]
    \ar[d]^{f_{n-1}-g_{n-1}}
    \ar[dl]^{h_{n-1}}
  & \cdots \\
\cdots \ar[r]
  & C'_{n+1}
    \ar[r]_-{\partial'_{n+1}}
  & C'_n
    \ar[r]_-{\partial'_n}
  & C'_{n-1}
    \ar[r]
  & \cdots
}
$$
This is an additive identity, not the
assertion that the diagonal diagram
commutes.  Chain-homotopic maps induce the
same map on homology.
\end{definition}

\section{The snake lemma}
\label{section-snake-lemma}

\begin{lemma}[Snake lemma]
Suppose the rows in the following commutative
diagram are exact.
% D21
$$
\xymatrix@C=2.5em@R=2.6em{
  & Z'_1 \ar[r]^{\phi'}
    \ar[d]_{\partial_1}
  & Z'_2 \ar[r]^{\psi'}
    \ar[d]_{\partial_2}
  & Z'_3 \ar[r]
    \ar[d]_{\partial_3}
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & Z_1 \ar[r]_{\phi}
  & Z_2 \ar[r]_{\psi}
  & Z_3
}
$$
There is a connecting homomorphism
$$
\delta_*:\ker\partial_3
\longrightarrow\operatorname{coker}\partial_1
$$
for which the following sequence is exact.
% D22
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.35em{
\ker\partial_1 \ar[r]^{\phi''}
  & \ker\partial_2 \ar[r]^{\psi''}
  & \ker\partial_3 \ar[r]^{\delta_*}
  & \operatorname{coker}\partial_1
    \ar[r]^{\bar\phi}
  & \operatorname{coker}\partial_2
    \ar[r]^{\bar\psi}
  & \operatorname{coker}\partial_3
}
$$
Here $\phi''$ and $\psi''$ are restrictions,
and $\bar\phi$ and $\bar\psi$ are induced
maps.

The famous snake is the bent connecting arrow
in the expanded diagram below.
% N01
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.7em@R=2.2em{
  & \ker\partial_1
    \ar@{^{(}->}[d]
    \ar@{-->}[r]^{\phi''}
  & \ker\partial_2
    \ar@{^{(}->}[d]
    \ar@{-->}[r]^{\psi''}
  & \ker\partial_3
    \ar@{^{(}->}[d]
    \ar@{-->}@/_4pc/[dddll]|{\delta_*}
  & \\
  & Z'_1 \ar[r]^{\phi'}
    \ar[d]_{\partial_1}
  & Z'_2 \ar[r]^{\psi'}
    \ar[d]_{\partial_2}
  & Z'_3 \ar[r]
    \ar[d]_{\partial_3}
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & Z_1 \ar[r]_{\phi}
    \ar@{->>}[d]
  & Z_2 \ar[r]_{\psi}
    \ar@{->>}[d]
  & Z_3 \ar@{->>}[d]
  & \\
  & \operatorname{coker}\partial_1
    \ar@{-->}[r]_{\bar\phi}
  & \operatorname{coker}\partial_2
    \ar@{-->}[r]_{\bar\psi}
  & \operatorname{coker}\partial_3
}
$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{remark}
The cokernel measures how far a homomorphism
is from being surjective. Notice that
$$
\operatorname{coker}\partial_i
=Z_i/\operatorname{im}\partial_i.
$$
\end{remark}

\section{Long exact homology sequences}
\label{section-long-exact-homology}

\begin{definition}
A sequence of chain complexes is
\emph{degreewise exact at $D_\bullet$} if
% D23
$$
\xymatrix@C=2em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & C_\bullet \ar[r]^f
  & D_\bullet \ar[r]^g
  & E_\bullet \ar[r]
  & \cdots
}
$$
induces an exact sequence
% D24
$$
\xymatrix@C=2em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & C_p \ar[r]^{f_p}
  & D_p \ar[r]^{g_p}
  & E_p \ar[r]
  & \cdots
}
$$
at $D_p$ for every $p\in\mathbb{Z}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-long-exact-homology}
Suppose that
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & A_\bullet \ar[r]^-\phi
  & B_\bullet \ar[r]^-\psi
  & C_\bullet \ar[r]
  & 0
}
$$
is a short exact sequence of chain
complexes.  There are connecting maps
$$
\delta_p:H_p(C_\bullet)
\longrightarrow H_{p-1}(A_\bullet)
$$
for which the sequence
$$
\xymatrix@C=.55em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & H_p(A_\bullet)
    \ar[r]^-{H_p(\phi)}
  & H_p(B_\bullet)
    \ar[r]^-{H_p(\psi)}
  & H_p(C_\bullet)
    \ar[r]^-{\delta_p}
  & H_{p-1}(A_\bullet)
    \ar[r]^-{H_{p-1}(\phi)}
  & H_{p-1}(B_\bullet)
    \ar[r]
  & \cdots
}
$$
is exact.
\end{theorem}

\noindent
The construction of the connecting map is
visible in the following commutative
diagram.  Its columns and rows are exact.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.55em@R=1.7em{
  & & 0 \ar[d]
    & 0 \ar[d]
    & 0 \ar[d] \\
  & \cdots \ar[r]
    & A_{p+1}
      \ar[r]^-{\partial_{p+1}}
      \ar[d]_{i_{p+1}}
    & A_p
      \ar[r]^-{\partial_p}
      \ar[d]_{i_p}
    & A_{p-1}
      \ar[r]
      \ar[d]_{i_{p-1}}
    & \cdots \\
  & \cdots \ar[r]
    & B_{p+1}
      \ar[r]^-{\partial_{p+1}}
      \ar[d]_{j_{p+1}}
    & B_p
      \ar[r]^-{\partial_p}
      \ar[d]_{j_p}
    & B_{p-1}
      \ar[r]
      \ar[d]_{j_{p-1}}
    & \cdots \\
  & \cdots \ar[r]
    & C_{p+1}
      \ar[r]^-{\partial_{p+1}}
      \ar[d]
    & C_p
      \ar[r]^-{\partial_p}
      \ar[d]
    & C_{p-1}
      \ar[r]
      \ar[d]
    & \cdots \\
  & & 0 & 0 & 0
}
$$

\begin{proof}
We spell out the diagram chase because it
is the useful part of the construction.
Begin with $c\in Z_p(C_\bullet)$.  Since
$j_p$ is surjective, choose $b\in B_p$
with $j_p(b)=c$.  Commutativity and
$\partial c=0$ give
$$
j_{p-1}(\partial b)=0.
$$
Exactness supplies $a\in A_{p-1}$ such
that
$$
i_{p-1}(a)=\partial b.
$$
Furthermore,
$$
i_{p-2}(\partial a)
=
\partial i_{p-1}(a)
=
\partial^2b
=0.
$$
The map $i_{p-2}$ is injective, so $a$ is
a cycle.  One checks that changing $b$ or
the representative $c$ changes $a$ by a
boundary.  Hence
$$
\delta_p[c]=[a]
$$
is well defined.  The same chase at each
term proves exactness.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-connecting-naturality}
Consider a commutative diagram of short
exact sequences of chain complexes.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.7em{
0 \ar[r]
  & A_\bullet \ar[r]^-i \ar[d]_f
  & B_\bullet \ar[r]^-j \ar[d]_g
  & C_\bullet \ar[r] \ar[d]_h
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & A'_\bullet \ar[r]
  & B'_\bullet \ar[r]
  & C'_\bullet \ar[r]
  & 0
}
$$
The induced long exact sequences form a
commutative diagram.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.35em@R=2.4em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & H_p(A) \ar[r] \ar[d]_{H_p(f)}
  & H_p(B) \ar[r] \ar[d]_{H_p(g)}
  & H_p(C)
    \ar[r]^-\delta
    \ar[d]_{H_p(h)}
  & H_{p-1}(A)
    \ar[r]
    \ar[d]_{H_{p-1}(f)}
  & H_{p-1}(B)
    \ar[r]
    \ar[d]_{H_{p-1}(g)}
  & H_{p-1}(C)
    \ar[r]
    \ar[d]_{H_{p-1}(h)}
  & \cdots \\
\cdots \ar[r]
  & H_p(A') \ar[r]
  & H_p(B') \ar[r]
  & H_p(C') \ar[r]_-{\delta'}
  & H_{p-1}(A') \ar[r]
  & H_{p-1}(B') \ar[r]
  & H_{p-1}(C') \ar[r]
  & \cdots
}
$$
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
All squares away from a connecting map
commute by functoriality.  At a connecting
map, choose a lift as in the preceding
proof.  Applying the vertical chain map
before or after the chase gives the same
class, by commutativity of the original
diagram.
\end{proof}

The square containing the connecting
homomorphisms is the substantive part of
the preceding naturality assertion.

\begin{lemma}[Five lemma]
\label{at-lemma-five}
Suppose the rows in the following
commutative diagram are exact.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.65em{
M_5 \ar[r]^-{f_5} \ar[d]_{h_5}
  & M_4 \ar[r]^-{f_4} \ar[d]_{h_4}
  & M_3 \ar[r]^-{f_3} \ar[d]_{h_3}
  & M_2 \ar[r]^-{f_2} \ar[d]_{h_2}
  & M_1 \ar[d]_{h_1} \\
N_5 \ar[r]_-{g_5}
  & N_4 \ar[r]_-{g_4}
  & N_3 \ar[r]_-{g_3}
  & N_2 \ar[r]_-{g_2}
  & N_1.
}
$$
If $h_5,h_4,h_2,h_1$ are isomorphisms,
then $h_3$ is an isomorphism as well.
\end{lemma}


Where will the five lemma be used? It is
the standard device for transporting
isomorphisms through the long exact
sequences in excision and universal
coefficient arguments.
\section{Hom and derived functors}
\label{at-section-hom-derived}

\noindent
Let $M$ and $N$ be $R$-modules.  The set
of $R$-linear maps from $M$ to $N$ is an
abelian group.  Its zero is the zero map,
and addition and scalar multiplication are
given pointwise by
$$
(f+g)(m)=f(m)+g(m)
$$
and
$$
(rf)(m)=r f(m).
$$
For a commutative ring, this makes
$\operatorname{Hom}_R(M,N)$ an $R$-module.

\noindent
Fixing $N$ gives the functor
$$
\operatorname{Hom}_R(-,N):
\operatorname{Mod}_R^{\mathrm{op}}
\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ab}.
$$
A map $\varphi:M\to M'$ induces
$$
\varphi^*:
\operatorname{Hom}_R(M',N)
\longrightarrow
\operatorname{Hom}_R(M,N)
$$
by precomposition.  In other words,
$$
\xymatrix{
M \ar[d]_\varphi
  \ar[rd]^{f\varphi} \\
M' \ar[r]_-f
  & N.
}
$$
The arrow reverses, so the functor is
contravariant.

\noindent
This functor is additive.  The universal
property behind the isomorphism
$$
\operatorname{Hom}_R(M_1\oplus M_2,N)
\cong
\operatorname{Hom}_R(M_1,N)
\oplus
\operatorname{Hom}_R(M_2,N)
$$
is pictured by
$$
\xymatrix{
M_1 \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
  \ar[rd]_f
  & M_1\oplus M_2
    \ar@{-->}[d]^{f\oplus g}
  & M_2 \ar@{_{(}->}[l]
    \ar[ld]^g \\
  & N.
}
$$
The map on the right sends $(f,g)$ to the
unique map satisfying
$$
(f\oplus g)(m_1,m_2)
=f(m_1)+g(m_2).
$$

\noindent
The functor is left exact.  A short exact
sequence
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & A \ar[r]^-\varphi
  & B \ar[r]^-\psi
  & C \ar[r]
  & 0
}
$$
induces an exact sequence
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(C,N)
    \ar[r]^-{\psi^*}
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(B,N)
    \ar[r]^-{\varphi^*}
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(A,N).
}
$$
The last map need not be surjective.

\begin{exercise}
\label{at-exercise-hom-left-exact}
Verify the preceding exactness and determine
what measures the cokernel of
$\varphi^*$.  The original attempted
solution observed only that left exactness
does not force a terminal zero.  A concrete
failure of surjectivity comes from
$$
0\longrightarrow2\mathbb{Z}
\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
\longrightarrow0
$$
after applying
$\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(-,
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})$.
Later we will identify the obstruction with
an $\operatorname{Ext}^1$ group.
\end{exercise}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-hom-second-variable}
Fixing the first variable instead gives a
covariant additive left-exact functor.  The
notes use $M^*$ for
$\operatorname{Hom}_R(M,N)$ and $M_*$ for
$\operatorname{Hom}_R(N,M)$.  These symbols
always depend on the module held fixed.
\end{remark}

\noindent
We now derive the contravariant functor.
An $R$-module is {\it free} if it has the
form
$$
L=\bigoplus_{i\in I}R e_i.
$$
Its basis has the following universal
property.  Every function from
$\{e_i\}_{i\in I}$ to an $R$-module $M$
extends uniquely to an $R$-linear map.
$$
\xymatrix{
\{e_i\}_{i\in I}
  \ar[r]^-f
  \ar@{^{(}->}[d]
  & M \\
L \ar@{-->}[ur]_-{\overline f}
}
$$
An $R$-module $P$ is {\it projective} if
there is an $R$-module $Q$ such that
$P\oplus Q$ is free.

\begin{example}
\label{at-example-chinese-remainder-six}
The source records
$$
\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
\cong
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
\oplus
\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}.
$$
This is the Chinese remainder theorem.  It
is not an example of a projective
$\mathbb{Z}$-module: a projective abelian
group is torsion-free.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-module-free-quotient}
Every $R$-module is a quotient of a free
$R$-module.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Take one basis element for every element of
$M$.  The universal property gives
$$
\xymatrix{
L=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{m\in M}R e_m
  \ar@{-->}[r]^-{\overline f}
  & M \\
M \ar@{^{(}->}[u]
  \ar[ur]
}
$$
where $\overline f(e_m)=m$.  This map is
surjective.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-projective-resolution}
A {\it free resolution} of an $R$-module
$M$ is an exact sequence
$$
\xymatrix{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & F_1 \ar[r]^-{d_1}
  & F_0 \ar[r]^-{d_0}
  & M \ar[r]
  & 0
}
$$
in which every $F_i$ is free.  Replacing
free modules by projective modules gives a
{\it projective resolution}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-projective-resolutions}
Every $R$-module has a free, hence a
projective, resolution.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose a free module $F_0$ surjecting onto
$M$.  Then choose $F_1$ surjecting onto
$\ker(d_0)$, and continue with successive
kernels.  The first step is
$$
\xymatrix{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & F_1 \ar[r]^-{d_1}
    \ar[rd]_{q_1}
  & F_0 \ar[r]^-{d_0}
  & M \ar[r]
  & 0 \\
  & & \ker(d_0)
      \ar@{^{(}->}[u].
}
$$
Here $d_1$ is the composite of $q_1$ with
the inclusion.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Comparison theorem]
\label{at-theorem-resolution-comparison}
Let $\alpha:M\to M'$ be a homomorphism and
choose projective resolutions of $M$ and
$M'$.  There is a chain map lifting
$\alpha$ in the diagram
$$
\xymatrix@C=.55em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & F_2 \ar[r]^-{d_2}
    \ar@{-->}[d]_{\alpha_2}
  & F_1 \ar[r]^-{d_1}
    \ar@{-->}[d]_{\alpha_1}
  & F_0 \ar[r]^-{d_0}
    \ar@{-->}[d]_{\alpha_0}
  & M \ar[r] \ar[d]_\alpha
  & 0 \\
\cdots \ar[r]
  & F'_2 \ar[r]_-{d'_2}
  & F'_1 \ar[r]_-{d'_1}
  & F'_0 \ar[r]_-{d'_0}
  & M' \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
Any two such lifts are chain homotopic.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $F_0$ is projective and
$d'_0:F'_0\longrightarrow M'$ is
surjective, the map
$\alpha d_0:F_0\longrightarrow M'$ lifts
to a map
$\alpha_0:F_0\longrightarrow F'_0$ such
that
$$
d'_0\alpha_0=\alpha d_0.
$$
The lift is represented by
$$
\xymatrix{
  & F'_0 \ar[d]^{d'_0} \\
F_0 \ar[r]_-{\alpha d_0}
  \ar@{-->}[ur]^{\alpha_0}
  & M'.
}
$$
Suppose maps have been constructed through
degree $n-1$.  The map
$\alpha_{n-1}d_n$ lands in
$\ker(d'_{n-1})=\operatorname{im}(d'_n)$.
Since $F_n$ is projective, it lifts through
the surjection
$$
F'_n\longrightarrow\operatorname{im}(d'_n).
$$
Thus the lifting step is
$$
\xymatrix{
  & F'_n \ar@{->>}[d]^{d'_n} \\
F_n \ar[r]_-{\alpha_{n-1}d_n}
  \ar@{-->}[ur]^{\alpha_n}
  & \operatorname{im}(d'_n).
}
$$
This gives $\alpha_n$, and induction gives
the required chain map.

\noindent
For uniqueness, let $\alpha_\bullet$ and
$\beta_\bullet$ be two lifts.  Their
difference is zero after augmentation.
Lift that zero successively through the
surjections onto boundaries.  The resulting
maps $h_n:F_n\to F'_{n+1}$ satisfy
$$
\alpha_n-\beta_n
=d'_{n+1}h_n+h_{n-1}d_n.
$$
Thus the two lifts are chain homotopic.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-ext}
Choose a projective resolution
$$
\xymatrix{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & F_1 \ar[r]^-{d_1}
  & F_0 \ar[r]^-{d_0}
  & M \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
Delete the augmentation to obtain
$$
\xymatrix{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & F_1 \ar[r]^-{d_1}
  & F_0 \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
Applying $\operatorname{Hom}_R(-,N)$ gives
the cochain complex
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F_0,N)
    \ar[r]^-{d_1^*}
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F_1,N)
    \ar[r]^-{d_2^*}
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F_2,N)
    \ar[r]
  & \cdots.
}
$$
The {\it derived functors of Hom} are
$$
\operatorname{Ext}^n_R(M,N)
=
H^n(\operatorname{Hom}_R(F_\bullet,N)).
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-ext-independent}
The module $\operatorname{Ext}^n_R(M,N)$
does not depend, up to canonical
isomorphism, on the projective resolution.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply the comparison theorem twice to two
resolutions of $M$ and the identity of $M$.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.5em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & F_2 \ar[r] \ar[d]_{\alpha_2}
  & F_1 \ar[r] \ar[d]_{\alpha_1}
  & F_0 \ar[r] \ar[d]_{\alpha_0}
  & M \ar[r] \ar@{=}[d]
  & 0 \\
\cdots \ar[r]
  & F'_2 \ar[r] \ar[d]_{\beta_2}
  & F'_1 \ar[r] \ar[d]_{\beta_1}
  & F'_0 \ar[r] \ar[d]_{\beta_0}
  & M \ar[r] \ar@{=}[d]
  & 0 \\
\cdots \ar[r]
  & F_2 \ar[r]
  & F_1 \ar[r]
  & F_0 \ar[r]
  & M \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
The composites $\beta\alpha$ and
$\alpha\beta$ are homotopic to the
respective identity maps.  Applying Hom
reverses the vertical arrows.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.7em{
\cdots
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F_1,N)
    \ar[l]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F_0,N)
    \ar[l] \\
\cdots
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F'_1,N)
    \ar[l]
    \ar[u]_{\alpha_1^*}
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F'_0,N)
    \ar[l]
    \ar[u]_{\alpha_0^*} \\
\cdots
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F_1,N)
    \ar[l]
    \ar[u]_{\beta_1^*}
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F_0,N)
    \ar[l]
    \ar[u]_{\beta_0^*}.
}
$$
Additivity carries the homotopies to the
dual complexes.  Hence the induced maps
on cohomology satisfy
$$
\xymatrix{
\operatorname{Ext}^n_R(M,N) \\
\operatorname{Ext}^n_R(M,N)
  \ar[u]^{H^n(\alpha^*)} \\
\operatorname{Ext}^n_R(M,N)
  \ar[u]^{H^n(\beta^*)}.
}
$$
After using the two resolutions to
distinguish the three displayed copies,
the adjacent maps are inverse
isomorphisms.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-ext-zero}
There is a natural isomorphism
$$
\operatorname{Ext}^0_R(M,N)
\cong\operatorname{Hom}_R(M,N).
$$
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The beginning of the resolution gives
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{im}(d_1)
    \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
  & F_0 \ar[r]^-{d_0}
  & M \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
Left exactness gives
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(M,N)
    \ar[r]^-{d_0^*}
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F_0,N)
    \ar[r]^-{d_1^*}
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(F_1,N).
}
$$
Thus $\ker(d_1^*)$ is the image of
$d_0^*$, which proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Horseshoe lemma]
\label{at-lemma-horseshoe}
Given a short exact sequence
$$
0\longrightarrow M'
\longrightarrow M
\longrightarrow M''
\longrightarrow0
$$
and projective resolutions of $M'$ and
$M''$, there is a projective resolution of
$M$ fitting into a short exact sequence of
resolutions.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.7em@R=1.5em{
  & 0
    & 0
    & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & M' \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & M \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & M'' \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & P'_0 \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & P_0 \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & P''_0 \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & P'_1 \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & P_1 \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & P''_1 \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & 0 \\
  & \vdots \ar[u]
  & \vdots \ar[u]
  & \vdots \ar[u]
}
$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
As modules, take the middle resolution to
be the direct sum of the outer two.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.7em@R=1.5em{
  & 0
    & 0
    & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & M' \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & M \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & M'' \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & P'_0 \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & P'_0\oplus P''_0 \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & P''_0 \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & 0 \\
  & \vdots \ar[u]
  & \vdots \ar[u]
  & \vdots \ar[u].
}
$$
Choose the off-diagonal pieces of the
differentials inductively so that each
square commutes.  Exactness follows by the
usual element chase.  This is the routine
part alluded to in the original notes.
\end{proof}

\noindent
Apply $\operatorname{Hom}_R(-,N)$ to the
horseshoe sequence.  Degree by degree we
obtain
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.1em{
  & 0 \ar[d]
    & 0 \ar[d]
    & 0 \ar[d] \\
0
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P'_0,N)
    \ar[l] \ar[d]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P_0,N)
    \ar[l] \ar[d]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P''_0,N)
    \ar[l] \ar[d]
  & 0 \ar[l] \\
0
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P'_1,N)
    \ar[l] \ar[d]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P_1,N)
    \ar[l] \ar[d]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P''_1,N)
    \ar[l] \ar[d]
  & 0 \ar[l] \\
  & \cdots & \cdots & \cdots.
}
$$
Equivalently, there is a short exact
sequence of cochain complexes
$$
\xymatrix{
0
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P'_\bullet,N)
    \ar[l]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P_\bullet,N)
    \ar[l]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P''_\bullet,N)
    \ar[l]
  & 0 \ar[l].
}
$$
The long exact sequence in cohomology is
$$
\xymatrix@C=.55em{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^0_R(M'',N)
    \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^0_R(M,N)
    \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^0_R(M',N)
    \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^1_R(M'',N)
    \ar[r]
  & \cdots.
}
$$
Its beginning can also be written
$$
\xymatrix@C=.55em{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(M'',N)
    \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(M,N)
    \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(M',N)
    \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^1_R(M'',N)
    \ar[r]
  & \cdots.
}
$$
This identifies the cokernel asked for in
Exercise
\ref{at-exercise-hom-left-exact}.

\noindent
A map between short exact sequences
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & M' \ar[r] \ar[d]
  & M \ar[r] \ar[d]
  & M'' \ar[r] \ar[d]
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & A' \ar[r]
  & A \ar[r]
  & A'' \ar[r]
  & 0
}
$$
lifts to a map of the corresponding
horseshoe resolutions and, after applying
Hom, to a reversed diagram
$$
\xymatrix@C=1em{
0
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P'_\bullet,N)
    \ar[l]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P_\bullet,N)
    \ar[l]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(P''_\bullet,N)
    \ar[l]
  & 0 \ar[l] \\
0
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(Q'_\bullet,N)
    \ar[l] \ar[u]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(Q_\bullet,N)
    \ar[l] \ar[u]
  & \operatorname{Hom}_R(Q''_\bullet,N)
    \ar[l] \ar[u]
  & 0 \ar[l].
}
$$
Consequently the long exact Ext sequences
form the commutative diagram
$$
\xymatrix@C=.4em{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^0_R(M'',N)
    \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^0_R(M,N)
    \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^0_R(M',N)
    \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^1_R(M'',N)
    \ar[r]
  & \cdots \\
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^0_R(A'',N)
    \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^0_R(A,N)
    \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^0_R(A',N)
    \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^1_R(A'',N)
    \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & \cdots.
}
$$
This is the basic game of derived functors:
the failure of exactness becomes a sequence
of new functors, naturally connected to one
another.

\noindent
From now until coefficients are changed
explicitly, take $R=\mathbb{Z}$.  If $A$
is an abelian group, choose a free
surjection $P_0\to A$.  Its kernel $P_1$
is free because every subgroup of a free
abelian group is free.  Thus
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & P_1 \ar[r]
  & P_0 \ar[r]
  & A \ar[r]
  & 0
}
$$
is a length-one free resolution.  Its dual
has the form
$$
\xymatrix{
0
  & \operatorname{Hom}(P_1,B) \ar[l]
  & \operatorname{Hom}(P_0,B) \ar[l]
  & 0 \ar[l].
}
$$
It follows that
$$
\operatorname{Ext}^n_{\mathbb{Z}}(A,B)=0
$$
for every $n\geq2$.

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-ext-calculations}
The following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The functor
$\operatorname{Ext}^n_R(-,N)$ is additive.
\item If $A$ is free, then
$$
\operatorname{Ext}^n_{\mathbb{Z}}(A,B)=0
$$
for every $n>0$.
\item There is a natural isomorphism
$$
\operatorname{Ext}^1_{\mathbb{Z}}
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z},B)
\cong B/nB.
$$
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For additivity, start with resolutions
$$
\xymatrix{
P'_\bullet \ar[r]
  & M' \ar[r]
  & 0 \\
P''_\bullet \ar[r]
  & M'' \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
Their direct sum is a resolution
$$
\xymatrix{
P'_\bullet\oplus P''_\bullet \ar[r]
  & M'\oplus M'' \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
Hom takes this finite direct sum to a
direct sum, and cohomology does likewise.
For a free $A$, use the resolution
$0\to A\to A\to0$.

\noindent
Finally, dualize the standard resolution of
$\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$.
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.2em{
0
  & \operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z},B)
    \ar[l]
    \ar[d]^\cong
  & \operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z},B)
    \ar[l]_-{n^*}
    \ar[d]^\cong
  & 0 \ar[l] \\
0
  & B \ar[l]
  & B \ar[l]^-n
  & 0 \ar[l].
}
$$
The degree-one cohomology is $B/nB$.
\end{proof}

\noindent
If $A$ is finitely generated and
$$
A\cong
\mathbb{Z}^r
\oplus
\mathbb{Z}/m_1\mathbb{Z}
\oplus\cdots\oplus
\mathbb{Z}/m_t\mathbb{Z},
$$
then additivity gives
$$
\operatorname{Ext}^1_{\mathbb{Z}}(A,B)
\cong
B/m_1B\oplus\cdots\oplus B/m_tB.
$$
\section{Cohomology and universal
coefficients}
\label{at-section-universal-coefficients}

\noindent
Let $G$ be an abelian group and let
$C_\bullet$ be a chain complex of free
abelian groups.
$$
\xymatrix{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & C_n \ar[r]^-{\partial_n}
  & C_{n-1}
    \ar[r]^-{\partial_{n-1}}
  & C_{n-2} \ar[r]
  & \cdots
}
$$
Applying
$\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(-,G)$
gives the cochain complex
$$
\xymatrix{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & C^{n-1} \ar[r]^-{\delta^{n-1}}
  & C^n \ar[r]^-{\delta^n}
  & C^{n+1} \ar[r]
  & \cdots,
}
$$
where
$$
C^n=\operatorname{Hom}(C_n,G)
$$
and
$$
\delta^n(\varphi)
=
\varphi\partial_{n+1}.
$$
The $n$-th cohomology group is
$$
H^n(C_\bullet;G)
=
\frac{\ker(\delta^n)}
{\operatorname{im}(\delta^{n-1})}.
$$
Thus the relevant part of the cochain
complex is
$$
\xymatrix{
\operatorname{Hom}(C_{n-1},G)
  \ar[r]^-{\delta^{n-1}}
  & \operatorname{Hom}(C_n,G)
    \ar[r]^-{\delta^n}
  & \operatorname{Hom}(C_{n+1},G).
}
$$
The coboundary is precomposition with the
chain boundary, as in
$$
\xymatrix{
C_n \ar[r]^-\varphi
  & G \\
C_{n+1} \ar[u]^{\partial_{n+1}}
  \ar[ur]_{\varphi\partial_{n+1}}.
}
$$
Cocycles are precisely the homomorphisms
that vanish on boundaries.

\noindent
Homology and dualization do not commute in
general.

\begin{example}
\label{at-example-uct-two-complex}
Consider the complex concentrated in
degrees one and zero,
$$
\xymatrix{
C_\bullet
  & 0 \ar[r]
  & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^-2
  & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
Dualizing with integer coefficients gives
$$
\xymatrix{
C^\bullet
  & 0
  & \mathbb{Z} \ar[l]
  & \mathbb{Z} \ar[l]_-2
  & 0 \ar[l].
}
$$
Consequently
$$
H^0(C_\bullet;\mathbb{Z})=0
$$
and
$$
H^1(C_\bullet;\mathbb{Z})
\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},
$$
whereas
$$
\operatorname{Hom}
(H_n(C_\bullet),\mathbb{Z})=0
$$
in every degree.
\end{example}

\noindent
There is nevertheless a natural evaluation
map
$$
h:H^n(C_\bullet;G)
\longrightarrow
\operatorname{Hom}(H_n(C_\bullet),G).
$$
If a cocycle $g:C_n\to G$ vanishes on
$B_n$, then its restriction to $Z_n$
factors through $Z_n/B_n$.  We set
$$
h[g]([z])=g(z).
$$

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-uct-map-surjective}
The map $h$ is surjective.  It has a group
homomorphism section, although the section
is not natural in $C_\bullet$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let
$$
\overline g:H_n(C_\bullet)\longrightarrow G.
$$
The sequence
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & Z_n \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
  & C_n \ar@{->>}[r]^-{\partial_n}
  & B_{n-1} \ar[r]
  & 0
}
$$
splits because $B_{n-1}$ is a subgroup of
the free abelian group $C_{n-1}$, hence is
free.  Choose a retraction $r:C_n\to Z_n$.
Then the dashed composite in
$$
\xymatrix{
Z_n \ar[r]
  & H_n(C_\bullet)
    \ar[r]^-{\overline g}
  & G \\
C_n \ar[u]^r
  \ar@{-->}[urr]_-g
}
$$
is a cocycle and restricts to
$\overline g$.  This produces a section of
$h$.  The choice of splitting explains why
the section is not natural.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{at-lemma-uct-split-form}
There is a noncanonical decomposition
$$
H^n(C_\bullet;G)
\cong
\operatorname{Hom}(H_n(C_\bullet),G)
\oplus\ker(h).
$$
\end{corollary}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-uct-kernel}
There is a natural isomorphism
$$
\ker(h)
\cong
\operatorname{Ext}^1_{\mathbb{Z}}
(H_{n-1}(C_\bullet),G).
$$
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $Z_n=\ker(\partial_n)$ and
$B_n=\operatorname{im}(\partial_{n+1})$.
There is a short exact sequence of graded
complexes
$$
\xymatrix@C=.6em@R=1.5em{
  & 0 \ar[d]
    & & \\
Z_\bullet \ar[d]
  & \cdots \ar[r]
  & Z_n \ar[r]^0 \ar@{^{(}->}[d]
  & Z_{n-1} \ar[r]^0
    \ar@{^{(}->}[d]
  & \cdots \\
C_\bullet \ar[d]
  & \cdots \ar[r]
  & C_n \ar[r]^-{\partial_n}
    \ar@{->>}[d]
  & C_{n-1}
    \ar[r]^-{\partial_{n-1}}
    \ar@{->>}[d]
  & \cdots \\
B_{\bullet-1} \ar[d]
  & \cdots \ar[r]
  & B_{n-1} \ar[r]^0
  & B_{n-2} \ar[r]^0
  & \cdots \\
  & 0
}
$$
whose degreewise rows are split.  After
dualizing, the order reverses:
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & B_{\bullet-1}^* \ar[r]
  & C_\bullet^* \ar[r]
  & Z_\bullet^* \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
The associated long exact sequence contains
$$
\xymatrix@C=.5em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & B_{n-1}^* \ar[r]
  & H^n(C_\bullet;G) \ar[r]
  & Z_n^* \ar[r]^-{i_n^*}
  & B_n^* \ar[r]
  & H^{n+1}(C_\bullet;G)
    \ar[r]
  & \cdots.
}
$$

\begin{lemma}[Claim]
\label{at-lemma-dual-inclusion-restriction}
The map
$$
i_n^*:Z_n^*\longrightarrow B_n^*
$$
is restriction along $B_n\subset Z_n$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-dual-inclusion}
More generally, the dual of an inclusion is
the corresponding restriction map.
\end{remark}

\noindent
The chain-level origin of the map can be
displayed as follows.  All arrows have now
been given the variance and indices forced
by dualization.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.35em@R=1.4em{
\cdots \ar[r]
  & Z_{n-1}^* \ar[r]^0
  & Z_n^* \ar[r]^0
  & Z_{n+1}^* \ar[r]
  & \cdots \\
\cdots \ar[r]
  & C_{n-1}^*
    \ar[r]^-{\delta^{n-1}} \ar[u]
  & C_n^* \ar[r]^-{\delta^n} \ar[u]
  & C_{n+1}^* \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & \cdots \\
\cdots \ar[r]
  & B_{n-2}^* \ar[r]^0 \ar[u]
  & B_{n-1}^* \ar[r]^0 \ar[u]
  & B_n^* \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & \cdots.
}
$$
Choosing an extension can be pictured by
$$
\xymatrix{
  & Z_n \ar[r]^-g
    \ar@{^{(}->}[d]
  & G \\
B_n \ar@{^{(}->}[ur]
  \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
  & C_n \ar@{-->}[ur]_-{\overline g} \\
  & C_{n+1}
    \ar[u]_{\partial_{n+1}}
    \ar[ul]^{\partial_{n+1}}
}
$$
but the extension is not canonical.  This
is why the source seemed to change a
restriction into an extension.

\noindent
Exactness now gives a short exact sequence
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{coker}(i_{n-1}^*)
    \ar[r]
  & H^n(C_\bullet;G) \ar[r]^-h
  & \ker(i_n^*) \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$

\begin{lemma}[Claim]
\label{at-lemma-uct-kernel-hom}
There is a natural equality
$$
\ker(i_n^*)
=
\operatorname{Hom}(H_n(C_\bullet),G).
$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[Claim]
\label{at-lemma-uct-kernel-cokernel}
Under the preceding identifications,
$$
\ker(h)=\operatorname{coker}(i_{n-1}^*).
$$
\end{lemma}

\noindent
It remains to identify this cokernel.  By
definition it completes the sequence
$$
\xymatrix{
Z_{n-1}^* \ar[r]^-{i_{n-1}^*}
  & B_{n-1}^* \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{coker}(i_{n-1}^*)
    \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
On the other hand, cycles and boundaries
give the free resolution
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
  & B_{n-1} \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
  & Z_{n-1} \ar@{->>}[r]
  & H_{n-1}(C_\bullet) \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
Dualizing it produces the exact sequence
{\small
$$
\xymatrix@C=.2em{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Hom}
    (H_{n-1}(C_\bullet),G)
  \ar[r]
  & Z_{n-1}^* \ar[r]^-{i_{n-1}^*}
  & B_{n-1}^* \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^1
    (H_{n-1}(C_\bullet),G)
  \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
}
This replaces the nonexact four-term sketch
in the source and proves the proposition.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Universal coefficient
theorem]
\label{at-theorem-universal-coefficients}
Let $G$ be an abelian group and let
$C_\bullet$ be a chain complex of free
abelian groups.  There is a natural split
short exact sequence
$$
\xymatrix@C=.55em{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^1_{
    \mathbb{Z}}(H_{n-1}(C_\bullet),G)
    \ar[r]
  & H^n(C_\bullet;G) \ar[r]^-h
  & \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}
    (H_n(C_\bullet),G)
    \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
The splitting need not be natural.

If $\alpha:C_\bullet\to C'_\bullet$ is a
chain map, naturality means that
$$
\xymatrix@C=.35em@R=2.3em{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^1
    (H_{n-1}(C_\bullet),G)
    \ar[r]
  & H^n(C_\bullet;G) \ar[r]^-h
  & \operatorname{Hom}
    (H_n(C_\bullet),G)
    \ar[r]
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{Ext}^1
    (H_{n-1}(C'_\bullet),G)
    \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & H^n(C'_\bullet;G)
    \ar[r]_-{h'} \ar[u]^{\alpha^*}
  & \operatorname{Hom}
    (H_n(C'_\bullet),G)
    \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & 0
}
$$
commutes.  The outer vertical maps are
induced contravariantly by the homology maps
$H_*(\alpha)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-name-universal}
The name reflects the fact that integral
homology determines cohomology with every
abelian coefficient group.
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
Only naturality remains.  The chain map
induces a map of split short exact
sequences
$$
\xymatrix@C=.7em{
0 \ar[r]
  & Z_\bullet \ar[r] \ar[d]
  & C_\bullet \ar[r] \ar[d]^\alpha
  & B_{\bullet-1} \ar[r] \ar[d]
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & Z'_\bullet \ar[r]
  & C'_\bullet \ar[r]
  & B'_{\bullet-1} \ar[r]
  & 0.
}
$$
Dualization reverses both rows and vertical
arrows.
$$
\xymatrix@C=.7em{
0 \ar[r]
  & B_{\bullet-1}^* \ar[r]
  & C_\bullet^* \ar[r]
  & Z_\bullet^* \ar[r]
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & B_{\bullet-1}^{'*}
    \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & C_\bullet^{'*}
    \ar[r] \ar[u]^{\alpha^*}
  & Z_\bullet^{'*}
    \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & 0.
}
$$
Passing to the short exact sequences
identified above gives
$$
\xymatrix@C=.5em{
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{coker}(i_{n-1}^*)
    \ar[r]
  & H^n(C_\bullet;G) \ar[r]
  & \ker(i_n^*) \ar[r]
  & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
  & \operatorname{coker}(i_{n-1}^{'*})
    \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & H^n(C'_\bullet;G)
    \ar[r] \ar[u]^{\alpha^*}
  & \ker(i_n^{'*})
    \ar[r] \ar[u]
  & 0,
}
$$
which is the desired naturality square.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{at-lemma-uct-finitely-generated}
Suppose $H_n(C_\bullet)$ and
$H_{n-1}(C_\bullet)$ are finitely
generated.  Write
$$
H_n(C_\bullet)
\cong\mathbb{Z}^{r_n}\oplus T_n
$$
and
$$
H_{n-1}(C_\bullet)
\cong\mathbb{Z}^{r_{n-1}}\oplus T_{n-1},
$$
where the $T_i$ are finite.  Then
$$
H^n(C_\bullet;\mathbb{Z})
\cong\mathbb{Z}^{r_n}\oplus T_{n-1}.
$$
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The universal coefficient theorem and the
calculations of Ext give
$$
\begin{aligned}
H^n(C_\bullet;\mathbb{Z})
&\cong
\operatorname{Hom}
(H_n(C_\bullet),\mathbb{Z})
\\
&\quad\oplus
\operatorname{Ext}^1
(H_{n-1}(C_\bullet),\mathbb{Z})
\\
&\cong
\mathbb{Z}^{r_n}\oplus T_{n-1}.
\end{aligned}
$$
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{at-lemma-homology-implies-cohomology}
If a chain map induces isomorphisms on
homology, then it induces isomorphisms on
cohomology with every fixed coefficient
group.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In the naturality diagram for the universal
coefficient theorem, the two outer vertical
maps are isomorphisms.  The five lemma gives
the result.
\end{proof}

\section{Simplices}
\label{section-simplices}

We begin with the geometric objects on which
singular chains are parametrized.

\begin{definition}
Let $v_0,\ldots,v_n$ be affinely
independent points in $\mathbb{R}^m$. The
\emph{$n$-simplex} with these vertices is
their convex hull, denoted
$[v_0,\ldots,v_n]$.
$$
[v_0,\ldots,v_n]
=
\{t_0v_0+\cdots+t_nv_n
\mid t_i\geq0,\ t_0+\cdots+t_n=1\}.
$$
\end{definition}

Here are the first four dimensions.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.86\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/simplices
}
\end{center}

\begin{definition}
The \emph{standard $n$-simplex} is the
convex hull of the standard basis vectors
$e_0,\ldots,e_n$ in $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$.
$$
\Delta^n=
\{(t_0,\ldots,t_n)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
\mid t_i\geq0,\ t_0+\cdots+t_n=1\}.
$$
\end{definition}

The corrected low-dimensional standard
simplices are shown below.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.72\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/standard-simplices
}
\end{center}

The order of the vertices matters. There is a
canonical affine homeomorphism

$$
\begin{aligned}
(v_0,\ldots,v_n):\Delta^n
&\longrightarrow[v_0,\ldots,v_n]\\
(t_0,\ldots,t_n)
&\longmapsto t_0v_0+\cdots+t_nv_n.
\end{aligned}
$$
The numbers $(t_0,\ldots,t_n)$ are the
\emph{barycentric coordinates} of the image
point.

\begin{definition}
A \emph{face} of $[v_0,\ldots,v_n]$ is
the subsimplex generated by a nonempty subset
of its vertices.
\end{definition}

We orient an edge $[v_i,v_j]$ from $v_i$ to
$v_j$ when $i<j$. The ordering of all the
vertices orients a higher-dimensional face.
Deleting $v_i$ gives the induced face with
the sign $(-1)^i$. This answers the question
from the original notes about the orientation
of two-dimensional faces.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.72\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/%
oriented-simplex-faces
}
\end{center}
\section{The singular chain complex}
\label{section-singular-chain-complex}

Take a topological space $X$ and an
associative ring with unit $R$.

\begin{definition}
A \emph{singular $n$-simplex} in $X$ is a
continuous map $\sigma:\Delta^n\to X$.
\end{definition}

The word ``singular'' comes from the fact
that no conditions
are imposed on $\sigma$ except continuity.
This means that a
singular simplex may look quite different
from what we first
imagine.

The free $R$-module on all singular
$n$-simplices is denoted $C_n(X)$. Its
elements are finite sums
$$
\sum_{i=1}^m r_i\sigma_i
\qquad
m\in\mathbb{N},\quad r_i\in R.
$$
These elements are called \emph{singular
$n$-chains}. We want to construct the
following sequence.
% D31
\begin{equation}
\label{equation-singular-chain-complex}
\xymatrix@C=1.7em{
\cdots \ar[r]
 & C_{n+1}(X) \ar[r]^{\partial_{n+1}}
 & C_n(X) \ar[r]^{\partial_n}
 & C_{n-1}(X) \ar[r]^{\partial_{n-1}}
 & \cdots \ar[r]^{\partial_1}
 & C_0(X)
}
\end{equation}
To do this it is enough to define
$\partial_n:C_n(X)\to C_{n-1}(X)$ as follows:
for a singular
$n$-simplex
$\sigma:\Delta^n=[v_0,\ldots,v_n]\to X$,
$$
\partial_n(\sigma)=
\sum_{i=0}^n(-1)^i
\sigma|[v_0,\ldots,\hat v_i,\ldots,v_n].
$$
Let $\delta_i:\Delta^{n-1}\to\Delta^n$
be the affine map onto the face obtained by
deleting $v_i$:
$$
[v_0,\ldots,\hat v_i,\ldots,v_n]
:=
[v_0,\ldots,v_{i-1},v_{i+1},\ldots,v_n],
$$
so the restriction in the formula means
$\sigma\delta_i$. The maps fit into the
following commutative diagram.
% D32
$$
\xymatrix@C=2.2em@R=2.5em{
[v_0,\ldots,\hat v_i,\ldots,v_n]
  \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
  & [v_0,\ldots,v_n] \ar[r]^{\sigma}
  & X \\
\Delta^{n-1} \ar[u]^{\cong}
  \ar[urr]_{\sigma\delta_i}
  & &
}
$$
The lower-left isomorphism sends vertices in
order and skips the $i$-th vertex. Defining
$\partial_n$ on this basis and extending
linearly gives the desired boundary map.

Now we see a proposition:

\begin{proposition}
$\partial_{n-1}\circ\partial_n=0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every codimension-two face occurs twice in
$\partial_{n-1}\partial_n\sigma$, with
opposite signs, according to which of its two
missing vertices is deleted first.
\end{proof}

Thus the sequence
\eqref{equation-singular-chain-complex} is a
chain complex, which we
call the \emph{singular chain complex of
$X$}, denoted
$C_\bullet(X)$. We can now consider its
homology groups and
define
$$
H_n(X;R):=H_n(C_\bullet(X))
$$
as the \emph{$n$-th singular homology group
of $X$ with
coefficients in $R$}.
\section{Homology and path-connected
components}
\label{section-homology-components}

\begin{proposition}
Let
$X=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}X_i$
be the decomposition of the topological
space $X$ into path-connected components.
Then
$$
H_n\left(\bigsqcup_{i\in I}X_i;R\right)
\cong
\bigoplus_{i\in I}H_n(X_i;R).
$$
\end{proposition}
\section{The zeroth homology group}
\label{section-zeroth-homology}

\begin{proposition}
For any space $X$, $H_0(X;R)$ is a direct sum
of copies of
$R$, one for each path-connected component.
\end{proposition}
\section{Homology of a point}
\label{section-homology-point}

\begin{proposition}
If $X$ consists of a single point, then
$$
H_0(X;R)\cong R,
$$
and $H_n(X;R)=0$ for $n\neq0$.
\end{proposition}

For this to be fully intuitive: we would like
the homology of
this space to be zero in all dimensions. We
will fix this in
the next section.
\section{Reduced
homology}\label{section-reduced-homology}

Reduced homology is a variant of singular
homology useful for
computations in low dimensions.

\begin{definition}
Let $X$ be a topological space and $R$ an
associative ring
with unit. The \emph{reduced homology} groups
of $X$ with
coefficients in $R$, denoted $\tilde H(X;R)$,
are the
homology groups of the chain complex
% D33
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.25em{
\cdots \ar[r]
 & C_p(X) \ar[r]^{\partial_p}
 & C_{p-1}(X) \ar[r]
 & \cdots \ar[r]
 & C_1(X) \ar[r]^{\partial_1}
 & C_0(X) \ar[r]^{\varepsilon}
 & R \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
where
$\varepsilon(\sum n_i\sigma_i)=\sum n_i$ is
the
\emph{augmentation map}.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Since the chain complex is identical to the
singular chain
complex except at the rightmost terms,
reduced homology can
only differ from singular homology in
dimension 0. That is,
$\tilde H_n(X;R)=H_n(X;R)$ for every
$n\geq1$.
\end{remark}

\begin{exercise}[Reduced homology of a
one-point space]
If $X=\{x\}$, then $\tilde H_0(X;R)=0$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
For a point, $C_0(X)$ is freely generated by
the constant zero-simplex. The augmentation
is
therefore an isomorphism. Equivalently, the
following sequence is exact.
% D34
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & \operatorname{im}\partial_1
   \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
 & C_0(X) \ar@{->>}[r]^{\varepsilon}
 & R \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
Here both $\ker\varepsilon$ and
$\operatorname{im}\partial_1$ vanish. Hence
$\tilde H_0(X;R)=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{exercise}
If $X$ is nonempty, then
$H_0(X;R)=\tilde H_0(X;R)\oplus R$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
By the definition of $\varepsilon$, we know
that
$\varepsilon|_{
\operatorname{im}\partial_1}=0$.

Recall that, in general, a map out of a group
descends to the
quotient by a normal subgroup if its kernel
contains that
subgroup. This means that we have an induced
map
$$
\bar\varepsilon:H_0(X;R)
=C_0(X)/\operatorname{im}\partial_1
\longrightarrow
R.
$$
This map is surjective, and its kernel is
$\ker\varepsilon/\operatorname{im}\partial_1
=\tilde H_0(X;R)$. Thus there is a short
exact
sequence.
% D35
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_0(X;R) \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
 & H_0(X;R) \ar@{->>}[r]^
   {\bar\varepsilon}
 & R \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
Choose a point $x\in X$. Sending $r\in R$ to
the class of $r x$ is a section of
$\bar\varepsilon$. The sequence therefore
splits, and
$$
H_0(X;R)\cong\tilde H_0(X;R)\oplus R.
$$
\end{proof}
\section{Functoriality}
\label{section-homology-functoriality}

In this section we will prove that, in
categorical language,
the assignment sending each topological space
to its chain
complex is functorial. Also, the assignment
sending each
space to its $n$-th homology group is
functorial.

In other words, what we will see is that maps
between
topological spaces induce maps between
singular chain
complexes and therefore also maps between
singular homology
groups; moreover, compositions go to
compositions and the
identity goes to the identity.
\section{Homotopy invariance}
\label{section-homology-homotopy-invariance}

First recall Definition
\ref{definition-homotopic-maps}.

\begin{definition}
Two functions $f,g:X\to Y$ are
\emph{homotopic} if there
exists a function $H:X\times I\to Y$ such
that
$$
H(x,0)=f(x)\qquad\forall x\in X,
\qquad
H(x,1)=g(x)\qquad\forall x\in X,
$$
and this is denoted by $f\simeq g$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
Two spaces $X$ and $Y$ are \emph{homotopy
equivalent} if
there exist functions called \emph{homotopy
equivalences}
$f:X\to Y$ and $g:Y\to X$ such that
$g\circ f\simeq\operatorname{id}_X$ and
$f\circ g\simeq\operatorname{id}_Y$. This is
denoted by $X\simeq Y$.
\end{definition}

Now:

\begin{theorem}
If two functions $f,g:X\to Y$ are homotopic,
then they induce
the same homomorphism on the $n$-th homology
group,
$f_*=g_*:H_n(X)\to H_n(Y)$, for every $n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}
If $f:X\to Y$ is a homotopy equivalence, then
the induced map
$f_*:H_n(X)\to H_n(Y)$ is an isomorphism for
every $n$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Using the theorem and the functoriality
properties.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
If $X\simeq\{x_0\}$, that is, if $X$ is
\emph{contractible}, then
$$
H_0(X;R)\cong R,
$$
and $H_n(X;R)=0$ for $n\neq0$.
\end{example}
\section{Relative homology}
\label{section-relative-homology}

Relative homology is a kind of singular
homology which
generalizes reduced homology. The point will
be to define the
homology of the space while ``ignoring what
happens inside
the subspace.''

Take topological spaces $A\subseteq X$. We
say that $(X,A)$
is a \emph{pair}. Notice that $C_\bullet(A)$
is a
subcomplex of $C_\bullet(X)$, so we can
define the relative
complex
$$
C_\bullet(X,A)=C_\bullet(X)/C_\bullet(A).
$$
This simply means that for every $n$,
$$
C_n(X,A)=C_n(X)/C_n(A),
$$
so chains in $A$ become trivial.

It is clear that the $n$-th boundary operator
restricted to
$C_n(A)$ maps to $C_{n-1}(A)$, since the
boundary of a chain
in $A$ could not leave $A$. This means that
the boundary map
is well defined on the quotient, giving a
chain complex
% D36
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.7em{
\cdots \ar[r]
 & C_{n+1}(X,A)
   \ar[r]^{\overline\partial_{n+1}}
 & C_n(X,A) \ar[r]^{\overline\partial_n}
 & C_{n-1}(X,A) \ar[r]
 & \cdots
}
$$
This induces the homology
$$
H_n(X,A)=
\ker\overline\partial_n/
\operatorname{im}\overline\partial_{n+1}.
$$

The first thing that happens is that we have
a short exact
sequence to which we will apply the
fundamental theorem of
homological algebra:
% D37
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & C_\bullet(A) \ar@{^{(}->}[r]^i
 & C_\bullet(X) \ar@{->>}[r]^j
 & C_\bullet(X,A) \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
Thus we obtain the \emph{long exact sequence
of the pair}
% D38
$$
\xymatrix@C=.65em{
\cdots \ar[r]
 & H_n(A) \ar[r]^{i_*}
 & H_n(X) \ar[r]^{j_*}
 & H_n(X,A) \ar[r]^{\delta_n}
 & H_{n-1}(A) \ar[r]^{i_*}
 & H_{n-1}(X) \ar[r]^{j_*}
 & H_{n-1}(X,A) \ar[r]
 & \cdots
}
$$

\begin{remark}
If the homology groups of the pair $C_p(X,A)$
were trivial,
we would automatically have that the map
induced by the
inclusion is an isomorphism. In fact, this is
an if and only
if. Thus the homology groups of the pair
measure how
different the homology groups of $A$ are from
those of $X$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
We add that although the map $\delta$ used to
complete the
long exact sequence of the pair comes from
the fundamental
theorem of homological algebra, when we
recall the proof of
that theorem we see that this map acts
exactly like the
original boundary operator of $X$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
It is also possible to define the
\emph{reduced homology of
the pair}; see Hatcher. In fact it turns out
to be equal to
the homology of the pair just defined, that
is,
$\tilde H_n(X,A)=H_n(X,A)$.
\end{remark}

\begin{exercise}
For a nonempty space $X$ and $x_0\in X$,
$$
H_n(X,\{x_0\})\cong\tilde H_n(X)
$$
for every $n\geq0$.
\begin{proof}[Solution]
The reduced long exact sequence of the pair
and the vanishing of all reduced homology
groups of a point give the isomorphism.

The original notes ask whether this can be
seen before introducing the long exact
sequence. It can: quotienting $C_0(X)$ by the
constant simplex at $x_0$ produces a chain
complex chain-homotopy equivalent to the
augmented singular complex. Thus the same
isomorphism follows directly at chain level.
\end{proof}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
Let $X=S^1\times S^1$, and let $A$ be a
nonempty finite subset with $k$ points.
Compute $H_n(X,A;R)$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}[Solution]
The positive homology of the discrete space
$A$ vanishes; its singular chain groups do
not vanish. Since $A$ has disjoint
contractible
neighbourhoods, $(X,A)$ is a good pair. The
relevant reduced exact sequence and its
evaluation are as follows.
% D63
$$
\xymatrix@C=.9em@R=2.5em{
\cdots \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_1(A) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_1(X) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_1(X/A) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_0(A) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_0(X) \ar[r]
 & \cdots \\
\cdots \ar[r]
 & 0 \ar[r]
 & R^2 \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_1(X/A) \ar[r]
 & R^{k-1} \ar[r]
 & 0 \ar[r]
 & \cdots
}
$$
The right-hand module is free, so this short
exact portion splits. Consequently
$$
H_1(X,A;R)\cong R^{k+1}.
$$
The rest of the pair sequence gives
$$
H_2(X,A;R)\cong R,
\qquad
H_0(X,A;R)=0,
$$
and all groups in degrees greater than two
vanish.
\end{proof}
\section{Maps of pairs}
\label{section-maps-of-pairs}

\begin{remark}
A function between pairs $f:(X,A)\to(Y,B)$
induces a function
$f_\#:C_\bullet(X,A)\to C_\bullet(Y,B)$ and
therefore also a
function $f_*:H_n(X,A)\to H_n(Y,B)$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
If two functions between pairs
$f,g:(X,A)\to(Y,B)$ are
homotopic through functions between pairs,
then the induced
maps $f_*$ and $g_*$ are equal.
\end{proposition}
\section{Splitting lemma}
\label{section-splitting-lemma}
\label{subsec:splitting}

Take the case where there is a retraction
$r:X\to A$, that
is, $r\circ i=\operatorname{id}_A$, where
$i$ is the inclusion.
This implies that
$(ri)_*=r_*i_*=id_{H_n(A)}$, so $i_*$ is
injective, and in
fact we can break the exact sequence of the
pair into short
exact sequences of the form
% D39
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & H_n(A) \ar@{^{(}->}[r]^{i_*}
 & H_n(X) \ar@{->>}[r]^{j_*}
   \ar@/^1pc/[l]^{r_*}
 & H_n(X,A) \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
But there is more.

\begin{lemma}[Splitting lemma]
For any short exact sequence of abelian
groups
% D40
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & A \ar@{^{(}->}[r]^i
 & B \ar@{->>}[r]^j
 & C \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
the following statements are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] there exists a homomorphism
$p:B\to A$ such that
$p\circ i=\operatorname{id}_A$;
\item[(b)] there exists a homomorphism
$q:C\to B$ such that
$j\circ q=\operatorname{id}_C$;
\item[(c)] there exists an isomorphism
$B\cong A\oplus C$
which makes the corresponding diagram
commute, with the
obvious maps in the last row, $a\mapsto(a,0)$
and
$(a,c)\mapsto c$.
\end{enumerate}
In this case we say that the sequence
\emph{splits}.
% D41
$$
\xymatrix@C=2em@R=2.6em{
A \ar@{^{(}->}[r]^i \ar@{=}[d]
 & B \ar@{->>}[r]^j \ar[d]^{\cong}
   \ar@/^1pc/[l]^p
 & C \ar@{=}[d] \ar@/^1pc/[l]^q \\
A \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
 & A\oplus C \ar@{->>}[r]
 & C
}
$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{remark}
\begin{itemize}
\item If $C$ is a free abelian group, then
the sequence splits. The converse need not
hold.
\item A retraction $r:X\to A$ gives
$H_n(X)\cong H_n(A)\oplus H_n(X,A)$.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}
\section{The long exact sequence of a triple}
\label{section-long-exact-triple}

If $Z\subseteq Y\subseteq X$ are topological
spaces, it is
immediate from the definition that
$$
C_\bullet(Z)\leq C_\bullet(Y)\leq
C_\bullet(X).
$$
Taking the quotient by $Z$ gives a short
exact sequence.
% D42
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & C_\bullet(Y,Z)
   \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
 & C_\bullet(X,Z)
   \ar@{->>}[r]
 & C_\bullet(X,Y) \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
In the last equality we use an isomorphism
theorem to see
that
$$
\frac{C_\bullet(X)/C_\bullet(Z)}
{C_\bullet(Y)/C_\bullet(Z)}
=
C_\bullet(X,Y).
$$
Applying the fundamental theorem of
homological algebra gives
the \emph{long exact sequence of the triple}
% D43
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.4em{
\cdots \ar[r]
 & H_n(Y,Z) \ar[r]^{i_*}
 & H_n(X,Z) \ar[r]^{j_*}
 & H_n(X,Y) \ar[r]^{\delta}
 & H_{n-1}(Y,Z) \ar[r]^{i_*}
 & \cdots
}
$$
\section{Excision}\label{section-excision}

This result makes much more precise the idea
that homology of
a pair is the homology of the space while
``ignoring what
happens in the subspace.''

Let $Z\subseteq A\subseteq X$ be such that
the closure of $Z$
is contained in the interior of $A$. Then
$$
(X-Z,A-Z)\hookrightarrow (X,A)
$$
induces an isomorphism
$$
H_n(X-Z,A-Z)\to H_n(X,A)
\qquad\forall n.
$$
Equivalently, for subspaces $A,B\subseteq X$
whose interiors
cover $X$, the inclusion
$$
(B,A\cap B)\hookrightarrow (X,A)
$$
induces an isomorphism
$$
H_n(B,A\cap B)\to H_n(X,A)
\qquad\forall n.
$$
\section{The homology of a quotient}
\label{section-homology-quotient}

Given a topological space $X$ and a subspace
$A\subseteq X$,
we can consider the space $X/A$, obtained by
identifying all
points of $A$. This space is like $X$ but
ignoring everything
that happens in $A$. This reminds us of
homology of a pair,
and the natural question arises: is there any
relation
between $H_n(X,A)$ and $H_n(X/A)$? We need
some conditions.

\begin{definition}
We say that $(X,A)$ is a \emph{good pair} if
$A$ is closed
and is a strong deformation retract of some
neighbourhood in
$X$.
\end{definition}

The Hawaiian earring with the attaching point
is not a good
pair because every neighbourhood of that
point contains an
entire circle, so it cannot be deformation
retracted.
