\begin{theorem}
Let $(X,A)$ be a good pair. Then
$$
q:(X,A)\to(X/A,A/A)
$$
induces isomorphisms for every $n$ of the
form
$$
q_*:H_n(X,A)\to H_n(X/A,A/A)
\cong\tilde H_n(X/A),
$$
where the tilde denotes reduced homology.
\end{theorem}

The proof uses the five lemma, homotopy
invariance, etc.

\begin{corollary}
Let $(X,A)$ be a good pair. Then we have the
following long
exact sequence:
% D44
$$
\xymatrix@C=.6em{
\cdots \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_n(A) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_n(X) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_n(X/A) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_{n-1}(A) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_{n-1}(X) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_{n-1}(X/A) \ar[r]
 & \cdots
}
$$
\end{corollary}
\section{Homology of the sphere and
applications}
\label{section-sphere-homology-applications}

\begin{theorem}[Homology of the sphere]
$$
\tilde H_n(S^n;R)\cong R.
$$
All other reduced homology groups of $S^n$
vanish.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Brouwer fixed point theorem]
Let $n\geq2$ and $f:D^n\to D^n$. Then $f$ has
a fixed point.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Homology of the wedge]
Consider a wedge of spaces
$\bigvee_{\alpha\in I}X_\alpha$
such that $(X_\alpha,x_\alpha)$ is a good
pair for every
$\alpha$, where $x_\alpha$ is the attaching
point. Then the
inclusions $i_\alpha:X_\alpha\hookrightarrow
\bigvee_{\alpha\in I}X_\alpha$ induce an
isomorphism
$$
\bigoplus i_{\alpha *}:
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in I}\tilde H_n(X_\alpha)
\to
\tilde H_n\left(
\bigvee_{\alpha\in I}X_\alpha
\right).
$$
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Invariance of dimension]
Let $U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ and
$V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^m$ be open sets. If
$U$ and $V$ are homeomorphic, then $n=m$.
\end{theorem}
\section{The Mayer--Vietoris sequence}
\label{section-mayer-vietoris}

Let $X$ be a topological space and let
$A,B\subseteq X$ be
such that $X=\operatorname{int}
A\cup\operatorname{int} B$. Then we have the
following
long exact sequence in reduced homology:
% D45
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.1em{
\cdots \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_n(A\cap B) \ar[r]^{\Phi}
 & \tilde H_n(A)\oplus\tilde H_n(B)
   \ar[r]^{\Psi}
 & \tilde H_n(X) \ar[r]^{\delta}
 & \tilde H_{n-1}(A\cap B) \ar[r]
 & \cdots
}
$$

To understand where this sequence came from,
we should look
at the chain subgroups $C_n(A+B)$ formed by
sums of chains in
$A$ and chains in $B$. It is possible to
construct a chain
complex, since the boundary homomorphism of
the whole space,
$\partial:C_n(X)\to C_{n-1}(X)$, restricts
correctly to
$C_n(A+B)\to C_{n-1}(A+B)$. Using the
machinery of the
excision theorem, one proves that the
inclusion
$C_n(A+B)\hookrightarrow C_n(X)$ induces
isomorphisms on
homology. Then, for every $n$, we have the
short exact
sequence
$$
0\to C_n(A\cap B)\xrightarrow{\varphi}
C_n(A)\oplus
C_n(B)\xrightarrow{\psi}C_n(A+B)\to0,
$$
where $\varphi(x)=(x,-x)$ and
$\psi(x,y)=x+y$.

This way of defining the functions ensures
that the sequence
is exact. It is easy to see that
$\psi\varphi=0$, so
$\operatorname{im}\varphi\subseteq\ker\psi$.
Also
$\ker\psi\subseteq\operatorname{im}\varphi$:
if
$(x,y)\overset{\psi}{\mapsto}(0,0)$, then
$x=-y$, so both
$x$ and $y$ lie in $A$ and in $B$, that is,
$(x,y)\in C_n(A\cap B)$; clearly it lies in
the image of
$\varphi$. It is also easy to see that
$\ker\varphi=0$ and
that $\operatorname{im}\psi=C_n(A+B)$.

Applying the fundamental theorem of
homological algebra to
the short exact sequence of chain complexes
induced by these
short exact sequences in each degree gives
the Mayer--Vietoris
sequence.
% D46
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.6em{
0 \ar[r]
 & C_\bullet(A\cap B)
   \ar@{^{(}->}[r]^{(i_\#,-j_\#)}
 & C_\bullet(A)\oplus C_\bullet(B)
   \ar@{->>}[r]^{k_\#+\ell_\#}
 & C_\bullet(A+B) \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$

It is possible to say how the connecting
homomorphism acts. By what
we said about excision, an element
$[w]\in H_n(X)\cong H_n(A+B)$ is of the form
$[a+b]$ for
chains $a\in C_n(A)$ and $b\in C_n(B)$ whose
sum $a+b$ is a
cycle, that is, $\partial(a+b)=0$. Then
$\partial(a)=-\partial(b)$ is a cycle in
$H_{n-1}(A\cap B)$,
so it is the perfect representative for the
equivalence class
to which we send $[w]$.

Write $i,j,k,\ell$ for the four inclusion
maps. Then
$$
\begin{aligned}
i:A\cap B&\hookrightarrow A,
&j:A\cap B&\hookrightarrow B,\\
\Phi(x)&=(i_*x,-j_*x),
&\Psi(y,z)&=k_*y+\ell_*z.
\end{aligned}
$$
If $w=a+b$ is a cycle, with $a$ supported
in $A$ and $b$ supported in $B$, then
$$
\delta[w]=[\partial a]=[-\partial b].
$$

\begin{example}
It is possible to compute the homology of the
torus using the
Mayer--Vietoris sequence, although it is not
enough to write
down the exact sequence: one has to
understand how the
homomorphisms act.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
There is a version of this sequence for
$X=A\cup B$ where
$A$ and $B$ are deformation retracts of
certain
neighbourhoods $U$ and $V$ and where $U\cap
V$ retracts onto
$A\cap B$.

In particular, if $X$ is a CW complex and
$A,B\subseteq X$
are CW subcomplexes, then it is possible to
prove that such
neighbourhoods exist, and we obtain the same
Mayer--Vietoris
sequence
% D47
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.2em{
\cdots \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_n(A\cap B) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_n(A)\oplus\tilde H_n(B)
   \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_n(X) \ar[r]
 & \tilde H_{n-1}(A\cap B) \ar[r]
 & \cdots
}
$$
Now $A$ and $B$ do not have to be open, and
it does not have
to be true that $\operatorname{int}
A\cup\operatorname{int} B=X$.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
\label{example-torus-mayer-vietoris}
The original notes ask how to complete the
torus calculation. The proposed ``disk plus
wedge'' is not a cover by CW subcomplexes:
the
closed characteristic disk is not embedded in
the quotient model. This is the missing
point.

Instead, cover the second circle by two arcs
$U$ and $V$ whose intersection has two
components. Put
$$
A=S^1\times U,
\qquad
B=S^1\times V.
$$
Both $A$ and $B$ retract to a circle, while
$A\cap B$ retracts to two circles. On first
homology, after compatible choices of
generators, $\Phi$ has rank one:
$$
\Phi(a,b)=(a+b,-a-b).
$$
Thus $\ker\Phi\cong R$ and
$\operatorname{coker}\Phi\cong R$. The
degree-one and degree-zero portions of the
sequence then give
$$
H_2(T^2;R)\cong R,
\qquad
H_1(T^2;R)\cong R\oplus R.
$$
This agrees with the later cellular
calculation.
\end{example}


\begin{exercise}
Glue a M\"obius band $M$ to the torus $T$
by identifying $\partial M$ with the circle
$S^1\times\{0\}$ in $T$. Compute the homology
of the resulting space $X$ using
Mayer--Vietoris.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}[Solution]
The cell pictures identify the common circle.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.75\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/%
torus-mobius-gluing
}
\end{center}

The boundary of the M\"obius band uses two
edges, while the chosen torus circle uses
one.
Thus a direct cellular gluing is
inconvenient.
Both pieces nevertheless retract from
suitable
open neighbourhoods, so Mayer--Vietoris
applies.
Its relevant abstract fragments are
% D68 and D69
$$
\xymatrix@C=.55em@R=2.5em{
0 \ar[r]
 & \widetilde H_2(T\cap M) \ar[r]
 & \widetilde H_2(T)\oplus
   \widetilde H_2(M) \ar[r]
 & \widetilde H_2(X)
   \ar@/_1pc/[dll]_{\delta_2}
 & \\
 & \widetilde H_1(T\cap M)
   \ar[r]^{\Phi_1}
 & \widetilde H_1(T)\oplus
   \widetilde H_1(M) \ar[r]
 & \widetilde H_1(X) \ar[r]
 & \widetilde H_0(T\cap M) \ar[r]
 & 0.
}
$$
After substituting the known groups, these
become
% D70 and D71
$$
\xymatrix@C=.75em@R=2.5em{
0 \ar[r]
 & 0 \ar[r]
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^{\Psi_2}
 & \widetilde H_2(X)
   \ar@/_1pc/[dll]_{\delta_2}
 & \\
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^{\Phi_1}
 & \mathbb{Z}^3 \ar[r]^{\Psi_1}
 & \widetilde H_1(X) \ar[r]
 & 0.
}
$$
With the convention
$\Phi_1=(i_*,-j_*)$, the common-circle
generator maps to
$$
\Phi_1(1)=(1,0,-2).
$$
This map is injective and its cokernel is
$\mathbb{Z}^2$. Exactness therefore gives
$$
H_2(X)\cong\mathbb{Z},
\qquad
H_1(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}^2,
\qquad
H_0(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}.
$$
All higher groups vanish.
\end{proof}
\section{Representatives of the homology of
the sphere}
\label{section-sphere-homology-generators}

In this section we use $R=\mathbb{Z}$.

Our method will be to find generators for the
relative
homology $H_n(D^n,\partial D^n)$, since this
should be
isomorphic to $H_n(S^n)$. The first thing we
do is replace
that pair by the following one, to which it
is also
isomorphic: $(\Delta^n,\partial\Delta^n)$.

\begin{proposition}
The identity map
$\operatorname{id}:\Delta^n\to\Delta^n$
is a cycle which
generates
$H_n(\Delta^n,\partial\Delta^n)
\cong\mathbb{Z}$.
\begin{proof}
The boundary of the identity simplex is
supported in $\partial\Delta^n$, so its class
is a relative cycle. For $n=0$ the assertion
is immediate. For $n>0$, contractibility of
$\Delta^n$ makes the connecting map
$$
H_n(\Delta^n,\partial\Delta^n)
\longrightarrow
\tilde H_{n-1}(\partial\Delta^n)
$$
an isomorphism. It sends the identity simplex
to its alternating boundary. Inductively that
oriented boundary is a generator of
$\tilde H_{n-1}(S^{n-1})\cong\mathbb{Z}$.
Hence the relative identity class is a
generator.
\end{proof}
\end{proposition}

Now let us think of $S^n$ as the union of two
standard
simplices glued along their boundary, denoted
$\Delta_1^n\cup_{\partial\Delta^n}
\Delta_2^n$.

\begin{proposition}
$\Delta^n_1-\Delta^n_2$ is a generator of
$H_n(S^n)$ for
$n>0$.
\begin{proof}
The two simplices are given opposite induced
orientations along their common boundary, so
their difference is a cycle. Collapsing one
simplex to a point identifies its class with
the relative generator above, hence it
generates $H_n(S^n)$.
\end{proof}
\end{proposition}
\section{Degree of a map between spheres}
\label{section-degree-sphere-maps}

Consider $f:S^n\to S^n$, which induces a
function
$$
f_*:\tilde H_n(S^n)\cong\mathbb{Z}
\to
\tilde H_n(S^n)\cong\mathbb{Z},
$$
so that $f_*$ has no choice but to be of the
form
$f_*(n\alpha)=dn\alpha$.

\begin{definition}
In the notation above, the \emph{degree} of
$f$ is $d$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\deg Id=1$, by functoriality.
\item If $f$ is not surjective, then
$\deg(f)=0$. Since $f$
is not surjective, choose a point
$y\notin\operatorname{im}f$. Then $f$ factors
through the punctured sphere.
% D48
$$
\xymatrix{
S^n \ar[rr]^f
  \ar[dr]_{\bar f}
  & & S^n \\
  & S^n\setminus\{y\}
    \ar[ur]_{i}
}
$$
The induced map factors through zero
homology.
% D49
$$
\xymatrix{
\tilde H_n(S^n) \ar[rr]^{f_*}
  \ar[dr]_{\bar f_*}
  & & \tilde H_n(S^n) \\
  & \tilde H_n(S^n\setminus\{y\})=0
    \ar[ur]_{i_*}
}
$$
Therefore $f_*=0$.
\item If $f\simeq g$, then $\deg f=\deg g$.
By homotopy
invariance, the induced functions are equal.
\item $\deg(fg)=\deg(f)\deg(g)$.
\item If $f$ is a reflection interchanging
the hemispheres,
then $\deg(f)=-1$.

This is because, as seen in the previous
section, the
$n$-sphere, viewed as the union of two
standard simplices,
$S^n=\Delta_1^n
\cup_{\partial\Delta^n}\Delta_2^n$,
has
$n$-th homology generated by the chain
$\Delta_1^n-\Delta_2^n$. The reflection
interchanging these
simplices sends the generator to its
negative.
\item The antipodal map
$-\operatorname{id}:S^n\to S^n$ has degree
$$
\deg(-\operatorname{id})=(-1)^{n+1}.
$$
Thus its degree is one for odd $n$ and minus
one for even $n$.
\item If $f$ has no fixed points, then it is
homotopic to the antipodal map, and hence
$\deg(f)=(-1)^{n+1}$. Indeed, the segment
from $f(x)$ to $-x$ avoids the origin. Thus
$$
H(x,t)=
\frac{(1-t)f(x)-tx}
{\lVert(1-t)f(x)-tx\rVert}
$$
is the required homotopy.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}


\begin{exercise}
Construct a surjective map $S^n\to S^n$ of
degree zero for every $n\geq1$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}[Solution]
On $S^1$, let $g$ traverse one semicircle out
and back. It is nonsurjective, hence has
degree
zero. The squaring map $q(z)=z^2$ maps that
semicircle onto the whole circle. Thus
$qg:S^1\to S^1$ is surjective and
$$
\deg(qg)=\deg(q)\deg(g)=0.
$$
Iterated suspension gives the required maps
in every higher dimension: suspension
preserves
both surjectivity and degree.
\end{proof}
\section{Local degree}
\label{section-local-degree}
\label{subsec:grloc}

Following Hatcher:

\begin{definition}
Suppose that $f:S^n\to S^n$ is such that the
inverse image of
some $y\in S^n$ consists of finitely many
points, say
$f^{-1}(y)=\{x_1,\ldots,x_m\}$. Take a family
of disjoint
open sets $U_1,\ldots,U_m$ containing each
$x_i$, and which
map to a neighbourhood $V$ of $y$ under $f$.
Through a
commutative diagram, using excision and the
long exact
sequence, Hatcher argues that the
homomorphism $f_*$
originally defined as
$$
H_n(S^n,S^n-x_i)\xleftarrow{\cong}
H_n(U_i,U_i-x_i)\xrightarrow{f_*}H_n(V,V-y)
$$
is of the form $f_*:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}$,
multiplication by a number
called the \emph{local degree} of $f$,
denoted
$\deg f|x_i$.
\end{definition}

And then:

\begin{theorem}
$\deg f=\sum_i\deg f|_{x_i}$.
\end{theorem}

We add another proposition.

\begin{proposition}
The degree of the suspension $Sf:S^{n+1}\to
S^{n+1}$ of a
function $f:S^n\to S^n$ is equal to the
degree of $f$.
\end{proposition}
\section{The hairy ball theorem}
\label{section-hairy-ball}

\begin{theorem}[Hairy ball theorem]
$S^n$ has a vector field which vanishes
nowhere if and only
if $n$ is odd.
\end{theorem}
\section{Free actions on the sphere}
\label{section-free-sphere-actions}

\begin{theorem}[Free actions on the sphere]
If a nontrivial finite group acts freely on
an
even-dimensional sphere, then it is
isomorphic
to $\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$.
\end{theorem}
\section{CW complexes}
\label{section-cw-complexes}

Let us establish some notation.
\begin{itemize}
\item $D^n=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^n:
\lVert x\rVert\leq1\}$ is the closed
$n$-disk.
\item $S^{n-1}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^n:
\lVert x\rVert=1\}$ is its boundary sphere.
\item $e^n=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^n:
\lVert x\rVert<1\}$ is the open $n$-cell,
or simply the $n$-cell.
\end{itemize}
Thus
\begin{itemize}
\item $\partial D^n=S^{n-1}$;
\item $D^n=e^n\cup S^{n-1}$ as sets, not
using the disjoint
union topology;
\item and, well, it should be true that
$e^0=\{pt\}$.
\end{itemize}

\begin{definition}
A \emph{CW complex} is a space $X$
constructed
by the following procedure:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Start with a discrete space $X^0$,
called the
0-skeleton.
\item Obtain the $n$-skeleton $X^n$ from the
$(n-1)$-skeleton
$X^{n-1}$ by gluing $n$-cells $e^n_\alpha$
via maps
$\varphi_\alpha:S^{n-1}\to X^{n-1}$.
Formally,
$$
X^n=X^{n-1}\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in I_n}
D^n_\alpha\Big/\sim,
\qquad
x\sim\varphi_\alpha(x).
$$
The displayed relation is imposed for
$x\in\partial D^n_\alpha$.
The point is to take points and join them
with
lines, and then take lines and fill them with
disks, and so on.
\item We could stop after finitely many
steps, say $m$, and
obtain $X=X^m$, but we can also continue
gluing cells of
arbitrarily large dimension and obtain
$X=\bigcup_nX^n$. In
this case $X$ has the weak topology: a subset
$A\subseteq X$ is closed if and only if
$A\cap X^n$ is closed in $X^n$ for every $n$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
If $X=X^n$ for some $n\in\mathbb{N}$, we say
that $X$ is \emph{finite-dimensional} and
define the \emph{dimension} of $X$ as the
dimension of the largest cell adjoined.
Formally,
$\dim X=\min\{n:X^n=X\}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
\begin{itemize}
\item The zero-dimensional CW complexes are
precisely the discrete spaces. Graphs are
one-dimensional CW complexes.
\item $S^n$ is a CW complex.
\item Real projective space. Initially
defined as
$\mathbb{R}
P^n=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}-\{0\}/x\sim\lambda x$
for $\lambda\neq0$,
we can take representatives of the lines on
the sphere to
obtain $\mathbb{R} P^n=S^n/x\sim -x$.

This is equivalent to taking either
hemisphere of the sphere,
$D^n$, and identifying the points of its
boundary by the
antipodal map. In turn, this is equivalent to
taking the
$(n-1)$-sphere, which is the boundary of the
disk,
identifying antipodal points, and adding the
interior of the
$n$-disk. And the $(n-1)$-sphere with that
identification is
precisely $\mathbb{R} P^{n-1}$.

In summary, by induction,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{R} P^n
&=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}-\{0\}/x\sim\lambda x,
\qquad\lambda\neq0\\
&=S^n/x\sim-x\\
&=D^n/x\sim-x,
\qquad x\in\partial D^n=S^{n-1}\\
&=\mathbb{R} P^{n-1}\cup e^n\\
&=\mathbb{R} P^{n-2}\cup e^{n-1}\cup e^n\\
&=e^0\cup e^1\cup\ldots\cup e^n,
\end{aligned}
$$
where the attaching maps are exactly the
antipodal maps.
\item Complex projective space is the space
of
complex lines in $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$, and it
also has the quotient description
$S^{2n+1}/S^1$. Its cellular decomposition is
$$
\mathbb{C} P^n=e^0\cup e^2\cup\ldots e^{2n}.
$$
The top cell is attached by the Hopf map
$S^{2n-1}\to\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}$, not by an
antipodal identification.
\end{itemize}
\end{example}

\begin{exercise}
Every CW complex is semilocally simply
connected. That is,
every CW complex has a universal covering.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}
For each $n$-cell $e^n_\alpha$ of a CW
complex, we have the
\emph{characteristic map}, which is of the
form
$\Phi_\alpha:D^n_\alpha\to X^n$ and extends
the attaching map
$\varphi_\alpha$. It is a homeomorphism from
the interior of
$D^n_\alpha$ onto $e^n_\alpha$.
It is the composite of the inclusion with the
quotient map $q$.
% N02
$$
\xymatrix@C=2em{
D^n_\alpha \ar@{^{(}->}[r]^\iota
  \ar@/_1.2pc/[rr]_{\Phi_\alpha}
 & X^{n-1}\sqcup D^n_\alpha
   \ar[r]^q
 & X^n
}
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
Let $X$ be a CW complex. A \emph{CW
subcomplex} $Z$ is a
closed subspace which is also a union of
cells of $X$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
For a subcomplex $Z$,
\begin{itemize}
\item $Z\subseteq X$ is a good pair, meaning
$Z$ is a
deformation retract of a neighbourhood in
$X$;
\item $X/Z$, the space obtained by collapsing
$Z$ to a point,
is a CW complex.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Very important]
Let $X$ be a CW complex. The quotient by the
$(n-1)$-skeleton
is a wedge of spheres, as many as there are
$n$-cells of $X$. In symbols,
$$
X^n/X^{n-1}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in I_n}S^n,
$$
where $I_n$ is the set indexing the
$n$-cells.
\end{remark}

\label{section-cw-compl-relat-cw-compl}
\label{section-topol-produ-cw-compl}
\begin{theorem}
If $X$ and $Y$ are CW complexes with at most
countably many
cells, then $X\times Y$ is a CW complex.
For general CW complexes, the same cell
description uses the compactly generated
topology on the product.
\end{theorem}

Here, the dimension of the product of two
cells, say
$e^1_\alpha\times e^2_\beta$, is the sum of
the dimensions of
each one. In this example, we have a 3-cell.
\section{The cellular definition of CW
complexes}
\label{section-cellular-cw-definition}

Let us see how Lee defines CW complexes in
his book
\textit{Introduction to Topological
Manifolds}.

\begin{definition}
If $X$ is a nonempty topological space, a
\emph{cellular
decomposition} of $X$ is a partition
$\mathcal E$ of $X$ into
subspaces which are open cells, such that for
each
$e\in\mathcal E$ of dimension $n\geq1$ there
exists a
\emph{characteristic map} $\phi$ from a
closed $n$-cell $D$
to $X$, restricting to a homeomorphism from
$\operatorname{Int}D$ to
$e$, and sending $\partial D$ into the union
of cells in
$\mathcal E$ of dimension strictly less than
$n$.

A \emph{cellular complex} is a Hausdorff
space $X$ together
with a cellular decomposition.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Although the cells are homeomorphic to open
disks, they do
not have to be open subsets of $X$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
A \emph{CW complex} is a cellular complex
$(X,\mathcal E)$
such that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[\emph{C}] \emph{Closure finiteness.}
The closure of
each cell intersects only finitely many cells
of $X$.
\item[\emph{W}] \emph{Weak topology.} The
topology of $X$
is coherent with the family of closed
subspaces
$\{\bar e:e\in\mathcal E\}$, that is,
$U\subseteq X$ is closed
in $X$ if and only if $U\cap\bar e$ is closed
for every
$e\in\mathcal E$.
\end{enumerate}
An equivalence between this definition and
Hatcher's, when
$X$ is Hausdorff, is in Hatcher's appendix.
\end{definition}
\section{The singular homology of a CW
complex}
\label{section-singular-homology-cw}

\begin{lemma}
\begin{itemize}
\item If the $n$-cells are indexed by the set
$I_n$, then
the group $H_k(X^n,X^{n-1})$ vanishes for
$k\neq n$, while
$$
H_n(X^n,X^{n-1})
\cong\bigoplus_{\alpha\in I_n}R.
$$
\begin{proof}
By the quotient theorem and wedge axiom,
$$
\begin{aligned}
H_k(X^n,X^{n-1})
&\cong\tilde H_k(X^n/X^{n-1})\\
&\cong\tilde H_k
\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in I_n}S^n\right).
\end{aligned}
$$
\end{proof}
\item $H_k(X^n)=0$ for every $k>n$.

Again, homology sees only up to the dimension
of the space.
\item The inclusion $X^n\hookrightarrow X$
induces an isomorphism on $H_k$ for $k<n$
and a surjection on $H_n$.

Here the idea is that attaching cells of
dimension greater
than the homology we are computing does not
change homology.
That is, for $n>k$ one has $H_k(X\cup
D^n)=H_k(X)$.
\end{itemize}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Look at $(X^n,X^{n-1})$. The long exact
sequence of the pair contains the exact
fragment
% D50
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.2em{
H_{k+1}(X^n,X^{n-1}) \ar[r]
 & H_k(X^{n-1}) \ar[r]
 & H_k(X^n) \ar[r]
 & H_k(X^n,X^{n-1})
}
$$
Using item 1, if $k\neq n,n-1$, then
$H_k(X^{n-1})\cong H_k(X^n)$.

To prove (2), we have $k>n$, and then, fixing
$k$ and going
down one by one, $H_k(X^n)\cong H_k(X^0)$,
which is a
discrete space, and homology in positive
degree of discrete
spaces is zero. This finishes (2).

For the last claim, attaching an $m$-cell
changes no homology below degree $m-1$ and is
surjective in degree $m-1$. Iteration proves
the finite-dimensional case. In general,
singular chains have compact image and hence
lie in a finite subcomplex of a CW complex.
Passing to the filtered union of the skeleta
proves the same stabilization for $X$.
\end{proof}
\section{Cellular homology}
\label{section-cellular-homology}

Next we construct a new type of homology for
CW complexes.

\begin{definition}
Consider the maps obtained from the long
exact sequences of
the pairs of skeleta. Define $d_n$ as the
following composite.
% D51
$$
\xymatrix@C=2.2em@R=2.8em{
H_n(X^n,X^{n-1})
  \ar[rr]^{d_n}
  \ar[dr]_{\partial_n}
 & & H_{n-1}(X^{n-1},X^{n-2}) \\
 & H_{n-1}(X^{n-1})
   \ar[ur]_{j_{n-1}}
 &
}
$$
The maps satisfy $d_n d_{n+1}=0$. The
resulting \emph{cellular chain complex} is
denoted $C_\bullet^{\mathrm{CW}}(X;R)$.
Its \emph{cellular homology} is
$$
H_n^{\mathrm{CW}}(X;R)
=H_n(C_\bullet^{\mathrm{CW}}(X;R)).
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
$$
H_n^{\mathrm{CW}}(X;R)
\cong H_n(X;R)
$$
for every $n\geq0$.
\end{theorem}

The first depends on the cellular structure
of the space $X$,
but the second does not. Thus the homology of
a CW complex is
independent of the cellular structure.

\begin{remark}
\label{remark-cellular-chain-groups}
These groups $H_n(X^n,X^{n-1})$ are direct
sums of $R$, one
for each $n$-cell of $X$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Consequences of the
definition]
Take $X$ a CW complex and $R=\mathbb{Z}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $H_n(X)=0$ if $X$ has no $n$-cells.
\item Suppose $X$ has exactly $k$ $n$-cells.
Then $H_n(X)$ is
generated by at most $k$ elements, since
$H_n(X^n,X^{n-1})$ is free abelian on
$k$ elements. Its subgroup $\ker d_n$ is
free of rank at most $k$, and its quotient
$$
H_n(X)=\ker d_n/
\operatorname{im}d_{n+1}
$$
is generated by at most $k$ elements. The
quotient need not be free.
\item If $X$ has no cells in adjacent
dimensions, then
$H_n(X)$ is free for every $n$, with rank
equal to the number
of $n$-cells.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\section{A formula for the boundary
homomorphism}
\label{section-cellular-boundary-formula}

Now let us try to understand what $d$ is.
Consider the usual
map from the boundary sphere of an $n$-cell
to
$X^{n-1}$, followed by the quotient
$X^{n-1}\to X^{n-1}/X^{n-2}$ and
projection to the sphere corresponding to
$e^{n-1}_\beta$.
% D52
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.1em{
S^{n-1}_\alpha
  \ar[r]^{\varphi_\alpha}
  \ar@/_1.2pc/[rrr]_{\varphi_{\alpha\beta}}
 & X^{n-1} \ar[r]^q
 & \displaystyle
   \bigvee_{\gamma\in I_{n-1}}
   S^{n-1}_\gamma
   \ar[r]^{p_\beta}
 & S^{n-1}_\beta
}
$$
The composite $\varphi_{\alpha\beta}$ is a
map between spheres. Write its degree as
$d_{\alpha\beta}$.

\begin{theorem}
For $n\geq2$, the homomorphism $d_n$ sends
$$
e^n_\alpha\mapsto\sum
d_{\alpha\beta}e^{n-1}_\beta.
$$
\end{theorem}
\section{Cellular computations}
\label{section-cellular-computations}

\begin{example}
We compute the homology of
$X=\bigvee_{\alpha\in I}S^1_\alpha$ in two
different ways:
first using directly that $H_0(X)=R$, and
then using the
preceding theorem. Since there is only one
0-cell, for every
$\alpha$, $e^1_\alpha\mapsto x_0-x_0=0$, so
in fact
$d_1=0$. It follows that
$H_1=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in I}R$.
\end{example}

In fact, this is true in general:

\begin{lemma}
If $X$ is a complex with a single 0-cell,
then $d_1=0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{example}[The
torus]\label{example-torus-cellular}
We know that the torus has a CW decomposition
with one
0-cell, two 1-cells, and one 2-cell. This
gives the chain
complex
% D53
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^{d_2=0}
 & \mathbb{Z}^2 \ar[r]^{d_1=0}
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
The square model records the attaching word.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.42\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/%
torus-cell-structure
}
\end{center}

We know $d_1=0$ because there is only one
0-cell.

The boundary of the 2-cell, $S^1$, maps by
the attaching map
to the wedge of two circles, and
\emph{traverses each one
twice and in opposite directions}. The
quotient $X^1/X^0$
does nothing, since $X^0$ was already just
one point. In the
last step, when choosing one of the two
circles in the wedge,
we realize that our map traverses it
\emph{out and then
back}. This map is homotopic to a constant,
so it has degree
zero. Doing this for the two circles, we
conclude that
$d_2=0$. Hence
$H_1(T^2)=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}$ and
$H_2(T^2)=\mathbb{Z}$.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[Extra]
A compact connected surface can only be a
sphere, a torus, a
connected sum of tori, the projective plane,
or a connected
sum of projective planes.
\end{theorem}

\begin{example}[The torus of genus $g$]
It turns out that the double torus can be
seen as the
quotient of an octagon with identified edges.
In general, the
$g$-torus is the quotient of a $4g$-gon. The
final result is
$$
H_0(S_g)=\mathbb{Z},
\qquad
H_1(S_g)=\mathbb{Z}^{2g},
\qquad
H_2(S_g)=\mathbb{Z}.
$$
\end{example}

\begin{example}[The projective plane]
Recall the simplest decomposition of the
projective plane,
with one cell in each dimension. Here,
% D54
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^{d_2=2}
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^{d_1=0}
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.38\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/%
projective-plane-cell-structure
}
\end{center}
Formally, to discover how the attaching map
acts, we reason
using local degree. The map in the drawing
sends the northern
hemisphere, without its boundary, to the
circle on the right
without the point. The restriction of the map
to the northern
hemisphere helps decompose the function in
order to compute
the degree. Since the restriction is a
homeomorphism, the
local degree here is 1.

The same happens for the piece of the
function on the
southern hemisphere. The key observation is
that each of
these local functions is obtained from the
other by composing
with the antipodal map, which has degree $1$
on $S^1$. The
degree of the whole function is the sum of
the local degrees,
so the degree is 2. Since there is only one
cell in each
dimension, $d_2=2$. Hence
$$
H_2(\mathbb{R} P^2)=0,
\qquad
H_1(\mathbb{R} P^2)=\mathbb{Z}/2.
$$
\end{example}

\begin{example}[$\mathbb{R} P^n$]
We only have to generalize the preceding
example. The CW
decomposition of $\mathbb{R} P^n$ is
$$
\mathbb{R} P^n=e_0\cup e_1\cup\cdots\cup e_n.
$$
The diagram above becomes the corresponding
diagram involving
$S^{k-1}$, $\mathbb{R}P^{k-1}$, and the
quotient
$\mathbb{R}P^{k-1}/\mathbb{R}P^{k-2}$.
% D55
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.7em{
S^{k-1} \ar[r]^{\varphi_k}
  \ar@/_1.2pc/[rrr]_{\bar\varphi_k}
 & \mathbb{R}P^{k-1}
   \ar[r]^q
 & \mathbb{R}P^{k-1}/
   \mathbb{R}P^{k-2}
   \ar[r]^{\cong}
 & S^{k-1}
}
$$

The attaching maps $\varphi_\alpha$ are the
quotient
projection by the antipodal map on the
sphere, i.e. the
covering of the projective plane. These maps
are two-to-one,
and this makes the northern hemisphere of
$S^{k-1}$ map
homeomorphically to $S^{k-1}$ minus one
point, just as in
dimension 2.

As we said, the functions obtained by
restricting
$\bar\varphi$ to each hemisphere differ from
each other by the
antipodal map. When computing degree, we
obtain the formula
$$
\deg(\bar\varphi)=1+(-1)^k.
$$
Therefore
the differential $d_k$ is multiplication by
2 for even $k$ and is zero for odd $k$.
This gives the corresponding cellular chain
complexes for
$n$ odd and $n$ even.

For odd $n$, the complex begins as follows
and continues with alternating maps.
% D56
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.25em{
0 \ar[r]
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^0
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^2
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^0
 & \cdots \ar[r]^2
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^0
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
For even $n$, it begins with multiplication
by two and still alternates.
% D57
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.25em{
0 \ar[r]
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^2
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^0
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^2
 & \cdots \ar[r]^2
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^0
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
Thus
$$
H_0(\mathbb{R} P^n)=\mathbb{Z},
$$
$H_k(\mathbb{R} P^n)=\mathbb{Z}/2$ for odd
$k<n$, and $H_n(\mathbb{R} P^n)$ is $0$
if $n$ is even and $\mathbb{Z}$ if $n$ is
odd. All other positive-degree groups
vanish.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[First example where things
change if we change
rings]
Take $\mathbb{R} P^n$ and the ring
$R=\mathbb{Q}$. Now the map
multiplication by 2 sends $\mathbb{Q}$ onto
all of $\mathbb{Q}$, something
that did not happen with $\mathbb{Z}$. This
makes the homology
easy to read from the cellular complex.
% D58
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.25em{
0 \ar[r]
 & \mathbb{Q} \ar[r]^{d_n}
 & \mathbb{Q} \ar[r]^{d_{n-1}}
 & \cdots \ar[r]^2
 & \mathbb{Q} \ar[r]^0
 & \mathbb{Q} \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
The maps alternate between zero and
multiplication by two. Therefore
$$
H_0(\mathbb{R}P^n;\mathbb{Q})
\cong\mathbb{Q}.
$$
For positive degrees the homology vanishes,
except in odd top degree. When $n$ is odd,
$$
H_n(\mathbb{R}P^n;\mathbb{Q})
\cong\mathbb{Q}.
$$
\end{example}

\begin{example}
What if we take $R=\mathbb{Z}/2$?
Multiplication by 2 is the zero map,
so
$$
H_m(\mathbb{R} P^n,\mathbb{Z}/2)=\mathbb{Z}/2
\qquad 0\leq m\leq n.
$$
\end{example}

\begin{example}[The M\"obius band]
Take the following cellular decomposition of
the M\"obius band. The directions have been
chosen so that the identified vertical sides
represent the same oriented edge.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.38\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/%
mobius-band-cell-structure
}
\end{center}

It corresponds to the chain complex
% D59
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^{d_2}
 & \mathbb{Z}^3 \ar[r]^{d_1}
 & \mathbb{Z}^2 \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
In this case $d_1$ is not automatically zero
because we have
two 0-cells. However, we know that the band
is connected, so
$H_0\cong\mathbb{Z}$.

We can compute $d_1$ explicitly, because we
know that it
behaves exactly like the boundary in
simplicial homology.
According to the names in the diagram, we can
simply see that
$$
\begin{array}{ccc}
d_1(e^1_1)=e^0_2-e^0_1&
 d_1(e^1_2)=e^0_1-e^0_2&
 d_1(e^1_3)=e^0_2-e^0_1.
\end{array}
$$
Equivalently,
$$
\begin{array}{ccc}
(1,0,0)\mapsto(-1,1)&
(0,1,0)\mapsto(1,-1)&
(0,0,1)\mapsto(-1,1).
\end{array}
$$
Thus $\operatorname{im}d_1\cong\mathbb{Z}$,
as expected, and
$$
\ker d_1=
\langle(1,1,0),(0,1,1)\rangle
\cong\mathbb{Z}^2.
$$

To compute $d_2$, traverse the oriented
boundary of the square. The vertical sides
each contribute $e^1_1$, while the horizontal
sides contribute $e^1_2$ and $-e^1_3$.
Thus, up to reversing the orientation of the
2-cell,
$$
d_2(e^2)=2e^1_1+e^1_2-e^1_3,
$$
or $1\mapsto(2,1,-1)$. This is a
primitive element of $\ker d_1$. Hence
$\operatorname{im}d_2\cong\mathbb{Z}$ and
$\ker d_2=0$. Therefore
$$
H_1(M)\cong\mathbb{Z},
\qquad
H_2(M)=0.
$$
\end{example}



\begin{exercise}
\label{exercise-same-homology-not-homotopy}
\label{ejer:homol-homot}
Show that $S^1\times S^1$ and
$S^1\vee S^1\vee S^2$ have isomorphic
integral homology groups but are not homotopy
equivalent.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}[Solution]
Both cellular chain complexes have the form
% D64
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]^{d_2=0}
 & \mathbb{Z}^2 \ar[r]^0
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
For the torus this was computed above. In the
wedge, the boundary of the two-cell is
collapsed to the wedge point, so $d_2=0$.
Their fundamental groups are not isomorphic:
one is $\mathbb{Z}^2$, while the other is the
free group $\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{exercise}
Compute the homology of the space obtained
from
$S^2$ by identifying its north and south
poles.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}[Solution]
The cell structure used in the original notes
has one zero-cell, two one-cells, and two
two-cells.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.36\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/%
sphere-poles-identified
}
\end{center}

Its cellular complex is
% D65
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & \mathbb{Z}^2 \ar[r]^{d_2}
 & \mathbb{Z}^2 \ar[r]^{d_1=0}
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
The boundary coefficient is read from this
diagram.
% D66
$$
\xymatrix@C=2.1em@R=2.6em{
S^1_\alpha \ar[r]^{\varphi_\alpha}
  \ar@{-->}[d]_{\varphi_{\alpha\beta}}
 & X^1=S^1\vee S^1 \ar[d]^q \\
S^1_\beta
 & X^1/X^0=S^1\vee S^1
   \ar[l]^{p_\beta}
}
$$
For either two-cell and either one-cell, the
resulting map has degree one. Therefore
$$
\begin{aligned}
d_2:\mathbb{Z}^2
&\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^2,\\
(1,0)&\longmapsto(1,1),\\
(0,1)&\longmapsto(1,1).
\end{aligned}
$$
Its image and kernel are both isomorphic to
$\mathbb{Z}$. Hence $H_0$, $H_1$, and $H_2$
are each isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}$, while
$H_n=0$ for $n\geq3$.
\end{proof}

\begin{exercise}
Compute the homology of
$S^1\times(S^1\vee S^1)$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}[Solution]
The geometry and its product-cell structure
are
shown together.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=.78\textwidth]{
algebraic-topology-assets/%
product-circle-wedge
}
\end{center}

The cellular chain complex is
% D67
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & \mathbb{Z}^2 \ar[r]^{d_2=0}
 & \mathbb{Z}^3 \ar[r]^0
 & \mathbb{Z} \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
The attaching words are commutators, as in
the
torus calculation, so $d_2=0$. Therefore
$$
H_2(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}^2,
\qquad
H_1(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}^3,
\qquad
H_0(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}.
$$
All higher groups vanish.
\end{proof}
\section{Euler characteristic}
\label{section-euler-characteristic}

Let $Y$ be a finite CW complex, i.e. one with
finitely many
cells. In fact this property is equivalent to
$Y$ being
compact. Let $c_i$ be the number of
$i$-cells.
The alternating description is: number of
vertices, minus number of edges, plus number
of two-cells, and so on. The
\emph{Euler characteristic} is
$$
\chi(Y)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^ic_i.
$$

\begin{example}
\begin{itemize}
\item The Euler characteristic of $S^n$ is
zero for odd $n$ and two for even $n$.
\item $\chi(\bigvee_{i=1}^nS^1)=1-n$;
\item $\chi(S_g)=1-2g+1=2-2g$.
\end{itemize}
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}
Let $X,Y$ be finite CW complexes. If $X\simeq
Y$, then
$\chi(X)=\chi(Y)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proposition}
Let $Y$ be a finite CW complex. Then
$$
\chi(Y)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^i
\operatorname{rank}
H_i(Y;\mathbb{Z}).
$$
To understand what $\operatorname{rank} H_i$
is, note that the chain complex
is made of finitely generated abelian groups,
since $Y$ is
finite. Its homology groups are finitely
generated abelian groups. By the
classification
theorem for finitely generated abelian
groups, we can
decompose a group in the form
$$
A\cong\mathbb{Z}^r\oplus T,
$$
where $T$ is finite. The integer
$r=\operatorname{rank}A$ is the rank
appearing
in the proposition.
\end{proposition}

\begin{exercise}
Let
$$
\xymatrix{
0 \ar[r]
 & A \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
 & B \ar@{->>}[r]
 & C \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
be an exact sequence of finitely generated
abelian groups.
Then
$$
\operatorname{rank}B=
\operatorname{rank}A+
\operatorname{rank}C.
$$
\begin{proof}[Solution]
Tensor the sequence with $\mathbb{Q}$. Since
$\mathbb{Q}$ is a flat $\mathbb{Z}$-module,
the resulting sequence of finite-dimensional
$\mathbb{Q}$-vector spaces is exact.
Dimensions
are additive in a short exact sequence, and
$\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(A\otimes\mathbb{Q})
=\operatorname{rank}A$.
\end{proof}
\end{exercise}

Now let us prove the proposition.

\begin{proof}
Take the cellular chain complex
% D60
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.5em{
0 \ar[r]
 & C_k \ar[r]^{d_k}
 & C_{k-1} \ar[r]^{d_{k-1}}
 & \cdots \ar[r]^{d_2}
 & C_1 \ar[r]^{d_1}
 & C_0 \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
and recall that $Z_n=\ker d_n$,
$B_n=\operatorname{im}d_{n+1}$,
and $H_n=Z_n/B_n$. Thus we have the short
exact sequences
% D61
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.8em@R=2.3em{
0 \ar[r]
 & Z_n \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
 & C_n \ar@{->>}[r]^{d_n}
 & B_{n-1} \ar[r]
 & 0 \\
0 \ar[r]
 & B_n \ar@{^{(}->}[r]
 & Z_n \ar@{->>}[r]
 & H_n \ar[r]
 & 0
}
$$
Using the exercise, we have
$$
\operatorname{rank}C_n=
\operatorname{rank}Z_n+
\operatorname{rank}B_{n-1},
\qquad
\operatorname{rank}Z_n=
\operatorname{rank}B_n+
\operatorname{rank}H_n.
$$
Now first by definition and then using the
above,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\chi(Y)
&=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^i
\operatorname{rank}C_i\\
&=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^i
\big(\operatorname{rank}B_i
+\operatorname{rank}H_i
+\operatorname{rank}B_{i-1}\big)\\
&=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^i
\operatorname{rank}H_i.
\end{aligned}
$$
Here $B_{-1}=0$. The two boundary sums cancel
after shifting the index.
\end{proof}

And with this proposition the theorem above
follows easily.





\medskip
\noindent
{\bf Organization of these notes.}
These notes consolidate material from three courses
of lecture notes.
The first part covers fundamental groups, covering
spaces, homological algebra, and singular homology
(from the Mexican algebraic topology courses).
The second part covers cohomology, cup products,
the K\"unneth theorem, Poincar\'e duality, and
geometric topology including cobordism, handles,
and characteristic classes.
The third part develops homotopy theory: homotopy
groups, fibrations, cofibrations, model structures,
spectral sequences, and representability.
Some subsections are lecture-note fragments and
are left visibly marked rather than silently
completed.


\section{Covering spaces}
\label{section-covering-spaces}

\begin{proposition}[Unique lifting property]
\label{proposition-unique-lifting-property}
\cite{hat}, Proposition 1.34. Given a
covering space $p:\tilde{X} \to X$ and a
map $f:Y \to X$, if two lifts
$\tilde{f}_1,\tilde{f}_2:Y \to \tilde{X}$ of
$f$
agree at one point of $Y$ and $Y$ is
connected, then $\tilde{f}_1$ and
$\tilde{f}_2$ agree on all of $Y$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We aim to show that the set of points where
$\tilde{f}_1$ and $\tilde{f}_2$
agree is both open and closed in $Y$. Let $y
\in Y$ and consider an evenly
covered neighbourhood $U$ of $f(y)$, so that
$p^{-1}(U)$ is a disjoint union of
sets, each of which is homeomorphic to $U$
via $p$. Let $\tilde{U}_1$ and
$\tilde{U}_2$ be the sheets containing
$\tilde{f}_1(y)$ and $\tilde{f}_2(y)$,
respectively. By continuity of $\tilde{f}_1$
and $\tilde{f}_2$, the intersection
$\tilde{f}^{-1}_1(\tilde{U}_1) \cap
\tilde{f}_2^{-1}(\tilde{U}_2):=N$ is
 an open neighbourhood of $y$.

Since  $\tilde{U}_1$ and $\tilde{U}_2$ are
mapped homeomorphically to the set
$U \subset X$, we can only have that either
$\tilde{U}_1$ coincides
with $\tilde{U}_2$, or they are disjoint. If
they are disjoint, then the
neighbourhood $N$ shows that the set of
points where $\tilde{f}_1$ and
$\tilde{f}_2$ agree is closed; that's because
in this case they don't agree at a
point, so that we are on the complement of
the set where they agree, and we have
shown it's an open set (every point has a
neighbourhood contained in the set).

If, on the other hand, $\tilde{U}_1$ and
$\tilde{U}_2$ are the same, which
happens when $\tilde{f}_1(y)=\tilde{f}_2(y)$,
then $\tilde{f}_1$ and
$\tilde{f}_2$ agree throughout $N$, so that
we have shown that the set where
$\tilde{f}_1$ and $\tilde{f}_2$ coincide is
open.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Properly discontinuous
action]
\label{definition-prope-disco-actio}
See \cite{hat} p. 72. The action of a group
$G$ on a space $Y$ is called
{\it properly discontinuous} or {\it covering
space action} if each $y \in Y$
has a neighbourhood $U$ such that $g_1(U)
\cap g_2(U)\neq \emptyset$ for any
$g_1,g_2 \in G$ implies $g_1=g_2$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
\label{proposition-deck-trans-act-prope}
The action of deck transformations on a
covering space is a properly
discontinuous action.
\end{proposition}

\section{Definitions in homotopy theory}
\label{section-defin-homot-theor}

This is taken from \cite{TomDieck}.

\begin{itemize}
\item Let $X$ and $Y$ be topological spaces
and
 $f,g:X\to Y$ continuous maps.
An {\it homotopy} from $f$ to $g$ is a
continuous map
$$
H:X\times[0,1]\to Y,\qquad(x,t)\mapsto
H(x,t)=H_t(x)
$$
\noindent
such that $f(x)=H(x,0)$ and $g(x)=H(x,1)$ for
all $x\in X$.
We denote this situation by $f\simeq g$.
The homotopy relation $\simeq$ is an
equivalence relation
on the set of continuous maps $X\to Y$.
A homotopy of maps $H_t:X\to Y$ is called
{\it relative to $A\subset X$} if $H_t|_A$ is
constant.


\item Topological spaces and homotopy classes
of maps form a quotient category
of $\text{Top}$, the {\it homotopy category
$\text{hTop}$}, where composition
of homotopy classes is induced by composition
of representing maps. If $f:X\to
Y$ represents an isomorphism in
$\text{hTop}$, then $f$ is called a
{\it homotopy equivalence} or
{\it $\text{h}$-equivalence}. In explicit
terms this means $f:X\to
Y$ is a homotopy equivalence if there exists
$g:Y\to X$, a
{\it homotopy inverse of $f$}, such that $gf$
and $fg$ are both
homotopic to the identity. Spaces $X$ and $Y$
are called
{\it homotopy equivalent} or of the same {\it
homotopy
type} if there exists a homotopy equivalence
$X\to Y$. A space is
{\it contractible} if it is homotopy
equivalent to a point. A map
$f:X\to Y$ is {\it null homotopic} if it is
homotopic to a constant
map.

\end{itemize}

\section{n-th homotopy
group of a pointed space}
\label{section-n-th-homot-group-point-spac}

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-homotopy-group}
Let $(X,x_0)$ be a pointed topological space
and $s_0\in S^n$. The elements of
the {\it $n$-th homotopy group} are homotopy
classes of maps
$(S^n,s_0)\to (X,x_0)$. Equivalently, they
are homotopy classes of maps
$(I^n,\partial I^n)\to (X,x_0)$. (Homotopies
are required to preserve the base
points, $s_0\mapsto x_0$ or $\partial
I^n\mapsto x_0$.)

Also,
$$
\pi_n(X,*)=[(I^n,\partial
I^n),(X,\{*\})]\cong[I^n/\partial I^n,X]^0
$$
where $[X,Y]$ denotes the set of homotopy
classes $[f]$ of maps $[f]:X\to Y$.
\end{definition}

\section{n-th relative
homotopy group of
a pointed pair of spaces}
\label{section-relative-homotopy-group}

\noindent
Let $A$ be a subspace of $X$ and $x_0\in A$.
The elements of the {\it relative homotopy
group $\pi_n(X,A,x_0)$}
are homotopy classes of maps $(I^n,\partial
I^n,J^{n-1})\to (X,A,x_0)$
where $J^{n-1}$ is the union of all but one
face of $I^n$.
That is,
$$
\pi_{n+1}(X,A,*)=[(I^{n+1},\partial
I^{n+1},J^n),(X,A,x_0)].
$$

The elements of such a group are homotopy
classes of based maps
$D^n\to X$ which carry the boundary $S^{n-1}$
into $A$.
Two maps $f,g$ are called {\it homotopic
relative to $A$}
if they are homotopic by a
basepoint-preserving homotopy
 $F:D_n\times[0,1]\to X$ such that, for each
 $p$ in $S^{n-1}$ and $t$ in $[0,1]$,
the element $F(p,t)$ is in $A$. Ordinary
homotopy groups are
recovered for the case in which $A=\{x_0\}$.

\begin{remark}
\label{remark-relative-homotopy}
This construction is motivated by studying the kernel of the
map induced by inclusion
\[
i_*:\pi_n(A,x_0)\longrightarrow\pi_n(X,x_0).
\]
\emph{[The rest of this lecture remark was not recorded.]}
\end{remark}

\section{Commutativity of higher homotopy
groups}
\label{section-commu-highe-homot-group}

\begin{proposition}
\label{proposition-commu-highe-homot-group}
$\pi_n(X,x_0)$ is an abelian group for $n>1$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-h-space}
A based space $(X,e)$ is an \emph{H-space}
if there is a based multiplication
$$
\mu:X\times X\longrightarrow X
$$
whose restrictions to the two axes are
based-homotopic to the identity.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}
Let $X$ be an H-space. Show that the
operation induced by its multiplication on
$\pi_n(X)$ is the usual addition.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
Let $+$ denote concatenation and let
$\star$ be the operation induced by
$\mu$.  Both have the constant map as a
unit.  Since $\mu$ is a map of spaces,
the two operations satisfy
$$
(a+b)\star(c+d)
=
(a\star c)+(b\star d).
$$
The Eckmann--Hilton argument gives
$$
a\star b=a+b=b+a.
$$
Thus the H-space operation is the usual,
necessarily commutative, operation.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Each element of $\pi_0(X,x_0)$ represents
a path-connected component of $X$.  But
$\pi_0(X,x_0)$ has no group structure.
\end{remark}
\begin{proposition}
$\pi_n:\operatorname{Top}^*
\longrightarrow\operatorname{Grp}$
is a functor for $n\geq1$. That is,
it is well defined on objects and
arrows, sends identities to identities,
and is associative.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
Homotopy invariance: if
$\varphi,\psi:(X,x_0)\to(Y,y_0)$
are homotopic as based maps, then the
induced maps on homotopy groups agree.
In particular, a basepoint-preserving
homotopy equivalence induces
isomorphisms on all $\pi_n$.
\end{proposition}

The converse is false: $\mathbb{R}^2$ and
$S^2$ have the same fundamental group
but different second homology groups.

\section{Action of fundamental groupoid
\texorpdfstring{$\Pi_1(X)$}{Π₁(X)} of
\texorpdfstring{$X$}{X} on
\texorpdfstring{$\pi_{n}(X)$}{π₁(X)}}
\label{section-actio-funda-group}

\begin{itemize}
\item A {\it groupoid} is a small category in
which every morphism is an isomorphism.
\item The {\it fundamental groupoid}
$\Pi_{1}(X)$
is the category whose objects
are the points of  $X$ and
morphisms are homotopy classes of paths
joining two points.
Composition is defined doubling the speed.
\end{itemize}

The fundamental groupoid acts on homotopy
group functorially.

\section{Homotopy groups of a product of
spaces
are products of homotopy groups of factors}
\label{section-homot-group-produ-space}

The coordinate projections induce
$$
\pi_n(X\times Y,(x_0,y_0))
\cong
\pi_n(X,x_0)\times\pi_n(Y,y_0).
$$

\section{Higher homotopy groups of a covering
space
are the same as those of a base space}
\label{section-highe-homot-group-cover}

If $p:\widetilde X\to X$ is a covering,
then for every $n\geq2$ and compatible
basepoints,
$$
p_*:\pi_n(\widetilde X,\widetilde x_0)
\longrightarrow
\pi_n(X,x_0)
$$
is an isomorphism.

\begin{proof}
For $n\geq2$, every based map $S^n\to X$
lifts because $S^n$ is simply connected.
This proves surjectivity. If a based map
upstairs projects to a nullhomotopic map,
lift a based nullhomotopy. Uniqueness of
lifts keeps its initial map fixed,
proving injectivity.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
The homotopy groups of the torus for
$n\geq2$ are trivial because its
universal covering is $\mathbb{R}^2$,
which is contractible. More generally,
any space with a contractible universal
covering has trivial higher homotopy
groups.
\end{example}

\section{Long exact sequence of homotopy
groups associated to a pair}
\label{section-long-exact-seque-homot-grou}

For any pair $(X,A,x)$ we have a long exact
sequence
$$
\xymatrix@C=1.5em{
\pi_n(A,x_0)\ar[r]^{i_*}
& \pi_{n}(X,x_0)\ar[r]^{j_*}
& \pi_{n-1}(X,A,x_0)\ar[r]^{\partial}
& \pi_{n-1}(A,x_0)\ar[r]
& \cdots\ar[r]
& \pi_0(X,x_0)
}
$$
where $i$ and $j$ are the inclusions
$(A,x_0)\hookrightarrow(X,x_0)$ and
$(X,x_0,x_0)\hookrightarrow(X,A,x_0)$. The
map $\partial$ comes from restricting maps
$(I^n,\partial I^n,J^{n-1})\to (X,A,x_0)$ to
$I^{n-1}$, or by restricting maps
$(D^n,S^{n-1},s_0)\to (X,A,x_0)$. The map,
called the {\it boundary map}, is a
homomorphism when $n>1$.

\section{n-connected
space}
\label{section-n-connected-space}

A space $X$ with basepoint $x_0$ is called
{\it $n$-connected} if $\pi_i(X,x_0)=0$ for
$i\leq n$. Thus 0-connected means
path-connected and 1 connected means
simply-connected.

\section{n-connected
pair}
\label{section-n-connected-pair}

A pair $(X,A)$ is {\it $n$-connected} if
$\pi(X,A,x_0)=0$ for $i\leq n$.

\section{n-equivalence of spaces}
\label{section-n-equivalence-of-spaces}

A map $f:X\to Y$ is an {\it  $n$-equivalence}
if the induced map on homotopy groups
$f_{*}:\pi_{i}(X)\to \pi_{i}(Y)$ is an
isomorphism for $i<n$ and a surjection for
$i=n$.

\begin{remark}
\label{remark-what-i-picke-up-lectu-if}
\begin{itemize}
\item $(X,A)$ is $n$-connected if and only if
$A\hookrightarrow X$ is an $n$-equivalence.
\item $f:X\to Y$ is an $n$-equivalence if and
only if when we replace $f$ by a cofibration
$X\to M_{f}$, $(M_{f},X)$ is $n$-connected.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\section{Compactly generated spaces}
\label{section-compactly-generated-spaces}

(What I picked up from the lecture.) If $X$
is compactly generated, then $X$ is
weakly Hausdorff if the diagonal subset
$\Delta_X\subset X\times X$ is
$k$-closed.

Now some other notes from the lectures:

In $\text{CGWH}$, $\Hom(X,Y)$ is a space with
the compact-open topology.
This is a compactly generated space,
$\mathbf{k}(\Hom(X,Y))$.

(Also see
\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%
Currying#Function_spaces}{wiki
on currying})
$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Map}(X,Y):=\text{ the space of maps
}X\to Y.\\
\text{Map}(X\times
Y,Z)\cong\text{Map}(X,\text{Map}(Y,Z))\\
\Hom(X\times Y,Z)\cong
\Hom(X,\text{Map}(Y,Z))
\end{aligned}
$$
In the last line, product is product in
$\text{CGWH}$, not in $\text{Top}$.
The functor $-\times Y$ is left adjoint to
$\text{Map}(Y,-)$.

We don't care so much about $\text{Top}$. We
care much more about $\text{CGWH}$,
the full subcategory of $\mathit{Top}$ on
 {\it compactly generated weakly Hausdorff}
 spaces.

\begin{definition}[Is this right?]
\label{definition-is-this-right}
$X$ is {\it compactly generated} if, for any
subset $C\subset X$, and for all continuous
maps $f:K\to X$ from compact Hausdorff
spaces, $$\text{if } f^{-1}(C) \text{is
closed in }K\text{, then } C\text{ is
closed}.$$
\end{definition}
{\bf Claim.}
If $X$ is compactly generated, then $X$ is
weakly Hausdorff if the diagonal subset
$\Delta_X\subset X\times X$ is $k$-closed.


{\bf Explanation from the book of May}
The ordinary category of spaces
allows pathology that obstructs a clean
development of the foundations.
The homotopy and homology groups of spaces
are supported on compact subspaces, and
it turns out that if one assumes a separation
property
that is a little weaker
than the Hausdorff property,
then one can refine the point-set topology of
spaces to eliminate
such pathology without changing these
invariants.

One major source of point-set level pathology
can be passage to quotient spaces.
Use of compactly generated topologies
alleviates this.

\begin{proposition}
\label{proposition-if-x-compa-gener-x-y}
If $X$ is compactly generated and $\pi:X\to
Y$ is a quotient map,
then $Y$ is compactly generated if and only
if $(\pi\times \pi)^{-1}(\Delta Y)$
 is closed in $X\times X$.
\end{proposition}

\noindent
The interpretation is that a quotient space
of a
compactly generated space by a “closed
equivalence relation”
is compactly generated.

\subsubsection{Weakly Hausdorff space}
\label{subsection-weakly-housdorff-space}

\begin{exercise}
Show naturally that
$$
\operatorname{Map}(\{*\},X)\cong X.
$$
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
The product with a point is naturally
isomorphic to the identity. Its universal
property is displayed by
$$
\xymatrix{
&Y\times\{*\}\ar[dr]^{\pi_2}
 \ar[dl]_{\pi_1}\\
Y\ar@/^1pc/[rr]^{!}
 \ar@{-->}[ur]^{u}
&&\{*\}.
}
$$
The candidate inverse sends $y$ to $(y,*)$.
Uniqueness follows from the expanded diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
Y\times\{*\}\ar[r]^{\pi_1}
 \ar@/_1pc/[drr]_{\pi_2}
&Y\ar@{-->}[r]^{u}
 \ar@/^1pc/[rr]^{\operatorname{id}_Y}
&Y\times\{*\}\ar[r]^{\pi_1}
 \ar[d]_{\pi_2}
&Y\\
&&\{*\}\ar[r]_{\operatorname{id}}
&\{*\}.
}
$$
Currying now identifies maps from a point
with their values.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Cartesian closedness of the
category $CGWH$}
\label{subsection-carte-close-categ-cgwh}
See "Compactly generated spaces" by Charles
Rezk.

\section{Wedge}
\label{section-wedge}

\section{Smash product of spaces, its
associativity in
\texorpdfstring{$\text{CGWH}$}{CGWH}}
\label{section-smash-produ-space-its-assoc}

The appropriate analogue of the Cartesian
product in the category of based spaces is
the {\it smash product} $X\wedge Y$ defined
by
$$X\wedge Y=X\times Y/X\vee Y.$$
Here $X\vee Y$ is viewed as the subspace of
$X\times Y$ consisting of those pairs $(x,y)$
such that either $x$ is the basepoint of $X$
or $y$ is the basepoint of $Y$.

\begin{example}[Basic example]
\label{example-basic-example}
	The smash product of two circles is a
	sphere, as may be seen by collapsing the
	generating circles on a revolution torus
	(see
	\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%
	Smash_product#/media/File:
	Visualization_of_the_smash_product_of_%
	two_circles.gif}{Wikipedia}).
\end{example}

\section{Reduced and unreduced suspension}
\label{section-reduc-unred-suspe}
We also have the {\it suspension of pointed
spaces}, which is like usual suspension but
also collapsing the distinguished point,
which has become an interval:
$$\Sigma X=(I\times X)/(t,x)\sim (0,y)\sim
(1,y)\;\forall y\in X.$$

The \emph{cone} on $X$ is
$$
CX = X\times I\,/\,(x,1)\sim(y,1)
\quad\forall x,y\in X.
$$

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-suspension-map}
\label{fsusp}
For a map $f:X\to Y$, its
\emph{suspension} is the map
$\Sigma f:\Sigma X\to\Sigma Y$
induced by $f\times\mathrm{id}_I$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that the reduced suspension is
$S^1\wedge X$, and deduce
$\Sigma S^n\cong S^{n+1}$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
The smash $S^1\wedge X$ is
$(I\times X)/((\partial I\times X)\cup
(I\times\{x_0\}))$.
Identifying $\partial I$ to a point gives
the suspension.  Taking $X=S^n$ and using
$\Sigma S^n\cong S^{n+1}$ inductively
finishes the argument.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
The cone and suspension of a CW complex
are CW complexes.
\end{theorem}

\section{Path and loop spaces of a pointed
space}
\label{section-path-loop-space-point-space}
[This is defined in Hatcher close to the
homotopy fiber construction--- it's related
to Blakers-Massey, Freudenthal theorem.]

\begin{definition}[Path fibration]
\label{definition-path-fibration}
An important case of the homotopy fiber
construction
is when the given function $f$ is the
inclusion of the basepoint $b_0$ into $B$.
Then $E_{f}$ is the space $PB$ of paths in
$B$ starting at $b_{0}$,
and $p:PB\to B$ sends each path to its
endpoint.
The fiber $p^{-1}(b_{0})$ is the loopspace
$\Omega B$
consisting of all loops in  $B$ based at
$b_{0}$.
Since $PB$ is contractible by progressively
truncating paths,
the long exact sequence of homotopy groups
for the path
fibration yields (another proof that)
$$
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})\cong \pi_{n-1}(\Omega
X,x_{0})\qquad \forall n.
$$

\begin{exercise}
Show that $\operatorname{Map}(I,Y)$
strongly deformation retracts onto its
constant maps. Show also that evaluation
at zero
$$
\operatorname{ev}_0:
\operatorname{Map}(I,Y)
\longrightarrow Y
$$
is a Hurewicz fibration.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
The contraction is the map
$$
\begin{aligned}
h:\operatorname{Map}(I,Y)\times I
&\longrightarrow\operatorname{Map}(I,Y)\\
(f,t)&\longmapsto
 \bigl(s\mapsto f((1-t)s)\bigr).
\end{aligned}
$$
At $t=0$ this is the identity; at $t=1$
it sends every $f$ to the constant path
$f(0)$, which is the constant map at
$f(0)$.

For the lifting assertion, a square
$$
\xymatrix{
A\times\{0\}\ar@{^{(}->}[d]
 \ar[r]^{h_0}
&\operatorname{Map}(I,Y)\ar[d]^{\operatorname{ev}_0}\\
A\times I\ar[r]_{H}
 \ar@{-->}[ur]^{\widetilde H}
&Y
}
$$
after currying becomes an extension
problem over $A\times I\times I$
from data on
$(A\times\{0\})\times I$ and
$(A\times I)\times\{0\}$.
The square $I\times I$ retracts onto its
left and bottom edges by
$$
r(s,t)=
\begin{cases}
(0,t-s)&s\leq t,\\
(s-t,0)&t\leq s.
\end{cases}
$$
The prescribed maps agree at the corner,
so they glue; composing with $r$ gives
the required extension.
\end{proof}

\end{definition}

\section{For pointed spaces: smash with any
\texorpdfstring{$Y$}{Y}
is adjoint to
\texorpdfstring{$\text{Map}(Y,-)$}{Map(Y,-)}}
\label{section-point-space-smash-any-y}

[From
\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%
Smash_product#}{Wikipedia}]
Adjoint functors make the analogy between the
tensor product
and the smash product more
precise. In the category of $R$-modules over
a commutative ring $R$, the tensor
functor $(-\otimes_{A} A)$ is left adjoint to
the internal Hom functor
$\Hom(A,-)$, so that
$$
\Hom(X\otimes A,Y)\cong\Hom(X,\Hom(A,Y)).
$$

In the category of pointed spaces, the smash
product plays the role the tensor product in
this formula: if $A$, $X$ are compact
Hausdorff then we have an adjunction
$$\text{Maps}_{*}(X\wedge A,Y)\cong
\text{Maps}_{*}(X,\text{Maps}_{*}(A,Y))$$
where $\text{Maps}_{*}$ denotes continuous
maps that send basepoint to basepoint, and
$\text{Maps}_{*}(A,Y)$ carries the
compact-open topology.

\section{Adjointness of Sigma and Omega}
\label{section-adjointness-of-and}
In particular, taking $A$ to be the unit
circle $S^{1}$, we see that the reduced
suspension functor $\Sigma$ is left adjoint
to the loop space functor $\Omega$:
$$\text{Maps}_{*}(\Sigma X,Y)\cong
\text{Maps}_{*}(X,\Omega Y).$$

[From lectures:] Then we have
$$\Hom_{\text{CGWH}_*}(\Sigma X,\Sigma
X)\cong\Hom_{\text{CGWH}_*}(X,\Omega\Sigma
X)$$
where $\Sigma X=S\wedge X$ and $\Omega\Sigma
X=\text{Map}(S^1,\Sigma X)$. That is,
$S^1\wedge-$ is adjoint to
$\text{Map}(S^1,-)$.

\section{Hurewicz homomorphism}
\label{section-hurewicz-homomorphism}

\begin{definition}[Hurewicz homomorphism]
\label{definition-hurewicz-homomorphism}
$$
\begin{aligned}
h: \pi_{n}(X) &\longrightarrow H_{n}(X) \\
[f] &\longmapsto f_{*}(\alpha)
.\end{aligned}
$$
\end{definition}
where $\alpha$ is a generator of $
H_{n}(S^{n})$.

\section{Absolute and relative versions of
Hurewicz theorem}
\label{section-absol-relat-hurew-theor}

We begin with some algebraic background.
Let $G$ be a group and $g,h\in G$.  The
\emph{commutator} is
$$
[g,h]=ghg^{-1}h^{-1}.
$$
The \emph{derived} (or \emph{commutator})
\emph{subgroup} is
$$
G'=\langle[g,h]\mid g,h\in G\rangle
\leq G.
$$
Every endomorphism of $G$ restricts to an
endomorphism of $G'$, so $G'$ is
characteristic, hence normal.  The quotient
$$
G^{\mathrm{ab}}:=G/G'
$$
is called the \emph{abelianization} of $G$.

\begin{remark}
If $G$ is nonabelian and simple, then
$G'=G$, so its abelianization is trivial.
Cyclic groups of prime order are already
abelian, so their abelianization is the
group itself.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
Let $G$ be a group and $A$ an abelian group.
If $\varphi:G\to A$ is a homomorphism,
there exists a unique
$\bar\varphi:G^{\mathrm{ab}}\to A$
such that
$$
\xymatrix@C=2.5em@R=2.5em{
G \ar[r]^{\varphi}
  \ar[d]_{q}
 & A \\
G^{\mathrm{ab}}
  \ar@{-->}[ur]_{\bar\varphi}
 &
}
$$
commutes.
\end{proposition}

\begin{theorem}[Hurewicz]
\label{theorem-hurewicz-absolute}
If $X$ is $n$-connected for some $n \geq 2$,
them $\tilde{H}_i(X)=0$ for $i<n$
and $\pi_{n}(X)\cong H_n(X)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma-hurew-homom-gives-homot-equiv}
If $X$ and $Y$ are simply connected CW-spaces
and  $f:X \to Y$ induces
isomorphisms $H_n(X) \cong H_n(Y)$ for all
$n$, then it is an homotopy
equivalence.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Apply relative Hurewicz to the mapping
cylinder pair.  If a relative homotopy group
were first nonzero, it would map
isomorphically to a relative homology group
by Hurewicz, contradicting the homology
hypothesis.  The map is therefore a weak
equivalence, and Whitehead's theorem applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Hurewicz]
\label{theorem-hurewicz-relative}
If $(X,A)$ is $(n-1)$-connected, i.e.
$\pi_{n}(X,A)=0$ for $i \leq n-1$, for
some $n\geq 2$ and $A$ is simply connected,
then $H_i(X,A)=0$ for $i\leq n-1$
and $\pi_{n}(X,A)\cong H_n(X,A)$.
\end{theorem}
Perhaps $X$ should be $(n-1)$-connected for
the relative version too---
they are stated together in Hatcher.

\begin{exercise}
Show that the suspension of an acyclic CW
complex is contractible.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
An acyclic CW complex is connected.  Its
suspension is simply connected, and the
suspension isomorphism in reduced homology
shows $\widetilde H_i(\Sigma X;\mathbb{Z})=0$
for all $i$.  If $\Sigma X$ had a first
nonzero homotopy group, Hurewicz would
identify it with a nonzero homology group,
a contradiction.  Whitehead then applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
$$
H_1(S^1)\cong\mathbb{Z},
\qquad
H_1(S^1\times S^1)\cong\mathbb{Z}^2.
$$
\end{corollary}

\begin{exercise}
For $n>k>0$, prove that no retraction
$\mathbb{R}P^n\to\mathbb{R}P^k$ exists.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
A retraction gives a splitting of
$\pi_i(\mathbb{R}P^k)
\hookrightarrow\pi_i(\mathbb{R}P^n)$.
For $k\geq2$ take $i=k$: covering-space
invariance gives
$\pi_k(\mathbb{R}P^k)\cong\mathbb{Z}$
but $\pi_k(\mathbb{R}P^n)=0$, a
contradiction.  For $k=1$, the splitting
would produce a section of
$\mathbb{Z}/2\to0$, impossible.
\end{proof}

\begin{exercise}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a map of connected CW
complexes, suppose $f_*$ is an isomorphism
on $\pi_1$, and let
$\widetilde f:\widetilde X\to\widetilde Y$
be the lift to universal covers.  If
$\widetilde f$ is a homology isomorphism,
prove that $f$ is a homotopy equivalence.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
The covers are simply connected CW
complexes, so the homological Whitehead
lemma makes $\widetilde f$ a homotopy
equivalence.  Covering projections identify
all homotopy groups in degrees at least two.
With the hypothesis on $\pi_1$, this makes
$f$ a weak equivalence.  Whitehead's theorem
concludes.
\end{proof}
\begin{exercise}
Show that an $n$-connected,
$n$-dimensional CW complex is contractible.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
The map $X\to*$ induces isomorphisms on
$\pi_i$ for all $i\leq n$.
The dimensional form of Whitehead's theorem
makes it a homotopy equivalence.
\end{proof}

\begin{exercise}
Let $\simeq_w$ be the equivalence relation
generated by weak homotopy equivalences.
Show that $X\simeq_wY$ if and only if $X$
and $Y$ have a common CW approximation.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
A common CW approximation
$Q\xrightarrow{\sim}X$ and
$Q\xrightarrow{\sim}Y$
gives a zigzag immediately.
Conversely, choose functorial CW
approximations $QZ\to Z$. A weak
equivalence $Z\to Z'$ induces a weak
equivalence $QZ\to QZ'$ between CW
complexes, hence a homotopy equivalence by
Whitehead. Along a finite zigzag, replace
backward maps by homotopy inverses after
applying $Q$. Composing from $QX$
produces weak equivalences
$$
QX\longrightarrow X,
\qquad
QX\longrightarrow Y.
$$
Thus $QX$ is a common CW approximation.
\end{proof}

\begin{exercise}
A map between connected $n$-dimensional CW
complexes is a homotopy equivalence if it
induces isomorphisms on $\pi_i$ for
$i\leq n$. Prove this.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
Covering invariance gives the commutative
square
$$
\xymatrix{
\pi_i(\widetilde X)
 \ar[r]^{\widetilde f_*}
 \ar[d]_{p_*}^{\cong}
&\pi_i(\widetilde Y)
 \ar[d]^{q_*}_{\cong}\\
\pi_i(X)\ar[r]_{f_*}
&\pi_i(Y)
}
$$
for $i\geq2$. Obstruction theory constructs
a homotopy inverse skeleton by skeleton.
All obstructions through dimension $n$
vanish because the maps are isomorphisms
for $i\leq n$. There are no higher cells on
which another obstruction could arise.
Hence $\widetilde f$, and then $f$, is a
homotopy equivalence.
\end{proof}

\section{Weak factorization systems}
\label{section-weak-factorization-systems}

Here we follow \cite{Riehl}.

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-weak-factorization-system}
A {\it weak factorization system
$(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R})$} on a category
$\mathcal{M}$ is comprised of two clases of
morphisms $\mathcal{L}$
and $\mathcal{R}$ so that

\begin{enumerate}
\item Every morphism in $\mathcal{M}$ may be
factored as a morphism in $\mathcal{L}$
followed by a morphism in $\mathcal{R}$:
$$
\xymatrix{
\bullet\ar[rr]^{f}\ar[rd]_{\mathcal{L}
\ni\ell}
&& \bullet\\
& \bullet\ar[ru]_{r\in\mathcal{R}}
}
$$

\item The maps in $\mathcal{L}$ have the {\it
left lifting property} with respect to each
map in $\mathcal{R}$ and equivalently the
maps in $\mathcal{R}$ have the {\it right
lifting property} with respect to each map in
$\mathcal{L}$, that is, any commutative
square
$$
\xymatrix{
\bullet\ar[d]_{\mathcal{L}\ni\ell}\ar[r]
& \bullet\ar[d]^{r\in\mathcal{R}}\\
\bullet\ar@{-->}[ur]\ar[r]
& \bullet
}
$$
admits a diagonal filler as indicated making
both triangles commute.
When this lift is unique,
we say the factorization system is {\it
orthogonal}.

\item The classes $\mathcal{L}$ and
$\mathcal{R}$ are each closed under
retracts in the arrow category:
given a commutative diagram
$$
\xymatrix{
\bullet\ar[r]\ar[d]_{t}\ar@/^1pc/@{-}[rr]
\ar@/^2pc/@{-}[rr]
& \bullet\ar[r]\ar[d]^{s}
& \bullet\ar[d]^{t}\\
\bullet\ar[r]\ar@/_1pc/@{-}[rr]\ar@/_2pc/@{-}
[rr]
& \bullet\ar[r]
& \bullet
}
$$

\noindent
if $s$ is in that class then so is its
retract $t$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}[3.1.8, Riehl]
\label{exercise-3-1-8-rihel}
Verify that the class of morphisms
$\mathcal{L}$ characterized
by the left lifting property against a fixed
class of morphisms $\mathcal{R}$ is closed
under coproducts,
closed under retracts, and contains the
isomorphisms.
\end{exercise}

\section{Model category structure on a
category}
\label{section-model-category-structure}

\begin{definition}[Lecture]
\label{definition-lecture}
A {\it model structure} on a category
$\mathcal{A}$
is a choice of subcategories
$\mathcal{W},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}$
called {\it weak-equivalences}, {\it
cofibrations} and {\it fibrations}
with the following properties:

\begin{enumerate}
\item[0.] All (finite) small limits an
colimits.

\item \textbf{(2 of 3)} Given
$\bullet\overset{f}{\to}\bullet\overset{g}
{\to}\bullet$,
if either 2 out of 3 among $f,g,f\circ g$
are in $\mathcal{W}$ then all of them are.

\item
$(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{W},\mathcal{F})$
and
$(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{W})$
are both weak factorization systems.

$(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{D})$ is a weak
factorization system. That is,

\begin{enumerate}
\item Any morphism in $\mathcal{A}$ can be
factored
as a morphism in $\mathcal{B}$
followed by a morphism in $\mathcal{D}$.

\item Lifts:

$$
\xymatrix{
\bullet\ar[d]_{f}\ar[r]&\bullet\ar[d]^{g}\\
\bullet\ar@{-->}[ur]^{\exists}\ar[r]&\bullet
}
$$

\item[(c')] Notice that the axiom of retracts
is not necessary. $r'\in\mathcal{R}\iff$ it
satisfies (b) for all $\ell\in\mathcal{L}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{definition-item}
\begin{itemize}
\item $X$ is {\it fibrant} if
$X\to\text{pt}$.
\item $X$ is {\it cofibrant} if
$$
\xymatrix{
X\ar@{->>}[r]&X
}
$$
\item $X$ is {\it bifibrant} if
$$
\xymatrix{
0\ar@{^{(}->}[r]&X\ar@{->>}[r]&\text{pt}
}
$$
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\medskip\noindent
Two examples of model structures
are Str\o m and Quillen model structures.

\begin{example}[Two interesting model
category structures on $\text{CGWH}$]
\label{example-two-inter-model-categ-struc}
\begin{enumerate}
\item {\it Hurewicz-Str\o m model structure}.
\begin{itemize}
\item Cofibrations = Huerwicz cofibrations.
\item Fibrations = Hurewicz fibrations.
\item Weak equivalences = homotopy
equivalences.
\end{itemize}
\item {\it Quillen model structure}. Defined
on $\text{Top}$.
\begin{itemize}
\item Cofibrations = "retracts of relative
cell complexes".
\item Fibrations = Serre fibrations.
\item Weak equivalences = weak homotopy
equivalences.
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\subsection{Hurewicz fibrations and
cofibrations}
\label{subsection-hurew-fibra-cofib}

\section{Fibrations}
\label{section-fibrations}

\begin{definition}[Hatcher]
\label{definition-hatcher}
A map $p:E\to B$ is said to have the {\it
homotopy lifting property} with respect to a
space $X$ if, given a homotopy $g_t:X\to B$
and a map $\tilde{g}_0:X\to E$ lifting $g_0$,
so $p\tilde{g}_0=g_0$, then there exists a
homotopy $\tilde{g}_t:X\to E$ lifting $g_t$.

The {\it lift extension property for a pair
$(Z,A)$} asserts that every map $X\to B$ has
a lift $Z\to E$ extending a given lift
defined on the subspace $A\subset Z$. The
case $(Z,A)=(X\times I,X\times\{0\})$ is the
homotopy lifting property.

A {\it fibration} is a map $p:E\to B$ having
the homotopy property with respect to all
spaces $X$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[\href{https://ncatlab.org%
/nlab/show/lift}{nLab}]
\label{definition-https-ncatl-org-nlab}
	A morphism $i$ has the {\it left lifting
	property with respect to a morphism $p$} and
	$p$ has the {\it right lifting property with
	respect to $i$} if for each morphisms $f$
	and $g$, if the outer square in the
	following diagram commutes, there exists
	$\phi$ (I think not necessarily unique)
	completing the diagram:
	$$
	\xymatrix@R=2.5em{
		A\ar[r]^{f}\ar[d]_{i}&X\ar[d]^{p}\\
		B\ar[r]_{g}\ar@{-->}[ur]^{\phi}&Y
	}
	$$
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[\href{https://ncatlab.org%
/nlab/show/homotopy+lifting+property}{nLab}]
Let $C$ be a category with products and with
interval object $I$. A morphism $E\to B$ has
the {\it homotopy lifting property} if it has
the right lifting property with respect to
all morphisms of the form $(\text{id},0):Y\to
Y\times I$.
\label{definition-homot-lifti-prope-nlab}

	This means that for all commuting squares
	$$
	\xymatrix{
		Y\ar[r]^{f}\ar[d]&E\ar[d]^{p}\\
		Y\times I\ar[r]_{F}\ar@{-->}[ur]^{\sigma}&B
	}
	$$
	there exists a morphism $\sigma:Y\times I\to
	E$ such that both triangles in the former
	diagram commute.

	A {\it fibration} (also called {\it Hurewicz
	fibration}) is a mapping $p:E\to B$
	satisfying the homotopy lifting property for
	all spaces $X$.
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{Cofibrations}
\label{subsection-cofibrations}

\begin{definition}[Hatcher]
\label{definition-hatcher-2}
	If in the definition of a fibration as a map
	satisfying the homotopy lifting property we
	reverse the direction of the arrows, we
	obtain the dual notion of a {\it
	cofibration}. This is a map $i:A\to B$
	(which is $E\to B$ backwards) satisfying the
	following property: (we used to have an
	homotopy from some space $X$ into the base,
	now we have:) given an homotopy $g_{t}:A\to
	X$ and (a lift)  $\tilde{g}:B\to X$ such
	that $\tilde{g}_{0}i=g_{0}$, there exists a
	homotopy $\tilde{g}_{0}:B\to X$ such that
	$\tilde{g}_{t}i=g_{t}$.

	In the special case that $i$ is the
	inclusion of a subspace, this is the
	homotopy extension property, and the next
	proposition says that this is indeed the
	general case. So a cofibration is an
	inclusion satisfying the homotopy extension
	property.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[4H.1]
\label{proposition-4h-1}
	If $i:A\to B$ is a cofibration, then $i$ is
	injective, and in fact a homeomorphism onto
	its image.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}[From Hatcher p. 461]
\label{remark-from-hatcher-p-461}
The duality between subobjects and quotient
objects is clear for abelian groups,
where subobjects are kernels and quotient
objects cokernels. The strict
topological analog of a kernel is a fiber of
a fibration. Dually, the
topological analog of a cokernel is the {\it
cofiber} $B/A$ of a
cofibration $A\hookrightarrow B$. If we make
an arbitrary map  $f:A\to B$ into a
cofibration $A\hookrightarrow M_{f}$, the
cofiber is the mapping cone
$C_{f}=M_{f}/(A\times {0}$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[\href{https://%
en.wikipedia.org/wiki/
Homotopy_extension_property}{wiki}]
\label{definition-https-en-wikip-org-wiki}
	Let $X$ be a topological space and let
	$A\subset X$. We say that the pair $(X,A)$
	has the {\it homotopy extension property}
	if, given a homotopy $f_\bullet:A\to Y^I$
	and a map $\tilde{f}_0:X\to Y$ such that
		$$\tilde{f}_0\circ\iota=f_0$$\
		(so $\tilde{f}$ is the lift of $f_0:A\to
		Y$) then there exists an {\it extension} of
		$f_\bullet$ to a homotopy
		$\tilde{f}_\bullet:X\to Y^I$ such that
		$\tilde{f}_\bullet\circ\iota=f_\bullet$.

		That is,
		$$
		\xymatrix{
			A\ar[r]^{f_\bullet}\ar[d]_{\iota}
			&Y^I\ar[d]^{\pi_0}\\
			X\ar@{-->}[ur]^{\tilde{f}_\bullet}
			\ar[r]_{\tilde{f}_0}&Y
		}
		$$
		So there's some
		\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%
		Currying#Function_spaces}{currying}
		to make usual homotopies $f_\bullet:A\times
		I\to Y$ look like $f_\bullet:A\to Y^I$. As
		said in our lectures: ``a homotopy $X\times
		I\to Y$ is the same as a map $X\to
		\text{Map}(I,Y)$".

	A map $\iota:A\to X$ is a {\it cofibration}
	if it has the homotopy extension property
	with respect to all topological spaces  $Y$.
\end{definition}

\begin{exercise}
For an inclusion $A\hookrightarrow X$,
prove that the homotopy extension
property is equivalent to the existence
of a retraction
$$
X\times I
\longrightarrow
X\times\{0\}\cup A\times I.
$$
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
Let $T=X\times\{0\}\cup A\times I$.
If $(X,A)$ has the extension property,
apply it to the identity on $T$: the
adjoint lifting square
$$
\xymatrix{
A\ar[r]\ar[d]_{\iota}
&\operatorname{Map}(I,T)
 \ar[d]^{\operatorname{ev}_0}\\
X\ar[r]
 \ar@{-->}[ur]
&T
}
$$
uncurries to give a retraction $X\times
I\to T$.

Conversely, if a retraction $r$ exists,
a map on $X\times\{0\}$ and a homotopy
on $A\times I$ glue to $\varphi:T\to Y$;
then $\varphi\circ r$ is the required
extension.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Mapping cylinder}
\label{subsection-mapping-cylinder}

\begin{definition}[\href{https://ncatlab.org%
/nlab/show/mapping+cylinder}{nLab}]
\label{definition-https-ncatl-org-nlab-2}
Given a continuous map $f:X\to Y$ of
topological spaces, one can define its {\it
mapping cylinder} as a pushout
$$
\xymatrix{
		X\ar[r]^{f}\ar[d]&Y\ar[d]\\
		X\times
		I\ar[r]_{(\sigma_0)_*(f)}&\text{Cyl}(f)
	}
$$
	in $\text{Top}$, where $I=[0,1]$ and
	$\sigma:X\to X\times I$ is given by
	$x\mapsto(x,0)$.

	Set theoretically, the mapping cyllinder is
	usually represented as que quotient space
	$$\left(X\times I\coprod Y\right)\Big/\sim$$
	where $\sim$ is the smallest equivalence
	relation identifying $(x,0)\sim f(x)$ for
	all $x\in X$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[\href{https://%
en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mapping_cylinder}{wiki}
]
\label{definition-https-en-wikip-org-wik-2}
The {\it mapping cylinder} of a function $f$
between topological spaces $X$ and $Y$ is the
quotient
$$M_f=(([0,1]\times X)\amalg Y)\big/\sim$$
where $\amalg$ denotes disjoint union, and
$\sim$ is the equivalence relation generated
by
$$(0,x)\sim f(x)\text{ for each }x\in X.$$
{\it That is, the mapping cylinder $M_f$ is
obtained by gluing one end of $X\times[0,1]$
to $Y$ via the map $f$.} Notice that the
``top" of the cylinder $\{1\}\times X$ is
homeomorphic to $X$, while the ``bottom" is
the space $f(X)\subset Y$.

Looks the mapping cylinder is just deforming
$X$ to $Y$ putting $X$ inside $Y$ via $f$…
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[From homework]
\label{definition-from-homework}
	Let $f:X\to Y$ be a map. Let
	$M_f=X\times[0,1]\cup_fY$ be the {\it
	mapping cylinder of $f$}, i.e. the pushout
	of
	$X\overset{\cong}{\to}X\times\{0\}
	\hookrightarrow
	X\times[0,1]$ and of $f:X\times Y$.
\end{definition}

The map $f$ factors as
$X\xrightarrow{g}M_f\xrightarrow{r}Y$,
where $g(x)=[x,1]$ and
$r([x,t])=f(x)$, $r(y)=y$.

\begin{exercise}
Prove that $r:M_f\to Y$ is a
deformation retraction and that
$g:X\to M_f$ is a Hurewicz cofibration.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
The homotopy
$$
K([x,t],s)=[x,(1-s)t],
\quad K(y,s)=y
$$
deforms $M_f$ to $Y$ and fixes $Y$ at
every stage.

For the cofibration, a lifting problem
$$
\xymatrix{
X\ar[r]^{H^\sharp}\ar[d]_{g}
&\operatorname{Map}(I,Z)
 \ar[d]^{\operatorname{ev}_0}\\
M_f\ar[r]_{u}
 \ar@{-->}[ur]
&Z
}
$$
has prescribed data on $X\times I\times I$
occupying the bottom face and two vertical
faces. That three-face subspace retracts
from the cube, and the extension agrees on
the glued face from the $Y$ part of $M_f$.
Hence $g$ is a cofibration.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Serre fibrations}
\label{subsection-serre-fibrations}

\begin{definition}[Hatcher, p. 376]
\label{definition-hatcher-p-376}
		The map $p:E\to B$ is said to have the {\it
		homotopy lifting property for a pair}
		$(X,A)$ if each homotopy $f_t:X\to B$ lifts
		to a homotopy $\tilde{g}_t:X\to E$ starting
		with a given lift $\tilde{g}_0$ and
		extending a given lift $\tilde{g}_t:A\to
		E$. In other words, the homotopy lifting
		property for $(X,A)$ is the lift extension
		property for $(X\times I,X\times\{0\}\cup
		A\times I)$.

	The important observation here is that
	homotopy lifting property for disks is
	equivalent to the homotopy lifting property
	for all CW pairs $(X,A)$. A map $p:E\to B$
	satisfying the homotopy lifting property for
	disks is sometimes called a {\it Serre
	fibration}.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Fiber bundles}
\label{subsection-fiber-bundles}

Here I include some more notes of the
discussion following Serre fibrations on
Hatcher.

	A {\it fiber bundle} structure on a space
	$E$, with fiber $F$, consists of a
	projection map $p:E\to B$ such that each
	point $B$ has a neighbourhood $U$ for which
	there is a homeomorphism $h:p^{-1}(U)\to
	U\times F$ making the following diagram
	commute
	$$
	\xymatrix{
		p^{-1}(U)\ar[rr]^{h}\ar[dr]_{p}
		&&U\times F\ar[dl]\\
		&U
	}
	$$
\begin{example}
\label{example-proje-space-yield-inter}
Projective spaces yield interesting fiber
bundles. In the real case we have the
familiar covering spaces $S^n\to\mathbb{R}
P^n$ with fiber $S^0$. Over the complex
numbers the analog of this is a fiber bundle
$S^1\to S^{2n+1}\to\mathbb{C} P^n$. Here
$S^{2n+1}$ is the unit sphere in
$\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ and $\mathbb{C} P^n$ is
viewed as the quotient space of $S^{2n+1}$
under the equivalence relation
$(z_0,\ldots,z_n)\sim\lambda(z_0,\ldots,z_n)$
for $\lambda\in S^1$. The projection
$p:S^{2n+1}\to\mathbb{C} P^n$ sends
$(z_0,\ldots,z_n)$ to its equivalence class
$[z_0,\ldots,z_n]$.

To see that the local triviality condition
for fibre bundles is satisfied, …

	The constructino of the bundle $S^1\to
	S^{2n+1}\to\mathbb{C} P^n$ also works when
	$n=\infty$, so there is a fiber bundle
	$S^1\to S^\infty\to \mathbb{C} P^\infty$.

	The case $n=1$ is particularly interesting
	since $\mathbb{C} P^1=S^2$ and bundle
	becomes $S^1\to S^3\to S^2$ with fiber,
	total space, and base all speres. This is
	known as the {\it Hopf bundle}. The
	projection $S^3\to S^2$ can be taken to be
	$(z_0,z_1)\mapsto
	z_0/z_1\in\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}=S^2$.
	(That is, seeing $S^2$ as the one-point
	compactification of $\mathbb{C}$.)

	In polar coordinates we may see $S^3$ as the
	union of several tori. Stereorgraphic
	projection yields the following figure:
	% Figure omitted: hopf-bundle.
	The limiting cases $T_0$ and $T_\infty$
	correspond to the unit circle in the
	$xy$-plane and the $z$-axis under the
	stereographic projection.	Each torus
	$T_\rho$ is aunion of circle fibers. These
	fiber circles have slope 1 on the torus,
	winding around once longitudinally and once
	meridionally. As $\rho$ goes to $0$ or
	$\infty$ the fiber circles approach the
	circles $T_0$ and $T_\infty$, which are also
	fibers. The figure below shows four tori
	decomposed into fibers:
	% Figure omitted: hopf-bundle2.

	Replacing the field $\mathbb{C}$ by the
	quaternions $\mathbb{H}$, the same
	constructions yield fiber bundles $S^3\to
	S^{4n+3}\to\mathbb{H} P^{n}$ over
	quaternionic projective spaces $\mathbb{H}
	P^n$. Here the fiber $S^3$ is the unit
