\mathbb{Z}[a,b]/
(2a,a^{m+1},ab,b^2),
$$
where $|a|=2$ and $|b|=2m+1$.

\noindent
For $m>0$, the reduced integral cohomology
ring of
$$
P^{2m}\vee S^{2m+1}
$$
is the product of the reduced rings of the
two summands.  It is isomorphic to the
reduced integral cohomology ring of
$P^{2m+1}$.  With mod-two coefficients the
underlying groups still agree, but the
rings do not: the top power in projective
space is nonzero, while products between
wedge summands vanish.

\begin{theorem}[Hopf]
\label{at-theorem-hopf-division-dimension}
If $A$ is a finite-dimensional division
algebra over $\mathbb{R}$, then its real
dimension is a power of two.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose $A\cong\mathbb{R}^n$.  Multiplying
on either side by a nonzero element is a
linear isomorphism.  Multiplication
therefore induces
$$
\varphi:P^{n-1}\times P^{n-1}
\longrightarrow P^{n-1}
$$
whose restriction to either factor is a
homeomorphism.  With mod-two coefficients,
$$
\varphi^*:
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})[x]/(x^n)
\longrightarrow
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})[x_1,x_2]/
(x_1^n,x_2^n).
$$
Restriction to the two factors shows that
$$
\varphi^*(x)=x_1+x_2.
$$
For example, the first restriction is the
commutative triangle
$$
\xymatrix{
H^*(P^{n-1}\times P^{n-1})
  \ar[d]_{i_1^*}
  & H^*(P^{n-1})
    \ar[l]_-{\varphi^*}
    \ar[ld]^\cong \\
H^*(P^{n-1}).
}
$$
It follows that
$$
0=\varphi^*(x^n)=(x_1+x_2)^n.
$$
Since $x_1^n=x_2^n=0$, every interior
binomial coefficient of order $n$ is even.
Write the binary expansion as
$$
n=2^{r_1}+\cdots+2^{r_s}.
$$
Over $\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$,
$$
(x_1+x_2)^n
=
\prod_{q=1}^s
(x_1^{2^{r_q}}+x_2^{2^{r_q}}).
$$
If $s>1$, selecting a nonempty proper
subset of factors produces a nonzero
interior monomial, a contradiction.  Hence
$s=1$ and $n$ is a power of two.
\end{proof}
\section{Poincare duality}
\label{section-poincare-duality}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-topological-manifold}
An \emph{$n$-dimensional topological
manifold} is a Hausdorff,
second-countable space that is locally
homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^n$.  In this
section manifolds have no boundary.  Such a
manifold is \emph{closed} when it is
compact.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Poincare duality]
\label{at-theorem-poincare-integral}
Let $M$ be an oriented $n$-manifold.
Suppose that $M$ is connected and closed.
Then, for every $k$,
$$
H_k(M;\mathbb{Z})
\cong H^{n-k}(M;\mathbb{Z}).
$$
Every closed manifold, orientable or not,
satisfies
$$
H_k(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
\cong
H^{n-k}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}).
$$
\end{theorem}

\noindent
The precise orientation data will be
developed below.  Tensoring the integral
isomorphism with $\mathbb{Q}$, and then
using universal coefficients over a field,
gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
H_k(M;\mathbb{Q})
&\cong H_k(M;\mathbb{Z})
  \otimes\mathbb{Q} \\
&\cong H^{n-k}(M;\mathbb{Z})
  \otimes\mathbb{Q} \\
&\cong H^{n-k}(M;\mathbb{Q}) \\
&\cong H_{n-k}(M;\mathbb{Q}).
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus the free ranks are symmetric.  The
integral theorem and universal coefficients
also identify the torsion subgroup of
$H_k(M;\mathbb{Z})$ with that of
$H_{n-k-1}(M;\mathbb{Z})$.

\noindent
Let $x\in M$, and choose a coordinate
neighborhood $U$ of $x$.  Excision and the
long exact sequence of a pair give
$$
\begin{aligned}
H_n(M,M-\{x\};\mathbb{Z})
&\cong H_n(U,U-\{x\};\mathbb{Z}) \\
&\cong H_n(\mathbb{R}^n,
  \mathbb{R}^n-\{0\};\mathbb{Z}) \\
&\cong \widetilde H_{n-1}
  (\mathbb{R}^n-\{0\};\mathbb{Z}) \\
&\cong \widetilde H_{n-1}
  (S^{n-1};\mathbb{Z}) \\
&\cong\mathbb{Z}.
\end{aligned}
$$
This group is the local homology of $M$ at
$x$.

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-manifold-orientation}
An \emph{orientation of $\mathbb{R}^n$}
is a choice of generator of
$$
H_n(\mathbb{R}^n,
\mathbb{R}^n-\{0\};\mathbb{Z}).
$$
A \emph{local orientation} of $M$ at $x$
is a generator
$$
\mu_x\in
H_n(M,M-\{x\};\mathbb{Z}).
$$

An \emph{orientation of $M$} is a choice
of one local orientation $\mu_x$ at every
$x\in M$, locally compatible as follows.
For each sufficiently small coordinate ball
$B$, there must be one generator
$$
\mu_B\in H_n(M,M-B;\mathbb{Z})
$$
whose image at every $y\in B$ is $\mu_y$.
The relevant inclusion-induced maps are
$$
\xymatrix{
H_n(M,M-B;\mathbb{Z})
  \ar[r]^\cong \ar[d]_\cong
  & H_n(M,M-\{x\};\mathbb{Z}) \\
H_n(M,M-\{y\};\mathbb{Z}).
}
$$
The manifold is \emph{orientable} if it
admits an orientation.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-two-orientations}
A connected orientable manifold has exactly
two integral orientations.  Once the local
generator at one point is chosen,
compatibility determines all the others;
the two choices differ by sign.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
\label{at-example-orientable-manifolds}
Every sphere $S^n$ is orientable.  The real
projective space $\mathbb{R}P^{2m}$ is not
orientable for $m\geq 1$, while
$\mathbb{R}P^{2m+1}$ is orientable.  The
Klein bottle is not orientable.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-orientation-cover}
Every manifold $M$ has a canonical
two-sheeted covering
$$
p:\widetilde M\longrightarrow M
$$
whose total space is orientable.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Construction]
The fiber over $x$ consists of the two
generators of the local homology group:
$$
\widetilde M
=
\bigl\{(x,\mu)\mid
H_n(M,M-\{x\};\mathbb{Z})
=\mathbb{Z}\mu\bigr\}.
$$
The projection sends $(x,\mu)$ to $x$.
Over a small coordinate ball, continuation
of either local generator gives one sheet.
These sheets define the topology and show
that $p$ is a two-sheeted covering.  On the
total space, choose at $(x,\mu)$ the local
orientation represented by $\mu$.  This
choice varies compatibly, so
$\widetilde M$ is orientable.  For the
Mobius band, the construction unwinds the
twist and produces a cylinder.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-orientation-section}
An orientation of $M$ is precisely a
continuous section of its orientation
cover $p:\widetilde M\to M$.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}
\label{at-lemma-cover-components}
If $M$ is connected, then $M$ is
orientable if and only if $\widetilde M$
has two connected components.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
An orientation gives a section, whose image
is one component; the opposite orientation
gives the other.  Conversely, if the
two-sheeted cover has two components, each
component maps homeomorphically to $M$ and
therefore supplies a section.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{at-lemma-no-index-two}
Let $M$ be connected.  If $\pi_1(M)$ has
no subgroup of index two, then $M$ is
orientable.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If $M$ were nonorientable, its orientation
cover would be connected.  Covering-space
theory would identify
$\pi_1(\widetilde M)$ with an index-two
subgroup of $\pi_1(M)$.
\end{proof}

\noindent
The orientation cover sits inside a larger
local-homology covering.  Its total space is
$$
M_{\mathbb{Z}}
=
\coprod_{x\in M}
H_n(M,M-\{x\};\mathbb{Z}),
$$
with the topology defined by continuation
over coordinate balls.  The projection
$q$ sends each local class over $x$ to
$x$.  The zero classes form a copy of $M$,
and the classes of absolute value $r$ form
a copy of $\widetilde M$ for each $r>0$.
Thus
$$
M_{\mathbb{Z}}
\cong M\sqcup
\coprod_{r\geq 1}\widetilde M.
$$
The maps fit into
$$
\xymatrix{
M_{\mathbb{Z}}\ar[dd]_q \\
&\widetilde M
  \ar@{^{(}->}[ul]
  \ar[dl]^p \\
M.
}
$$

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-ring-orientation}
Let $R$ be a commutative ring with unit.
An \emph{$R$-orientation} chooses at each
$x$ a generator of the rank-one
$R$-module
$$
H_n(M,M-\{x\};R),
$$
with the same local compatibility as above.
The manifold is \emph{$R$-orientable} if
such a choice exists.
\end{definition}

\noindent
The generators in a local fiber correspond
to the units $R^\times$.  When this group is
finite, their bundle is an
$|R^\times|$-sheeted covering, and an
$R$-orientation is a section.  In
$\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$ there is only one
unit, so every manifold is orientable with
these coefficients.  More generally, every
manifold is $R$-orientable when $2=0$ in
$R$.  The weaker condition that some
nonzero element is killed by two is not
enough: a local orientation must be a
generator, hence a unit.  An integral
orientation induces an $R$-orientation for
every coefficient ring $R$.

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-top-manifold-homology}
Let $M$ be a connected, closed
$n$-manifold.  For every $x\in M$, the map
$$
H_n(M;R)\longrightarrow
H_n(M,M-\{x\};R)
$$
is injective.  If $M$ is $R$-orientable,
it is an isomorphism.  If it is not
$R$-orientable, its image is
$$
\{r\in R\mid 2r=0\}
\subseteq H_n(M,M-\{x\};R)
\cong R.
$$
Moreover,
$$
H_j(M;R)=0
\qquad (j>n).
$$
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Local classes continue along paths.  The
standard local-to-global argument for top
homology identifies the image of the map
with the classes fixed by continuation
around every loop, and also proves
injectivity.  The orientation monodromy of
a loop is either $1$ or $-1$.  In the
orientable case all loops act trivially.  In
the nonorientable case some loop acts by
$-1$, so its fixed classes are exactly
those satisfying $r=-r$.  Local homology
vanishes above degree $n$; the same
local-to-global argument, applied to a
finite coordinate-ball cover, gives the
last assertion.
\end{proof}

This is \cite[Theorem 3.26]{hat}.

\begin{lemma}
If $M$ is an $n$-manifold, then
$H_i(M;R)=0$ for $i>n$. This follows
componentwise from the closed and noncompact
cases.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proposition}
Let $M$ be a connected noncompact
$n$-manifold. Then
$$
H_i(M;R)=0
$$
for every $i\geq n$.
\end{proposition}

This is \cite[Proposition 3.29]{hat}.

\begin{remark}[Question]
\label{at-remark-local-determinant}
How does local homology relate to the line
$\bigwedge^n T_x^*M$ of a smooth manifold?
The integral local homology group is the
orientation line: choosing one of its two
generators chooses a sign.  A nonzero
element of $\bigwedge^n T_x^*M$ also
chooses a sign after one passes to its two
possible orientations.  These choices
correspond, but the abelian group
$H_n(M,M-\{x\};\mathbb{Z})$ is not
literally the real vector space
$\bigwedge^n T_x^*M$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-fundamental-class}
Suppose $M$ is $R$-oriented.  A
\emph{fundamental class} is an element
$$
[M]\in H_n(M;R)
$$
whose image in every local group
$H_n(M,M-\{x\};R)$ is the chosen local
generator.
\end{definition}

\noindent
The preceding theorem gives a fundamental
class for every $R$-orientation.  Conversely,
such a class gives compatible local
generators and hence an orientation.  If
$B$ is a coordinate ball containing $x$,
the compatibility is visible in
$$
\xymatrix{
H_n(M;R)
  \ar[r]\ar[dr]
  &H_n(M,M-\{x\};R) \\
&H_n(M,M-B;R)\ar[u]^\cong.
}
$$

\begin{theorem}[Poincare duality]
\label{at-theorem-poincare-ring}
Let $M$ be a connected, closed,
$R$-oriented $n$-manifold.  Then
$$
H^k(M;R)\cong H_{n-k}(M;R)
$$
for every integer $k$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Cellular picture]
For a triangulated manifold, a cell of
dimension $k$ has a dual cell of dimension
$n-k$.  With orientations chosen
compatibly, the incidence numbers for dual
cells are the transposes of the original
ones, up to the conventional signs.  Thus
the cochain complex of the original cell
structure identifies with the chain
complex of the dual structure, with degrees
reversed.  The source notes record the
degree reversal as
$$
\xymatrix@R=.8em{
C&C^* \\
0\ar[r]&n \\
1\ar[r]&n-1 \\
\vdots&\vdots \\
n\ar[r]&0.
}
$$
Passing to homology gives the displayed
isomorphism.  The cap-product formulation
below gives the intrinsic map and extends
beyond this cellular picture.
\end{proof}
\section{Cap products}
\label{section-cap-products}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-cap-product}
Let $X$ be a space and $R$ a commutative
ring.  The \emph{cap product} is the
bilinear operation
$$
\frown:
C_k(X;R)\times C^\ell(X;R)
\longrightarrow C_{k-\ell}(X;R).
$$
For a singular simplex
$\sigma:[v_0,\ldots,v_k]\to X$, set
$$
\sigma\frown\varphi
=
\varphi
(\sigma|[v_0,\ldots,v_\ell])
\,\sigma|[v_\ell,\ldots,v_k],
$$
and extend $R$-bilinearly.
\end{definition}

\noindent
The boundary and coboundary satisfy
$$
\partial(\sigma\frown\varphi)
=(-1)^\ell
\bigl(
\partial\sigma\frown\varphi
-\sigma\frown\delta\varphi
\bigr).
$$
Consequently a cycle capped with a cocycle
is a cycle.  If either representative is
changed by a boundary or coboundary, the
result changes by a boundary.  Thus cap
product descends to
$$
\frown:
H_k(X;R)\times H^\ell(X;R)
\longrightarrow H_{k-\ell}(X;R).
$$

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-poincare-cap}
Let $M$ be a connected, closed,
$R$-oriented $n$-manifold.  Capping with
its fundamental class gives an isomorphism
$$
[M]\frown\mathord{-}:
H^k(M;R)\longrightarrow H_{n-k}(M;R)
$$
for every $k$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-cup-cap-adjunction}
For $\alpha\in C_{k+\ell}(X;R)$,
$\varphi\in C^k(X;R)$, and
$\psi\in C^\ell(X;R)$,
$$
\psi(\alpha\frown\varphi)
=(\varphi\smile\psi)(\alpha).
$$
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Both sides evaluate $\varphi$ on the front
$k$-face, evaluate $\psi$ on the remaining
$\ell$-face, and multiply the two values.
\end{proof}

\noindent
In particular, the two chain maps
$$
\begin{aligned}
\varphi\smile\mathord{-}:
C^\ell(X;R)
&\longrightarrow C^{k+\ell}(X;R),\\
\mathord{-}\frown\varphi:
C_{k+\ell}(X;R)
&\longrightarrow C_\ell(X;R)
\end{aligned}
$$
are dual.  On homology, the universal
coefficient evaluation maps fit into the
commutative square
$$
\xymatrix@C=3.2em{
H^\ell(X;R)
  \ar[d]_{\varphi\smile\mathord{-}}
  \ar[r]^h
  &\operatorname{Hom}_R(H_\ell(X;R),R)
  \ar[d]^{
  (\mathord{-}\frown\varphi)^*} \\
H^{k+\ell}(X;R)
  \ar[r]^h
  &\operatorname{Hom}_R
  (H_{k+\ell}(X;R),R).
}
$$
Over a field the maps $h$ are
isomorphisms.  With integral coefficients
they are surjective, and $h$ in degree $r$
is an isomorphism when
$H_{r-1}(X;\mathbb{Z})$ is free abelian,
for example when it is finitely generated
and torsion-free.
\section{Consequences of duality}
\label{section-duality-consequences}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-nonsingular-pairing}
Let $A$ and $B$ be $R$-modules.  A
bilinear pairing
$$
\lambda:A\times B\longrightarrow R
$$
is \emph{nonsingular} if both adjoint maps
$$
\begin{aligned}
A&\longrightarrow
\operatorname{Hom}_R(B,R),\\
a&\longmapsto
\bigl(b\longmapsto\lambda(a,b)\bigr),
\end{aligned}
$$
and
$$
\begin{aligned}
B&\longrightarrow
\operatorname{Hom}_R(A,R),\\
b&\longmapsto
\bigl(a\longmapsto\lambda(a,b)\bigr)
\end{aligned}
$$
are isomorphisms.
\end{definition}

\noindent
For a closed $R$-oriented $n$-manifold,
Poincare duality gives the intersection
pairing
$$
\begin{aligned}
H^k(M;R)\times H^{n-k}(M;R)
&\longrightarrow R,\\
(\varphi,\psi)
&\longmapsto
(\varphi\smile\psi)([M]).
\end{aligned}
$$
It is independent of the chosen cocycle
representatives.

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-duality-pairing}
The intersection pairing is nonsingular if
$R$ is a field.  For $R=\mathbb{Z}$, it
induces a nonsingular pairing after the
torsion subgroups of both cohomology groups
are factored out.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let
$$
D=[M]\frown\mathord{-}:
H^k(M;R)\longrightarrow H_{n-k}(M;R).
$$
In the field case, both arrows in
$$
\xymatrix@C=2.6em{
H^{n-k}(M;R)\ar[r]^h
  &\operatorname{Hom}_R
  (H_{n-k}(M;R),R)
  \ar[r]^{D^*}
  &\operatorname{Hom}_R
  (H^k(M;R),R)
}
$$
are isomorphisms.  The composite sends
$\psi$ to the functional
$$
\varphi\longmapsto
\psi([M]\frown\varphi)
=(\varphi\smile\psi)([M]),
$$
so it is the second adjoint of the
intersection pairing.  The first adjoint
is handled in the same way, or by graded
commutativity.

For integral coefficients, the kernel of
the evaluation map $h$ is the torsion
subgroup supplied by the universal
coefficient theorem.  Poincare duality
preserves torsion.  The same argument
therefore becomes an isomorphism after
passing to torsion-free quotients.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{at-lemma-primitive-dual-class}
Assume the hypotheses of the proposition.
Let $\alpha$ be a class in $H^k(M;R)$.
It generates a rank-one direct summand if
and only if there is a class
$\beta\in H^{n-k}(M;R)$ for which
$$
(\alpha\smile\beta)([M])
$$
is a unit of $R$.  In the integral case,
the statement is understood in the
torsion-free quotient.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The class $\alpha$ generates a direct
summand precisely when some homomorphism to
$R$ takes $\alpha$ to a unit.  By
nonsingularity, every such homomorphism is
pairing with a suitable $\beta$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Real projective space]
\label{at-example-rp-duality-product}
Put $P^n=\mathbb{R}P^n$ and use
$R=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$.  The additive
cohomology groups are recorded by
$$
\xymatrix@C=.15em{
0\ar[r]&1\ar[r]&\ldots\ar[r]
  &i\ar[r]&\cdots\ar[r]
  &j\ar[r]&\cdots\ar[r]
  &i+j\ar[r]&\cdots\ar[r]&n\\
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\ar[r]
  &\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\ar[r]
  &\ldots\ar[r]
  &\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\ar[r]
  &\cdots\ar[r]
  &\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\ar[r]
  &\cdots\ar[r]
  &\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\ar[r]
  &\cdots\ar[r]
  &\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}.
}
$$
Let $x$ be the nonzero degree-one class.
In $P^{i+j}$, nonsingularity says that the
nonzero class $x^i$ pairs nontrivially with
the unique nonzero degree-$j$ class,
namely $x^j$.  Hence $x^{i+j}\neq 0$.
Naturality for
$P^{i+j}\hookrightarrow P^n$ gives the
same conclusion in $P^n$ whenever
$i+j\leq n$.  Thus duality recovers the
projective-space cup-product calculation.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[The torus]
\label{at-example-torus-pairing}
Let $T=S^1\times S^1$.  Its integral
cohomology has the pattern
$$
\xymatrix@R=.8em{
0&1&2\\
\mathbb{Z}\ar[r]
  &\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\ar[r]
  &\mathbb{Z}\\
&\left\langle{}\alpha\right\rangle{}
 \oplus
 \left\langle{}\beta\right\rangle{}
  &\left\langle{}\tau\right\rangle{}.
}
$$
Choose $\alpha$ to restrict to a generator
on a meridian, and choose $\beta$ from the
other circle factor.  The source records
this choice schematically as
$$
\xymatrix{
T&\mathbb{Z}\ar[r]
  &\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\ar[r]
  &\mathbb{Z}\\
S^1\ar@{^{(}->}[u]
  &\mathbb{Z}
  &\mathbb{Z}
   =\left\langle{}\alpha
    \right\rangle{}
  &0.
}
$$
Graded commutativity gives
$2\alpha^2=0$, and $H^2(T;\mathbb{Z})$
is torsion-free, so $\alpha^2=0$; this is
not a dimension argument.  If
$\gamma=n_1\alpha+n_2\beta$ satisfies
$\alpha\smile\gamma=\tau$, then
nonsingularity forces $n_2=\pm1$.
Reversing the choice of $\beta$ if needed,
we obtain
$$
\alpha\smile\beta=\tau,
\qquad
\beta\smile\alpha=-\tau.
$$
This agrees with the Kunneth description
of $H^*(S^1\times S^1;\mathbb{Z})$.
\end{example}
\section{Manifolds with boundary}
\label{section-manifolds-with-boundary}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-boundary-manifold}
An \emph{$n$-manifold with boundary} is a
Hausdorff, second-countable space locally
homeomorphic to
$$
\mathbb{R}_+^n
=\{(x_1,\ldots,x_n)\in\mathbb{R}^n
\mid x_n\geq0\}.
$$
Its \emph{boundary} $\partial M$ consists
of the points corresponding locally to
$x_n=0$; its complement is the
\emph{interior}.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-oriented-boundary}
The boundary of an $n$-manifold is an
$(n-1)$-manifold without boundary.  If $M$
is oriented, its boundary inherits an
orientation.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Collar theorem]
\label{at-proposition-boundary-collar}
The boundary has a neighborhood in $M$
homeomorphic to
$$
\partial M\times[0,1),
$$
with $\partial M$ corresponding to
$\partial M\times\{0\}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-boundary-orientation}
A manifold with boundary is
\emph{$R$-orientable} if its interior is
$R$-orientable.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Question]
\label{at-remark-does-manifold-bound}
Given a closed manifold $M$, must there be
a compact manifold $W$ with
$\partial W=M$?  No: not every closed
manifold bounds.  This failure motivates
cobordism.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-unoriented-cobordism}
Two closed smooth $n$-manifolds $M$ and
$N$ are \emph{unoriented cobordant} if
there is a compact smooth
$(n+1)$-manifold $W$ such that
$$
\partial W\cong M\sqcup N.
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-cobordism-equivalence}
Unoriented cobordism is an equivalence
relation.  Cylinders give reflexivity,
relabeling the boundary gives symmetry,
and gluing cobordisms along their common
boundary gives transitivity.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-cobordism-ring}
The \emph{unoriented smooth cobordism ring}
is the graded set of cobordism classes
$$
\Omega_*^O
=\coprod_{n\geq0}\Omega_n^O,
$$
with addition induced by disjoint union and
multiplication induced by Cartesian product.
\end{definition}


\section{Poincare conjectures and
homotopy recognition}
\label{section-poincare-recognition}

Closed manifolds are completely understood
in the first two dimensions.  The only
connected closed one-manifold is the circle,
and a connected closed surface is determined
by its orientability and genus.  In dimension
three there is already an abundance of
examples: the sphere, the three-torus,
connected sums of three-tori, and products of
a circle with a surface, among many others.

Poincare first asked whether a closed
three-manifold with the homology of a sphere
must be homeomorphic to the sphere.  His own
homology sphere showed that the answer is no.
The construction identifies faces of a
dodecahedron and can be described as a
quotient of $S^3$ by the binary icosahedral
group $2I$.  Thus
$$
\pi _1(S^3/(2I))\cong 2I,
\qquad |2I|=120,
$$
although the quotient has the integral
homology of $S^3$.

\begin{remark}[Question]
\label{at-question-manifold-homotopy-type}
If two connected closed manifolds are
homotopy equivalent, must they be
homeomorphic?
\end{remark}

The answer is no, even in dimension three.
The lens spaces $L_{1/7}$ and $L_{2/7}$ are
homotopy equivalent but not homeomorphic
\cite[Example 2.43]{hat}.

In dimensions at most two the classification
and homology of surfaces give a positive
answer.  For example, the orientable surface
of genus $g$ has first homology
$\mathbb{Z}^{2g}$.  Poincare's conjecture is
instead the special case in which one of the
manifolds is $S^3$.

\begin{remark}[Poincare conjecture]
\label{at-question-poincare-homotopy}
If a closed three-manifold $M$ satisfies
$M\simeq S^3$, must $M$ be homeomorphic to
$S^3$?
\end{remark}

Equivalently, one can ask the following.

\begin{remark}[Poincare conjecture]
\label{at-question-poincare-simply-connected}
If a closed three-manifold is simply
connected, must it be homeomorphic to $S^3$?
\end{remark}

This is now a theorem.  The
homotopy-theoretic
part of the assertion is much easier.

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-three-manifold-sphere}
Every closed, simply connected
three-manifold is homotopy equivalent to
$S^3$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{exercise}
Show that a closed simply connected
$3$-manifold is homotopy equivalent to
$S^3$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{proof}
Let $M$ be a closed simply connected
three-manifold.  It is orientable, and
Poincare duality and universal coefficients
give
$$
H_i(M;\mathbb{Z})\cong
\begin{cases}
\mathbb{Z}&i=0,3,\\
0&i=1,2.
\end{cases}
$$
Indeed, $H_1(M;\mathbb{Z})$ is the
abelianization of $\pi_1(M)$, and
$$
H_2(M;\mathbb{Z})
\cong H^1(M;\mathbb{Z})=0.
$$
Hurewicz first gives $\pi_2(M)=0$, and then
identifies $\pi_3(M)$ with
$H_3(M;\mathbb{Z})$.  Choose
$$
f:S^3\longrightarrow M
$$
whose homotopy class maps to a generator of
$H_3(M;\mathbb{Z})$.  The map $f$ is a
homology isomorphism in every degree, so
homological Whitehead finishes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-four-manifold-example}
Simple connectivity alone does not recognize
a sphere in dimension four.  For example,
$S^2\times S^2$ is simply connected but has
$$
H_2(S^2\times S^2;\mathbb{Z})
\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}.
$$
\end{remark}

The high-dimensional version motivated the
rest of these lectures.

\begin{theorem}[Smale]
\label{at-theorem-smale-poincare}
If $M^n$ is a smooth closed manifold,
$n\geq6$, and $M\simeq S^n$, then $M$ is
homeomorphic to $S^n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Stallings--Newman]
\label{at-theorem-stallings-newman}
If $M^n$ is a closed manifold, $n\geq5$,
and $M\simeq S^n$, then $M$ is homeomorphic
to $S^n$.
\end{theorem}

The smooth statement is subtler.

\begin{remark}[Question]
\label{at-question-smooth-poincare}
If a smooth closed manifold $M^n$ is
homotopy equivalent to $S^n$, must it be
diffeomorphic to $S^n$?
\end{remark}

Milnor's exotic seven-spheres show that the
answer is no.  They are homeomorphic but not
diffeomorphic to the standard sphere.
Characteristic classes and cobordism supply
some of the invariants that detect smooth
phenomena of this kind.
\section{Cobordisms and the
h-cobordism theorem}
\label{section-h-cobordism}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-cobordism}
An $n$-dimensional \emph{cobordism} from
$M_-$ to $M_+$ is a compact smooth
$n$-manifold $W$ together with a
decomposition
$$
\partial W=\partial_-W\sqcup\partial_+W,
\qquad
\partial_-W=M_-,\quad
\partial_+W=M_+.
$$
The manifolds $M_-$ and $M_+$ are then
\emph{cobordant}.
\end{definition}

The following picture is the nontrivial
figure from the original notes.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=.48\linewidth]{%
algebraic-topology-assets/cobordism.pdf}
\caption{A cobordism with three boundary
components.}
\label{at-figure-cobordism}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}
\label{at-example-trivial-cobordism}
The \emph{trivial cobordism} on $M$ is
$M\times I$, with the two boundary copies of
$M$ distinguished as incoming and outgoing.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-h-cobordism}
A cobordism $W$ is an
\emph{$h$-cobordism} when both boundary
inclusions in
$$
\xymatrix@C=15pt@R=8pt{
\partial_-W\ar[dr]^{\lhook}&&\\
&W&\\
\partial_+W\ar[ur]^{\lhook}&&
}
$$
are homotopy equivalences.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
\label{at-example-non-h-cobordisms}
The pair of pants is not an $h$-cobordism:
one end has two circle components and the
other has one.  A cylinder is an
$h$-cobordism.  A cylinder with a handle is
not one, since its fundamental group is
nonabelian free whereas an end has
fundamental
group $\mathbb{Z}$.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[Smooth h-cobordism theorem]
\label{at-theorem-h-cobordism}
Let $W^m$ be a compact, simply connected
$h$-cobordism of dimension $m\geq6$.  Then
$$
W\cong\partial_-W\times I
$$
diffeomorphically, relative to
$\partial_-W$.
\end{theorem}

Here is why this theorem implies Smale's
result.  The argument is worth retaining
because it ties together Mayer--Vietoris,
duality, van Kampen, and Whitehead.

\begin{theorem}[Smale]
\label{at-theorem-smale-from-h-cobordism}
If $M^n$ is a smooth closed manifold,
$n\geq6$, and $M\simeq S^n$, then $M$ is
homeomorphic to $S^n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Remove the interiors of two disjoint smooth
$n$-disks $D_-$ and $D_+$ from $M$, and set
$$
W=M-\bigl(
\mathop{\rm int}(D_-)
\cup\mathop{\rm int}(D_+)
\bigr).
$$
Its boundary components are spheres.  Cap one
end at a time:
$$
V_-=W\cup_{\partial_+W}D_+,
\qquad
V_+=W\cup_{\partial_-W}D_-.
$$

\begin{lemma}
\label{at-lemma-punctured-sphere-h-cobordism}
The manifold $W$ is a simply connected
$h$-cobordism.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Apply reduced Mayer--Vietoris to
$M=V_-\cup_{\partial_-W}D_-$.  It contains
the exact segment
$$
\xymatrix@C=13pt{
\cdots\ar[r]&
\widetilde H_i(\partial_-W)\ar[r]&
\widetilde H_i(V_-)\oplus
\widetilde H_i(D_-)\ar[r]&
\widetilde H_i(M)\ar[r]&
\widetilde H_{i-1}(\partial_-W)
\ar[r]&\cdots .
}
$$
It follows that the reduced homology of
$V_-$ can be nonzero only in degrees $n$ and
$n-1$.  Poincare--Lefschetz duality gives
$$
H_i(V_-;\mathbb{Z})
\cong H^{n-i}(V_-,\partial_-W;
\mathbb{Z}).
$$
The beginning of the cohomology sequence is
$$
\xymatrix@C=14pt{
0\ar[r]&H^0(V_-,\partial_-W)\ar[r]&
H^0(V_-)\ar[r]&H^0(\partial_-W).
}
$$
The last map is an isomorphism
$\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}$.
Continuing the sequence gives
$$
\xymatrix@C=10pt{
0\ar[r]&H^0(V_-,\partial_-W)\ar[r]&
H^0(V_-)\ar[r]&H^0(\partial_-W)
\ar[r]&H^1(V_-,\partial_-W)\ar[r]&
H^1(V_-)\ar[r]&\cdots .
}
$$
Thus both relative groups displayed here
vanish.  Duality eliminates the two possible
exceptional homology groups, so $V_-$ is
acyclic.  The same argument applies to $V_+$.

Van Kampen applied to the same decompositions
shows that $V_-$, $V_+$, and $W$ are simply
connected.  Finally, Mayer--Vietoris for
$V_-=W\cup_{\partial_+W}D_+$ contains
$$
\xymatrix@C=13pt{
0\ar[r]&H_i(\partial_+W)\ar[r]&
H_i(W)\oplus H_i(D_+)\ar[r]&
H_i(V_-)\ar[r]&\cdots .
}
$$
In reduced positive degrees the last two disk
and capped-manifold terms vanish, and degree
zero is immediate.  Hence
$\partial_+W\longrightarrow W$ is a homology
isomorphism.  Homological Whitehead makes it
a homotopy equivalence.  The other end is
identical.
\end{proof}

The h-cobordism theorem now identifies $W$
with $S^{n-1}\times I$.  Capping its two ends
produces a homeomorphism $M\cong S^n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-exotic-capping-map}
The final homeomorphism need not be a
diffeomorphism, exactly as exotic spheres
warn us.  It can nevertheless be chosen to
be a diffeomorphism away from one point.
\end{remark}

We recall three smooth notions needed for
the handle argument.

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-immersion-embedding}
A smooth map $f:M\longrightarrow N$ is an
\emph{immersion} if
$$
df_x:T_xM\longrightarrow T_{f(x)}N
$$
is injective for every $x$.  It is an
\emph{embedding} if it is an immersion and
$M\longrightarrow f(M)$ is a homeomorphism.
An \emph{isotopy} of embeddings is a smooth
map
$$
F:M\times I\longrightarrow N
$$
for which $F_t=F(-,t)$ is an embedding for
every $t$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Isotopy extension]
\label{at-theorem-isotopy-extension}
Let $f_t:M\longrightarrow N$ be a smooth
isotopy, with $M$ compact, or more generally
with the isotopy proper.  There is an ambient
isotopy
$$
\Phi_t:N\longrightarrow N
$$
such that $\Phi_0$ is the identity and
$f_t=\Phi_t\circ f_0$.
\end{theorem}
\section{Handles and cancellation}
\label{section-handles}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-handle}
An $n$-dimensional \emph{handle of index
$i$}, where $0\leq i\leq n$, is
$$
h=D^i\times D^{n-i}.
$$
Its incoming and outgoing boundary pieces are
$$
\partial^-h=S^{i-1}\times D^{n-i},
\qquad
\partial^+h=D^i\times S^{n-i-1}.
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-handle-boundary}
The boundary of an index-$i$ handle is
$$
\begin{aligned}
\partial h
&=(\partial D^i)\times D^{n-i}
\mathbin{\cup}
D^i\times\partial D^{n-i}\\
&=\partial^-h\cup\partial^+h.
\end{aligned}
$$
\end{proposition}

\begin{example}
\label{at-example-three-dimensional-handles}
In dimension three the four handles are
$$
\begin{matrix}
i&0&1&2&3\\
h&D^0\times D^3&D^1\times D^2&
D^2\times D^1&D^3\times D^0.
\end{matrix}
$$
They are all three-balls as manifolds, but
their incoming and outgoing boundary pieces
play different roles.
\end{example}

If $M^n$ has boundary and
$f:\partial^-h\longrightarrow\partial M$ is
an embedding, the result of attaching $h$ is
$$
M+_fh=M\cup_f(D^i\times D^{n-i}).
$$
The smooth structure uses collars of the
boundary.

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-closed-handle-decomp}
Every closed smooth manifold admits an
ordered handle decomposition
$$
M\cong h_1+h_2+\cdots+h_r,
\qquad I(h_j)\leq I(h_{j+1}).
$$
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-handle-sum-order}
Handle attachment is neither a commutative
nor a formally associative operation.  The
attaching data at each stage are part of the
decomposition.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-relative-handles}
Every smooth cobordism $W^n$ has a relative
handle decomposition
$$
W=\partial_-W\times I+h_1+\cdots+h_m
$$
in which no attaching map meets the lower
boundary $\partial_-W\times\{0\}$.
\end{theorem}

The remaining problem is geometric algebra:
find conditions under which handles can be
removed from this expression.

\begin{lemma}
\label{at-lemma-isotopic-handle-attachments}
If two embeddings
$$
f,g:\partial^-h\longrightarrow\partial M
$$
are isotopic, then
$$
M+_fh\cong M+_gh
$$
diffeomorphically.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Extend the isotopy to an ambient isotopy
$\Phi_t$ of $\partial M$.  On a boundary
collar use
$$
\Phi'_t(x,s)=(\Phi_t(x),s).
$$
It yields the commutative gluing diagram
$$
\xymatrix@C=35pt{
\partial M\times I\ar[d]_{\Phi'_1}&
\partial^-h\ar[l]_{f}\ar[d]^{1}\\
\partial M\times I&
\partial^-h\ar[l]^{g}.
}
$$
The induced map of the pushouts is the
required diffeomorphism.
\end{proof}

The disk $D^i\times\{0\}$ is the
\emph{core}, and its boundary
$S^{i-1}\times\{0\}$ is the
\emph{attaching sphere}.  Dually,
$\{0\}\times D^{n-i}$ is the
\emph{cocore}, and
$\{0\}\times S^{n-i-1}$ is the
\emph{belt sphere}.  For a
$D^1\times D^2$ handle, the core is the axis
of the cylinder and the cocore is its middle
disk.

A CW decomposition can be thickened, or a
Morse function can be used, to produce a
handle decomposition grouped by index.

\begin{theorem}[Whitehead]
\label{at-theorem-indexed-handle-decomp}
Every smooth manifold has a decomposition
$$
(h^0_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup h^0_{r_0})+
(h^1_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup h^1_{r_1})+
\cdots
$$
with handles ordered by index.  There is an
analogous relative decomposition of a
cobordism beginning at
$\partial_-W\times I$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}
\label{at-lemma-simultaneous-handles}
Suppose an index-$i$ handle $h_1$ is followed
by an index-$j$ handle $h_2$.  If the belt
sphere of $h_1$ is disjoint from the
attaching
sphere of $h_2$, then, after isotopy,
$$
(M+h_1)+h_2=M+(h_1\sqcup h_2).
$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If the attaching regions are already
disjoint, there is nothing to prove.  In
general, the assumed disjointness of the two
distinguished spheres lets one shrink the
attaching neighborhood of $h_2$ and isotope
it off the first handle.  The preceding lemma
turns that isotopy into a diffeomorphism of
the attached manifolds.
\end{proof}

For consecutive indices $i$ and $i+1$, the
belt sphere of the first handle has dimension
$n-i-1$ and the attaching sphere of the
second has dimension $i$.  Both lie in an
$(n-1)$-dimensional boundary, so a transverse
intersection is zero-dimensional.  A handle
decomposition in which all such intersections
are transverse will be called
\emph{transverse standard}.

\begin{lemma}[Handle cancellation]
\label{at-lemma-handle-cancellation}
Let $h_i$ and $h_{i+1}$ be handles of
consecutive indices.  If the belt sphere of
$h_i$ and the attaching sphere of $h_{i+1}$
meet transversely in exactly one point, then
$$
M+h_i+h_{i+1}\cong M.
$$
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For indices zero and one, the first handle is
a disjoint ball and the second joins it back
to the original manifold.  For indices one
and two, the first creates a tunnel and the
second fills it along a longitude.  In
general, product neighborhoods of the unique
intersection reduce the attachment to this
same local model.  The remaining portions
are carried away by isotopy, and the pair is
equivalent to attaching a collar.
\end{proof}

To turn algebraic intersection into geometric
intersection, orient the ambient boundary and
the two spheres.  Each transverse
intersection point $x$ then has a sign
$\epsilon(x)$.

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-oriented-intersection}
The \emph{oriented intersection number} of
oriented transverse submanifolds $S$ and $A$
of complementary dimensions is
$$
S\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\cdot}}A
=\sum_{x\in S\cap A}\epsilon(x).
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Whitney trick]
\label{at-lemma-whitney-handle-trick}
Suppose $n\geq6$, the relevant boundary is
simply connected, and
$2\leq i\leq n-3$.  If the belt sphere of an
index-$i$ handle and the attaching sphere of
an index-$(i+1)$ handle have oriented
intersection number $1$ or $-1$, the latter
can be isotoped so that the two spheres meet
in exactly one point.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Pair intersection points of opposite signs.
Join each pair by one arc in each sphere.
Simple connectivity fills the resulting loop
by a Whitney disk.  The dimension hypotheses
allow its interior to be made embedded and
disjoint from both spheres.  A Whitney move
across the disk removes the pair.  Repeating
leaves the single point required by the
algebraic intersection number.
\end{proof}

Consequently, under these hypotheses, a pair
with intersection number $1$ or $-1$
cancels.

Let $C_i$ be the free abelian group generated
by the index-$i$ handles.  The incidence maps
form the complex
$$
\xymatrix@C=12pt{
C_n\ar[r]^{D_n}&C_{n-1}\ar[r]&\cdots
\ar[r]&C_1\ar[r]^{D_1}&C_0\ar[r]&0.
}
$$
If $S_j^i$ is the belt sphere of the
$j$-th index-$i$ handle and $A_k^{i+1}$ is
the attaching sphere of the $k$-th
index-$(i+1)$ handle, then
$$
(D_{i+1})_{jk}
=\sum_{x\in S_j^i\cap A_k^{i+1}}
\epsilon(x).
$$

% The commented matrix sketch after this
% formula in the source was empty and carries
% no additional mathematical content.

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-handle-chain-complex}
The incidence maps satisfy
$D_iD_{i+1}=0$, and
$$
H_i(C_*)\cong H_i(W,\partial_-W;
\mathbb{Z}).
$$
\end{proposition}

In particular, this complex is acyclic for an
$h$-cobordism.

\begin{lemma}[Handle slides]
\label{at-lemma-handle-slides}
Sliding one index-$i$ handle over another
gives a transverse-standard decomposition
with the same number of handles.
Algebraically
it performs an elementary column operation on
$D_i$ and the inverse row operation on
$D_{i+1}$.  Sliding index-$(i+1)$ handles
performs elementary column operations on
$D_{i+1}$ and the inverse row operations on
$D_{i+2}$.  Elementary row operations on
$D_i$ are realized by sliding index-$(i-1)$
handles, with the inverse column operations
on $D_{i-1}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-middle-index-handles}
A simply connected $h$-cobordism of dimension
$n\geq6$ has a transverse-standard handle
decomposition with no handles of indices
$0$, $1$, $n-1$, or $n$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof of the
h-cobordism theorem]
Use the handle structure in the proposition
and its acyclic incidence complex.  If
$C_{i-1}$ is the first nonzero group, the
relevant tail is
$$
\xymatrix@C=16pt{
\cdots\ar[r]&C_i\ar[r]^{D_i}&
C_{i-1}\ar[r]&0\ar[r]&\cdots .
}
$$
Acyclicity makes $D_i$ surjective.  Smith
normal form, realized by handle slides, puts
an identity block into its matrix.  Thus a
chosen index-$(i-1)$ handle has algebraic
intersection $1$ or $-1$ with a chosen
index-$i$ handle and zero with the others.

Reorder handles of the same index so this
pair is adjacent.  The boundary at that stage
is simply connected: the initial boundary is
simply connected, index-two handles can only
kill elements of the fundamental group, and
higher-index handles do not change it.  The
Whitney trick changes algebraic intersection
into one geometric intersection, and handle
cancellation removes the pair.  Iterate until
the incidence complex is empty.  Applying the
dual handle decomposition deals with the
remaining high-index handles.  What remains
is the product cobordism.
\end{proof}
\section{Vector bundles and K-theory}
\label{section-vector-bundles-k-theory}

We work over either $\mathbb{R}$ or
$\mathbb{C}$, specifying the field when it
matters.

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-vector-bundle}
A rank-$n$ \emph{vector bundle} is a map
$p:E\longrightarrow B$ whose fibers are
$n$-dimensional vector spaces and which has
local trivializations
$$
p^{-1}(U)\cong U\times\mathbb{F}^n.
$$
A \emph{bundle morphism} over $B$ is a
continuous map, linear on fibers, in a
commutative diagram
$$
\xymatrix@C=30pt@R=12pt{
E_1\ar[rr]^f\ar[dr]_{p_1}&&
E_2\ar[dl]^{p_2}\\
&B.&
}
$$
Given a map $f:X\longrightarrow Y$ and a
bundle $E\longrightarrow Y$, the
\emph{pullback bundle} is the cartesian
square
$$
\xymatrix{
f^*E\ar[r]\ar[d]_{p'}&E\ar[d]^p\\
X\ar[r]_f&Y.
}
$$
It defines a contravariant map on isomorphism
classes of bundles.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
\label{at-lemma-fiberwise-bundle-isomorphism}
In a bundle morphism
$$
\xymatrix@C=30pt@R=12pt{
E_1\ar[rr]^f\ar[dr]_{p_1}&&
E_2\ar[dl]^{p_2}\\
&B,&
}
$$
the map $f$ is a bundle isomorphism if its
restriction to every fiber is a linear
isomorphism.
\end{lemma}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-bundle-direct-sum}
For two bundles over the same base,
$$
\xymatrix@C=30pt@R=12pt{
E_1\ar[dr]_{p_1}&&E_2\ar[dl]^{p_2}\\
&B,&
}
$$
their \emph{direct sum} is
$$
E_1\oplus E_2
=\{(e_1,e_2)\mid p_1(e_1)=p_2(e_2)\}.
$$
It is a subspace of $E_1\times E_2$ with
fiber the direct sum of the two fibers.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-bundle-complements}
Writing $\varepsilon^n$ for the trivial
rank-$n$ bundle, one has
$$
\varepsilon^n\oplus\varepsilon^m
\cong\varepsilon^{n+m}.
$$
If $B$ is compact Hausdorff, every vector
bundle $E$ has a complementary bundle $E'$:
$$
E\oplus E'\cong\varepsilon^N.
$$
The two bundles lie over the common base as
in
$$
\xymatrix@C=22pt@R=10pt{
E\ar[d]_p&E'\ar[dl]^{p'}\\
B.&
}
$$
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-bundle-tensor-product}
The \emph{tensor product} of two bundles over
$B$ has fiber
$$
(E_1\otimes E_2)_b
=(E_1)_b\otimes(E_2)_b.
$$
Its topology is defined locally from tensor
products of bundle charts.  Namely,
trivializations over $U$ induce
$$
(E_1\otimes E_2)|_U
\cong U\times
(\mathbb{F}^n\otimes\mathbb{F}^m).
$$
\end{definition}

Two bundles are \emph{stably isomorphic} if
they become isomorphic after taking direct
sums with trivial bundles.  The Grothendieck
group completion of bundles under direct sum
is $K(X)$.  Its elements are formal
differences $[E]-[F]$, subject to
$$
[E]-[F]=[E']-[F']
$$
when there is a vector bundle $G$ over $X$
such that
$$
E\oplus F'\oplus G
\cong
E'\oplus F\oplus G.
$$
If $X$ is compact Hausdorff, the bundle
complement theorem lets one replace $G$ by
a trivial bundle in this relation.  Pullback
makes $K$ a contravariant functor.

For a based compact space $(X,x_0)$, define
$$
\widetilde K(X)=
\ker\bigl(K(X)\longrightarrow K(x_0)\bigr).
$$
The restriction map comes from the pullback
square
$$
\xymatrix{
E|_{x_0}\ar[r]\ar[d]&E\ar[d]\\
\{x_0\}\ar[r]&X.
}
$$
Since $K(x_0)\cong\mathbb{Z}$ by dimension,
$\widetilde K(X)$ records the virtual
rank-zero classes.

Tensor product gives $K(X)$ a ring structure.
For bundles over a common base one again has
the diagram
$$
\xymatrix@C=30pt@R=12pt{
E_1\ar[dr]_{p_1}&&E_2\ar[dl]^{p_2}\\
&B.&
}
$$
For different bases the external product is
$$
K(X)\otimes K(Y)
\longrightarrow K(X\times Y),
$$
$$
a\otimes b\longmapsto
p_X^*(a)p_Y^*(b).
$$

Let $H$ be the tautological complex line
bundle over $\mathbb{C}P^1$:
$$
H=\{(L,v)\in\mathbb{C}P^1\times
\mathbb{C}^2\mid v\in L\}.
$$
Its projection is pictured by
$$
\xymatrix@C=20pt@R=10pt{
H\ar[d]_p&(L,v)\ar[d]|-{
\hspace{-4pt}\longmapsto}\\
\mathbb{C}P^1&L.
}
$$

\begin{proposition}[Claim]
\label{at-claim-hopf-bundle-relation}
In $K(\mathbb{C}P^1)$,
$$
(H\otimes H)\oplus1=H\oplus H.
$$
Equivalently, $(H-1)^2=0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{theorem}[Product theorem]
\label{at-theorem-k-product}
For compact Hausdorff $X$, the composition
$$
\xymatrix@C=13pt{
\mathbb{Z}[H]/(H-1)^2\otimes K(X)
\ar[r]&K(S^2)\otimes K(X)\ar[r]&
K(S^2\times X)
}
$$
is an isomorphism.  In particular,
$$
K(S^2)\cong\mathbb{Z}[H]/(H-1)^2.
$$
\end{theorem}

For a finite based CW pair, write the
quotient sequence as
$$
\xymatrix{
A\ar@{^{(}->}[r]^i&X\ar[r]^q&X/A.
}
$$
Contravariance gives the left-exact segment
$$
\xymatrix@C=20pt{
\widetilde K(X/A)\ar[r]^{q^*}&
\widetilde K(X)\ar[r]^{i^*}&
\widetilde K(A).
}
$$
Cones and suspensions extend it.  At the
space level one has the ladder
$$
\xymatrix@C=7pt@R=12pt{
A\ar@{^{(}->}[r]&X\ar@{^{(}->}[r]&
X\cup CA\ar[r]\ar[d]&
(X\cup CA)\cup CX\ar[r]\ar[d]&
((X\cup CA)\cup CX)\cup C(X\cup CA)
\ar[d]\\
&&X/A&\Sigma A&\Sigma X.
}
$$

\begin{lemma}
\label{at-lemma-contractible-bundle-quotient}
Let $A$ be a contractible subcomplex of a
finite CW complex $X$.  Pullback along
$q:X\longrightarrow X/A$ gives a bijection
between stable isomorphism classes of vector
bundles on $X/A$ and on $X$.
\end{lemma}

It follows that reduced K-theory has the
exact
sequence
$$
\xymatrix@C=8pt{
\cdots\ar[r]&
\widetilde K(\Sigma(X/A))\ar[r]&
\widetilde K(\Sigma X)\ar[r]&
\widetilde K(\Sigma A)\ar[r]&
\widetilde K(X/A)\ar[r]&
\widetilde K(X)\ar[r]&
\widetilde K(A).
}
$$

\begin{theorem}[Bott periodicity]
\label{at-theorem-bott-periodicity}
For complex K-theory and a finite based CW
complex $X$, there is a natural isomorphism
$$
\widetilde K(X)
\cong\widetilde K(\Sigma^2X).
$$
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}
\label{at-lemma-k-theory-spheres}
For complex K-theory,
$$
\widetilde K(S^n)\cong
\begin{cases}
\mathbb{Z}&n=2r,\\
0&n=2r+1.
\end{cases}
$$
\end{corollary}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-parallelizable-sphere}
A smooth $n$-manifold is
\emph{parallelizable} if its tangent bundle
is trivial, equivalently if it admits $n$
pointwise linearly independent vector fields.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-division-algebra-spheres}
The only dimensions in which
$\mathbb{R}^n$ admits a normed real division
algebra structure are
$$
n=1,2,4,8.
$$
Correspondingly, $S^{n-1}$ is parallelizable
for these values.  In fact, these are the
only parallelizable spheres.
\end{theorem}

\emph{Dani's question.}
What does triviality of the tangent bundle
say about curvature?

It says very little by itself.  The circle is
locally Euclidean, but the parallelizable
three-sphere carries its usual positively
curved metric and cannot admit a flat metric:
a compact simply connected flat manifold
would have universal cover $\mathbb{R}^3$.

The H-space notion introduced with higher
homotopy now gives a topological use of
these bundle calculations.

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-parallelizable-h-space}
Each sphere arising as the unit sphere in
$\mathbb{R}$, $\mathbb{C}$, the quaternions,
or the octonions is an H-space.  A
parallelization of $S^{n-1}$ also gives such
a multiplication.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a normed division algebra use
$$
(x,y)\longmapsto\frac{xy}{\lVert xy\rVert}.
$$
Alternatively, a parallelization determines
continuously, for every $x$, an orthonormal
frame with first vector $x$.  Choose the
standard frame at the basepoint.  The unique
orthogonal transformation carrying the
standard frame to the frame at $x$, applied
to $y$, defines the multiplication.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-k-theory-h-spaces}
Bott periodicity and the product theorem give
$$
\widetilde K(S^{2n})\cong\mathbb{Z},
\qquad
\widetilde K(S^{2n+1})=0,
$$
and
$$
K(S^{2k})\otimes K(X)
\longrightarrow K(S^{2k}\times X)
$$
is an isomorphism for finite CW complexes.
The resulting K-theoretic obstruction shows
that no positive-dimensional even sphere is
an H-space.  Stronger arguments leave only
$S^0$, $S^1$, $S^3$, and $S^7$ among the
spheres.
\end{remark}
\section{Characteristic classes}
\label{section-characteristic-classes}

Let $X=(x^i_j)$ be an $r\times r$ matrix of
indeterminates.

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-invariant-polynomial}
A polynomial $P(X)$ is
\emph{conjugation invariant} if
$$
P(A^{-1}XA)=P(X)
$$
for every
$A\in\operatorname{GL}_r(\mathbb{R})$.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
\label{at-example-invariant-polynomials}
The determinant, trace, and power traces
$$
\operatorname{tr}_k(X)=
\operatorname{tr}(X^k)
$$
are invariant.  So are the coefficients
$f_j(X)$ defined by
$$
\det(I+tX)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}f_j(X)t^j.
$$
For a two-by-two matrix,
$$
\det
\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\z&w\end{pmatrix}
=xw-yz,
$$
while its trace is $x+w$.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-invariants-algebra}
Let $\mathcal{A}$ be a commutative unital
$\mathbb{R}$-algebra.  If $P$ is invariant
under conjugation, it satisfies
$$
P(A^{-1}XA)=P(X)
$$
for matrices $X$ over $\mathcal{A}$ and
invertible matrices $A$ over $\mathcal{A}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-bundle-connection}
A \emph{connection} on a smooth vector bundle
$E\longrightarrow M$ is an
$\mathbb{R}$-bilinear map
$$
\nabla:\Gamma(TM)\times\Gamma(E)
\longrightarrow\Gamma(E)
$$
which is $C^\infty(M)$-linear in the vector
field and satisfies
$$
\nabla_X(fs)=(Xf)s+f\nabla_Xs
$$
in the section variable.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-bundle-curvature}
The \emph{curvature} of a connection is the
$\mathbb{R}$-multilinear operator
$$
R(X,Y)s=
\nabla_X\nabla_Ys-
\nabla_Y\nabla_Xs-
\nabla_{[X,Y]}s.
$$
\end{definition}

For a local frame $e_1,\ldots,e_r$, write
$$
R(X,Y)e_j
=\sum_i\Omega^i_j(X,Y)e_i.
$$
Thus $\Omega=(\Omega^i_j)$ is a matrix of
two-forms.  Under a change of frame by
$a:U\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}_r(
\mathbb{R})$, the curvature matrix changes
by conjugation:
$$
\widetilde\Omega=a^{-1}\Omega a.
$$

At a point $p$, the even exterior algebra
$$
\bigoplus_{i\geq0}
\bigwedge^{2i}T_p^*M
$$
is commutative.  Hence an invariant
homogeneous polynomial $P$ of degree $k$
yields a well-defined $2k$-form
$P(\Omega)$.  For example, in rank two,
$$
\det(\Omega)=
\Omega^1_1\wedge\Omega^2_2-
\Omega^2_1\wedge\Omega^1_2.
$$

\begin{theorem}[Chern--Weil]
\label{at-theorem-chern-weil}
The form $P(\Omega)$ is closed, and its de
Rham cohomology class is independent of the
chosen connection.
\end{theorem}

The construction is multiplicative:
$$
[P(\Omega)\wedge Q(\Omega)]
=[P(\Omega)]\smile[Q(\Omega)].
$$
Invariant polynomials may be generated either
by the $f_j$ or by the power traces, so this
defines the full Chern--Weil homomorphism.

For a real bundle with a metric connection,
$\Omega$ is skew symmetric.

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-pontryagin-class}
The $k$-th \emph{real Pontryagin class} is
$$
p_k(E)=
\left[
f_{2k}\left(\frac{\Omega}{2\pi}\right)
\right]
\in H^{4k}(M;\mathbb{R}).
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-skew-det-square}
The determinant of a skew-symmetric
$2m\times2m$ matrix is the square of a
polynomial in its entries.
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-pfaffian}
The polynomial whose square is the
determinant
is the \emph{Pfaffian}, denoted
$\operatorname{Pf}(X)$.  It is invariant
under oriented orthogonal changes of frame.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-euler-class-curvature}
For an oriented real bundle $E$ of rank
$2m$, its \emph{Euler class} in the
Chern--Weil normalization is
$$
e(E)=\left[
\frac{1}{(2\pi)^m}
\operatorname{Pf}(\Omega)
\right].
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Chern--Gauss--Bonnet]
\label{at-theorem-chern-gauss-bonnet}
For a closed oriented Riemannian
$2m$-manifold,
$$
\int_M e(TM)=\chi(M).
$$
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-chern-classes}
For a rank-$r$ complex bundle with a unitary
connection, its \emph{Chern classes} are the
homogeneous components in
$$
\det\left(I+\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}
\Omega\right)
=1+c_1(\Omega)+\cdots+c_r(\Omega).
$$
Their de Rham classes are denoted $c_j(E)$.
\end{definition}

Characteristic classes are natural.  For a
map $f:M\longrightarrow N$, the square
$$
\xymatrix{
f^*E\ar[r]\ar[d]&E\ar[d]\\
M\ar[r]_f&N
}
$$
is cartesian, and every characteristic class
$c$ satisfies
$$
c(f^*E)=f^*c(E).
$$

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-hypersurface-p-class}
Let $M^{4m}$ be a closed hypersurface in
$\mathbb{R}^{4m+1}$.  Every positive-degree
Pontryagin class of $TM$ vanishes.
\end{proposition}

The normal line is trivial, and
$$
TM\oplus\varepsilon^1
\cong\varepsilon^{4m+1}.
$$
Stability and the Whitney product formula for
Pontryagin classes give $p(TM)=1$.

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-calabi-yau}
In the terminology of these notes, a
\emph{Calabi--Yau manifold} is a compact
Kahler manifold with first Chern class zero.
\end{definition}

For a finite CW complex, Euler characteristic
can be computed from either cells or rational
Betti numbers:
$$
\chi(M)=\sum_i(-1)^ic_i
=\sum_i(-1)^i\beta_i.
$$

\begin{theorem}[Poincare--Hopf]
\label{at-theorem-poincare-hopf}
If a vector field $V$ on a closed manifold
has isolated zeros, then
$$
\chi(M)=\sum_{p\in Z(V)}
\operatorname{ind}_p(V).
$$
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-dependent-fields-locus}
Let $M^n$ be closed, let $1\leq k\leq n$,
and let $V_1,\ldots,V_k$ be generic vector
fields.  The associated section of
$\operatorname{Hom}(\varepsilon^k,TM)$ is
transverse to every rank stratum.  Its
dependency locus is a stratified subset.
The top stratum, where the rank is $k-1$,
is a submanifold of dimension $k-1$.  The
locus carries a mod-two fundamental cycle
Poincare dual to
$$
w_{n-k+1}(TM).
$$
\end{theorem}

This generalizes the geometric idea behind
the hairy-ball theorem.  We now apply it to
real projective spaces.
\section{Stiefel--Whitney classes and
projective space}
\label{section-stiefel-whitney-classes}

For a real rank-$n$ vector bundle $\xi$ over
$B$, there are classes
$$
w_i(\xi)\in H^i(B;\mathbb{Z}/2).
$$
They are characterized by the following
properties:

\begin{itemize}
\item $w_0(\xi)=1$ and $w_i(\xi)=0$ for
$i>n$.

\item Naturality gives
$w_i(f^*\xi)=f^*w_i(\xi)$.

\item The Whitney formula is
$$
w_k(\xi\oplus\eta)=
\sum_{i=0}^k
w_i(\xi)\smile w_{k-i}(\eta).
$$

\item For the tautological line bundle over
$\mathbb{R}P^1$, the class $w_1$ is the
nonzero element of
$H^1(\mathbb{R}P^1;\mathbb{Z}/2)$.
\end{itemize}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-sw-bundle-isomorphism}
If $\xi\cong\eta$, then
$w_i(\xi)=w_i(\eta)$ for every $i$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A bundle isomorphism appears over the
identity
in the square
$$
\xymatrix{
E(\xi)\ar[r]^{\cong}\ar[d]_{\xi}&
E(\eta)\ar[d]^{\eta}\\
B\ar[r]_1&B.
}
$$
Naturality and the identity map on cohomology
give the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-sw-trivial-bundle}
If $\varepsilon$ is trivial, then
$w_i(\varepsilon)=0$ for every $i>0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A trivialization and the inclusion of a fiber
give
$$
\xymatrix@C=18pt{
E(\varepsilon)\ar[r]^{\cong}\ar[d]&
B\times\mathbb{R}^k\ar[d]&
\{b\}\times\mathbb{R}^k
\ar@{^{(}->}[l]\ar[d]\\
B\ar[r]_1&B&\{b\}\ar@{^{(}->}[l].
}
$$
Positive-degree cohomology of the point
vanishes.  More directly, the trivial bundle
is pulled back from
$\mathbb{R}^k\longrightarrow\{b\}$ along the
unique map $B\longrightarrow\{b\}$.
Naturality gives the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-sw-stable}
If $\varepsilon$ is trivial, then
$$
w_j(\varepsilon\oplus\eta)=w_j(\eta).
$$
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Whitney formula and the previous
proposition give
$$
\begin{aligned}
w_k(\varepsilon\oplus\eta)
&=\sum_{i=0}^k
w_i(\varepsilon)w_{k-i}(\eta)\\
&=w_0(\varepsilon)w_k(\eta)\\
&=w_k(\eta).
\end{aligned}
$$
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-section-kills-top-sw}
If a rank-$n$ real bundle $\eta$ admits a
nowhere-zero section, then $w_n(\eta)=0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Choose a bundle metric.  The section spans a
trivial line subbundle $L$, and
$$
\eta\cong L\oplus L^\perp.
$$
Since $L^\perp$ has rank $n-1$, stability
gives
$$
w_n(\eta)=w_n(L^\perp)=0.
$$
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{at-lemma-sections-kill-sw-classes}
If a rank-$n$ bundle has $k$ pointwise
linearly independent sections, then
$$
w_n(\eta)=w_{n-1}(\eta)=\cdots
=w_{n-k+1}(\eta)=0.
$$
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-parallelizable-sw}
A parallelizable manifold has total
Stiefel--Whitney class $1$.  The converse is
false: the vanishing of these classes is only
one family of obstructions to a framing.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-total-sw-class}
The \emph{total Stiefel--Whitney class} of a
rank-$n$ real bundle is
$$
w(\xi)=1+w_1(\xi)+\cdots+w_n(\xi).
$$
\end{definition}

Let $\gamma_n^1$ be the tautological line
bundle over $\mathbb{R}P^n$.  Inclusion of a
projective line gives the pullback square
$$
\xymatrix{
E(\gamma_1^1)\ar[r]\ar[d]&
E(\gamma_n^1)\ar[d]\\
\mathbb{R}P^1\ar@{^{(}->}[r]^i&
\mathbb{R}P^n.
}
$$
If $a=w_1(\gamma_n^1)$, naturality and the
normalization axiom give
$$
w(\gamma_n^1)=1+a.
$$
The orthogonal complement satisfies
$$
\gamma_n^1\oplus(\gamma_n^1)^\perp
\cong\varepsilon^{n+1},
$$
so, in
$H^*(\mathbb{R}P^n;\mathbb{Z}/2)$,
$$
w((\gamma_n^1)^\perp)
=1+a+\cdots+a^n.
$$
Moreover,
$$
T\mathbb{R}P^n
\cong
\operatorname{Hom}
(\gamma_n^1,(\gamma_n^1)^\perp).
$$

\begin{theorem}
\label{at-theorem-projective-tangent-stable}
There is a stable bundle isomorphism
$$
T\mathbb{R}P^n\oplus\varepsilon^1
\cong(n+1)\gamma_n^1.
$$
Consequently,
$$
w(T\mathbb{R}P^n)=(1+a)^{n+1}.
$$
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The endomorphism bundle of a real line is
trivial.  Therefore
$$
\begin{aligned}
T\mathbb{R}P^n\oplus\varepsilon^1
&\cong
\operatorname{Hom}
(\gamma_n^1,(\gamma_n^1)^\perp)
\oplus
\operatorname{Hom}
(\gamma_n^1,\gamma_n^1)\\
&\cong
\operatorname{Hom}
(\gamma_n^1,\varepsilon^{n+1})\\
&\cong(n+1)\gamma_n^1.
\end{aligned}
$$
The Whitney formula and stability now give
the formula for the total class.
\end{proof}

The equation $w(T\mathbb{R}P^n)=1$ is
possible only if $n+1$ is a power of two.
Thus the first candidates are
$\mathbb{R}P^1$, $\mathbb{R}P^3$,
$\mathbb{R}P^7$, and
$\mathbb{R}P^{15}$.  The first three are
parallelizable, using complex, quaternionic,
and octonionic multiplication.  The
Stiefel--Whitney calculation alone does not
settle the later candidates; the full theorem
is that, among positive-dimensional real
projective spaces, only dimensions $1$, $3$,
and $7$ are parallelizable.
\section{Sheaves and etale spaces}
\label{section-sheaves-etale-spaces}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-presheaf-and-sheaf}
Let $\mathcal{O}(X)$ be the category of open
subsets of $X$, with inclusions as morphisms.
A \emph{presheaf} on $X$ is a contravariant
functor
$$
F:\mathcal{O}(X)^{\mathrm{op}}
\longrightarrow\operatorname{Set}.
$$
An element of $F(U)$ is a \emph{section over
$U$}; it is global when $U=X$.  The maps
induced by inclusions are
\emph{restriction maps}, as in
$$
\xymatrix@C=35pt@R=10pt{
U&&F(U)\ar[d]^{\rho^U_V}\\
V\ar@{^{(}->}[u]&&F(V).
}
$$
A morphism of presheaves is a natural
transformation.

A presheaf $F$ is a \emph{sheaf} if every
compatible family of local sections has a
unique gluing: for every open cover
$U=\bigcup_\alpha U_\alpha$ and sections
$s_\alpha\in F(U_\alpha)$ satisfying
$$
s_\alpha|_{U_\alpha\cap U_\beta}
=s_\beta|_{U_\alpha\cap U_\beta},
$$
there is a unique $s\in F(U)$ whose
restriction to each $U_\alpha$ is
$s_\alpha$.  Thus
$\operatorname{Sh}(X)$ is a full subcategory
of $\operatorname{Psh}(X)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
\label{at-example-presheaves-sheaves}
Continuous real-valued functions on open sets
form a sheaf.  On a smooth manifold, smooth
functions form a sheaf.  Assigning one fixed
set $A$ to every open set, including the
empty set, with identity restrictions gives
the constant presheaf, but generally not a
sheaf.  Its sheafification is the sheaf of
locally constant $A$-valued functions.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-etale-space}
An \emph{etale space} over $X$ is a local
homeomorphism $p:E\longrightarrow X$.  A
\emph{morphism of etale spaces} is a map over
$X$, represented by
$$
\xymatrix@C=30pt@R=12pt{
E\ar[rr]^\alpha\ar[dr]_p&&
F\ar[dl]^q\\
&X.&
}
$$
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\label{at-remark-sheaves-etale-equivalence}
Sheaves on $X$ are equivalent to etale spaces
over $X$.  The construction is made explicit
below rather than leaving the gap marked in
the original notes.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-sheaf-stalk}
For a sheaf $F$ and $x\in X$, the
\emph{stalk at $x$} is
$$
F_x=\underset{U\ni x}{\operatorname{colim}}
F(U).
$$
It consists of pairs $(s,U)$ with $x\in U$
and $s\in F(U)$, modulo the relation
$(s,U)\sim(t,V)$ when the restrictions agree
on some neighborhood of $x$ contained in
$U\cap V$.
\end{definition}

The relation among the four categories is
$$
\xymatrix@C=38pt@R=16pt{
\operatorname{Psh}(X)
\ar@<.6ex>[r]&
\operatorname{Top}/X
\ar@<.6ex>[l]\\
\operatorname{Sh}(X)
\ar@<.6ex>[r]\ar@{^{(}->}[u]&
\operatorname{Et}(X)
\ar@<.6ex>[l]\ar@{^{(}->}[u].
}
$$
For a sheaf $F$, form
$E(F)=\coprod_{x\in X}F_x$.  A section
$s\in F(U)$ determines a map
$U\longrightarrow E(F)$ by sending $x$ to
its germ.  Taking the images of all such maps
as a basis defines a topology for which
$E(F)\longrightarrow X$ is a local
homeomorphism.  Conversely, an etale space
gives the sheaf of its continuous local
sections.  These constructions are inverse
up to natural isomorphism.
\section{Derived functors and de Rham's
theorem}
\label{section-derived-functors-de-rham}

The source now returns to the derived-functor
technique previously met in Ext and Tor.

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-abelian-derived-functor}
An \emph{abelian category} is a category with
finite biproducts, kernels, and cokernels, in
which every monomorphism is a kernel and
every
epimorphism is a cokernel.  A functor between
abelian categories is \emph{additive} if it
preserves finite biproducts.

An object $I$ is \emph{injective} if every
map into $I$ extends across every
monomorphism, as pictured by
$$
\xymatrix@C=22pt@R=13pt{
A\ar@{^{(}->}[r]\ar[dr]&B\ar@{-->}[d]\\
&I.
}
$$
A category has \emph{enough injectives} when
every object embeds into an injective one.

Let $F:\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}$ be additive and left
exact, and suppose $\mathcal{C}$ has enough
injectives.  Its \emph{right derived
functors} $R^iF$ satisfy $R^0F\cong F$ and
turn every short exact sequence
$$
\xymatrix@C=18pt{
0\ar[r]&A\ar[r]&B\ar[r]&C\ar[r]&0
}
$$
into the long exact sequence
$$
\xymatrix@C=12pt@R=12pt{
0\ar[r]&F(A)\ar[r]&F(B)\ar[r]&F(C)
\ar[dll]\\
&R^1F(A)\ar[r]&R^1F(B)\ar[r]&
R^1F(C)\ar[dll]\\
&R^2F(A)\ar[r]&R^2F(B)\ar[r]&
R^2F(C)\ar[dll]\\
&\cdots .&&
}
$$
Moreover, $R^iF(I)=0$ for injective $I$ and
$i>0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-derived-functors-exist}
The right derived functors of every additive
left-exact functor between abelian categories
exist when the source has enough injectives.
\end{proposition}

An \emph{injective resolution} of $A$ is an
exact sequence
$$
\xymatrix@C=16pt{
0\ar[r]&A\ar[r]&I^0\ar[r]^{d^0}&
I^1\ar[r]^{d^1}&I^2\ar[r]&\cdots
}
$$
with each $I^j$ injective.  Applying $F$
gives the cochain complex
$$
\xymatrix@C=18pt{
F(I^0)\ar[r]^{F(d^0)}&
F(I^1)\ar[r]^{F(d^1)}&
F(I^2)\ar[r]&\cdots .
}
$$
Its $i$-th cohomology is $R^iF(A)$.

\begin{definition}
\label{at-definition-f-acyclic-object}
An object $A$ is \emph{$F$-acyclic} if
$$
R^iF(A)=0
$$
for every $i>0$.
\end{definition}

Injectives can be replaced by acyclic
objects.

\begin{proposition}
\label{at-proposition-acyclic-resolution}
Suppose every $M^i$ is $F$-acyclic and
$$
\xymatrix@C=15pt{
0\ar[r]&A\ar[r]&M^0\ar[r]^{d^0}&
M^1\ar[r]^{d^1}&M^2\ar[r]&\cdots
}
$$
is exact.  Then
$$
R^iF(A)\cong H^i(F(M^\bullet)).
$$
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $B^0$ be the cokernel of
$A\longrightarrow M^0$.  There is a short
exact sequence
$$
\xymatrix@C=18pt{
0\ar[r]&A\ar[r]&M^0\ar[r]&B^0
\ar[r]&0.
}
$$
Its derived long exact sequence is
$$
\xymatrix@C=10pt@R=11pt{
0\ar[r]&F(A)\ar[r]&F(M^0)\ar[r]&
F(B^0)\ar[dll]\\
&R^1F(A)\ar[r]&R^1F(M^0)\ar[r]&
R^1F(B^0)\ar[dll]\\
&R^2F(A)\ar[r]&R^2F(M^0)\ar[r]&
R^2F(B^0)\ar[dll]\\
&\cdots .&&
}
$$
Acyclicity of $M^0$ gives
$$
\xymatrix@C=15pt{
F(M^0)\ar[r]&F(B^0)\ar[r]&
R^1F(A)\ar[r]&0
}
$$
and, for $i\geq1$,
$$
R^iF(B^0)\cong R^{i+1}F(A).
$$
In particular, the exact tail identifies
$$
\xymatrix@C=18pt{
\operatorname{coker}\bigl(
F(M^0)\longrightarrow F(B^0)\bigr)
\ar[r]^-{\cong}&R^1F(A).
}
$$
Now let $B^j$ be the image of
$M^j\longrightarrow M^{j+1}$.  Repeating
the argument for
$$
0\longrightarrow B^{j-1}
\longrightarrow M^j
\longrightarrow B^j
\longrightarrow0
$$
dimension-shifts the higher derived functors.
The cokernels produced at each stage are
exactly the cohomology groups of
$F(M^\bullet)$.
\end{proof}

The category of sheaves of abelian groups on
$X$ is abelian and has enough injectives.
The commented diagram in the source merely
repeats the already recorded full inclusion
of abelian sheaves into abelian presheaves;
it has no additional arrows or objects.

The global-sections functor is
$$
\xymatrix@C=32pt@R=8pt{
\operatorname{Sh}_{\operatorname{Ab}}(X)
\ar[r]^\Gamma&
\operatorname{Ab}\\
F\ar[r]|-{\hspace{-4pt}\longmapsto}&F(X).
}
$$
Its right derived functors define sheaf
cohomology:
$$
H^i(X;F)=R^i\Gamma(F).
$$

Let $M$ be a smooth manifold and let
$\underline{\mathbb{R}}$ denote the constant
sheaf.  Sheafified singular cochains form the
resolution
$$
\xymatrix@C=12pt{
0\ar[r]&\underline{\mathbb{R}}\ar[r]&
\mathcal{C}^0_{\rm sing}\ar[r]&
\mathcal{C}^1_{\rm sing}\ar[r]&
\mathcal{C}^2_{\rm sing}\ar[r]&\cdots ,
}
$$
and differential forms form the resolution
$$
\xymatrix@C=14pt{
0\ar[r]&\underline{\mathbb{R}}\ar[r]&
\Omega^0\ar[r]^d&\Omega^1\ar[r]^d&
\Omega^2\ar[r]^d&\cdots .
}
$$
The first resolution is flabby and the second
is fine, hence both are $\Gamma$-acyclic.
Exactness of the de Rham resolution is the
Poincare lemma.  Applying global sections to
the two resolutions proves de Rham's theorem:
$$
H^i_{\rm dR}(M)
\cong R^i\Gamma(\underline{\mathbb{R}})
\cong H^i_{\rm sing}(M;\mathbb{R}).
$$
